CCKE Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Most nonsteroidal anti inflammatory drugs, which promote sodium retention and blunt diuretic effect): NSAIDs reduce renal prostaglandin synthesis, causing sodium and water retention, diminished diuretic and renin angiotensin blocker effectiveness, and worsening renal function, so the guideline names them among drugs of potential harm in HFrEF, alongside nondihydropyridine calcium channel blockers and thiazolidinediones. Topical capsaicin has negligible systemic effect and is a reasonable alternative. Duloxetine and tramadol are not heart failure precipitants of that kind, and a single intra articular steroid injection carries far less systemic load than oral NSAID courses.
  2. The correct answer is A (Intravenous magnesium sulfate given as a bolus to suppress the arrhythmia): The strip shows a polymorphic ventricular tachycardia whose QRS complexes twist around the baseline in a patient loaded with QT prolonging drugs, which identifies torsades de pointes, and magnesium is the first line suppressive therapy along with stopping offending agents. Procainamide and amiodarone both prolong the QT interval further and can perpetuate the arrhythmia. Adenosine targets the AV node, which plays no role here, and beta blockade alone does not address the acute repolarization abnormality driving the rhythm.
  3. The correct answer is B (Cardiac magnetic resonance imaging, identifying myocardial edema and late gadolinium enhancement patterns): CMR applies the Lake Louise criteria, combining T2 based evidence of edema with T1 based abnormalities including late gadolinium enhancement in a nonischemic, typically subepicardial or midwall distribution, making it the noninvasive test of choice. Echocardiography assesses function but has no texture finding specific to myocarditis. Perfusion imaging and calcium scoring evaluate coronary disease rather than inflammation, and noncontrast chest CT cannot characterize myocardial tissue this way.
  4. The correct answer is E (Within 90 minutes, the goal for hospitals capable of percutaneous coronary intervention): when a patient presents directly to an intervention capable hospital, the goal from first medical contact to device is 90 minutes or less. The 120 minute standard applies when interfacility transfer is needed, and the 30 minute goal applies to starting fibrinolytics when timely intervention is impossible. Neither a 60 minute ambulance specific target nor routine fibrinolysis before onsite intervention is a guideline goal.
  5. The correct answer is B (Risk of myocardial infarction remains elevated, driven partly by chronic inflammation and immune activation): even with suppressive therapy, people living with human immunodeficiency virus carry roughly one and a half to two times the risk of atherosclerotic events, reflecting persistent inflammation, immune activation, traditional risk factor clustering, and some antiretroviral metabolic effects. Suppression narrows but does not erase the gap, and events occur across CD4 strata rather than clustering in advanced disease alone. Direct viral myocarditis and drug effects each contribute far less than the inflammatory atherosclerotic burden.
  6. The correct answer is E (A restrictive orifice maintains a high velocity turbulent jet across a large pressure gradient): A small defect preserves the normal pressure difference between the ventricles, so flow crosses at high velocity and creates intense turbulence. Larger defects equalize pressures, and their murmurs soften as jet velocity falls. The loud murmur therefore signals a hemodynamically restrictive lesion rather than a dangerous one.
  7. The correct answer is D (The right coronary artery, in roughly 60 percent of individuals studied): the sinoatrial nodal artery arises from the proximal right coronary artery in about 60 percent of people, with the circumflex supplying it in most of the remainder, which is why proximal right coronary occlusion can produce sinus bradycardia and arrest. The circumflex figure of 90 percent inverts the true proportions. The left anterior descending, a ramus branch, and the left main directly are not typical origins of this artery.
  8. The correct answer is E (Supporting the bare arm at heart level after five minutes of quiet rest): Accurate technique requires about five minutes of quiet rest, then a bare arm supported at heart level with the back supported and feet flat on the floor. An unsupported or dependent arm, a cuff over clothing, talking during the reading, or dangling legs each raise the recorded value by several mm Hg. Measuring the moment the patient sits captures activity related elevation rather than resting pressure.
  9. The correct answer is A (The interatrial communication equalizes respiratory variation in venous return between the two atria): With a large defect, inspiratory increases in systemic venous return are shared across both atria, so the split stays constant through the respiratory cycle. Right bundle branch delay widens the split but still allows respiratory variation. Pulmonary hypertension narrows the split and accentuates the pulmonic component rather than fixing the interval.
  10. The correct answer is E (A corrected QT interval exceeding 500 milliseconds, or an increase over 60 milliseconds): A QTc beyond 500 milliseconds, or a rise of more than 60 milliseconds from baseline after starting a drug, is the widely used threshold that marks substantially increased torsades risk and prompts medication review. Values of 440 milliseconds in men and 460 milliseconds in women sit near the upper limits of normal rather than at the high risk boundary. An uncorrected QT of 380 milliseconds is unremarkable, and 420 milliseconds falls within the normal range for most adults.
  11. The correct answer is D (Hold the spironolactone and recheck potassium and renal function within several days): his potassium of 5.8 mmol/L with worsened renal function crosses the threshold at which mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist therapy should be interrupted, since continuing risks dangerous hyperkalemia. Uptitrating the dose or adding potassium supplements would push the potassium higher still. Eplerenone carries the same hyperkalemia liability as spironolactone, and waiting a full year to recheck chemistry ignores the abnormality entirely.
  12. The correct answer is D (It is an immune mediated pericarditis appearing weeks to months after infarction): Dressler syndrome is a late autoimmune pericarditis presenting with fever, pleuritic pain, a rub, elevated inflammatory markers, and effusion, typically weeks to months after the infarct. Early post infarction pericarditis occurs within the first days as a direct response to adjacent necrosis, which the 4 week interval argues against. The presentation is not reinfarction, bacterial pericarditis, or a slowly leaking wall rupture.
  13. The correct answer is C (Atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia with retrograde atrial activation hidden in the QRS): The strip shows a regular narrow complex tachycardia with no discernible P waves, the classic appearance of typical AVNRT, in which near simultaneous atrial and ventricular activation buries the retrograde P wave. Sinus tachycardia and multifocal atrial tachycardia would both show visible P waves, and multifocal atrial tachycardia would also be irregular. Flutter at a two to one ratio usually reveals sawtooth waves between complexes, and ventricular tachycardia produces wide rather than narrow QRS complexes.
  14. The correct answer is E (Anomalous drainage of the right upper pulmonary vein into the superior vena cava): Superior sinus venosus defects are frequently accompanied by partial anomalous pulmonary venous return, most often involving the right upper vein. Identifying this changes the operation, since the vein must be baffled to the left atrium during repair. A cleft mitral leaflet belongs with primum defects, and apical tricuspid displacement defines Ebstein anomaly.
  15. The correct answer is A (Progressive PR prolongation across successive beats until a P wave fails to conduct): Mobitz type I, or Wenckebach, block shows lengthening PR intervals that culminate in a dropped beat, reflects decremental conduction within the AV node, and is common and benign during sleep in young people with high vagal tone. Fixed PR intervals with sudden failure describe Mobitz type II, an infranodal problem with a different prognosis. Two to one conduction with a wide QRS also suggests infranodal disease, independent atrial and ventricular activity defines complete block, and progressively shortening PR intervals describe none of the block patterns.
  16. The correct answer is A (Bivalirudin, a direct thrombin inhibitor that does not cross react with heparin antibodies): bivalirudin inhibits thrombin directly without a cofactor and is a standard procedural choice after heparin induced thrombocytopenia. Unfractionated heparin is unsafe here, and enoxaparin can cross react with the pathogenic antibodies. Fondaparinux still works through antithrombin rather than directly, and warfarin is unsuitable for procedural anticoagulation and can worsen this condition acutely.
  17. The correct answer is E (It inhibits the late inward sodium current, reducing calcium overload and diastolic tension): ranolazine blocks the late inward sodium current, lowering intracellular calcium accumulation, diastolic wall tension, and compressive resistance to subendocardial flow, which is why it works without hemodynamic change. It does not donate nitric oxide, inhibit the funny current, block peripheral calcium channels, or alter hemoglobin oxygen affinity. That hemodynamic neutrality makes it attractive when blood pressure or heart rate is already low.
  18. The correct answer is A (It lowers heart rate and contractility, reducing myocardial oxygen demand during exertion): beta blockade blunts sympathetic stimulation of rate and contractility, and slower rates also lengthen diastole, when coronary perfusion occurs. Late sodium current inhibition is the mechanism of ranolazine, and nitric oxide mediated dilation describes nitrates. Beta blockers neither stimulate collateral growth through growth factors nor change hemoglobin oxygen affinity.
  19. The correct answer is A (A blowing apical systolic murmur radiating toward the axilla, often with an S3): the mitral regurgitant jet is directed into the left atrium, so the murmur is loudest at the apex and follows the jet toward the axilla, and in severe disease the large diastolic inflow volume generates a third heart sound. A harsh basal murmur with carotid radiation and a delayed upstroke describes aortic stenosis. An apical diastolic rumble after a snap indicates mitral stenosis, and an early diastolic sternal border murmur with bounding pulses indicates aortic regurgitation. Inspiratory intensification at the lower sternal border points to tricuspid regurgitation.
  20. The correct answer is D (Above goal on three optimally dosed agents including a diuretic, or controlled on four): The accepted definition requires blood pressure above goal despite three antihypertensives at optimal or best tolerated doses, one of which is a diuretic, or control achieved only with four or more agents. Any three drugs regardless of dose or class misses the diuretic and dosing requirements. Two drug regimens and controlled three drug regimens without a fourth agent describe difficult or well managed hypertension, not resistance.
  21. The correct answer is C (It transiently blocks conduction through the atrioventricular node, interrupting the reentrant circuit): Adenosine activates A1 receptors, increasing potassium conductance and suppressing calcium mediated conduction in the AV node, which briefly interrupts any circuit that requires the node, such as AVNRT or orthodromic AVRT. Sodium channel blockade in atrial tissue describes class I agents, and inhibition of the rapid delayed rectifier potassium current describes class III drugs. Beta receptor stimulation would speed rather than block nodal conduction, and adenosine does not act by selective ventricular calcium channel blockade.
  22. The correct answer is E (The click occurs earlier in systole and the murmur becomes longer and often louder): standing abruptly reduces venous return, so the ventricle is smaller and the redundant leaflet reaches its prolapsed position earlier in ejection. The earlier click leaves more of systole for the regurgitant murmur, which lengthens and frequently intensifies. A later click with a shorter murmur is the response to squatting, which enlarges the ventricle. The timing of prolapse depends on chamber size as well as leaflet anatomy, and the murmur remains systolic rather than moving into diastole.
  23. The correct answer is C (The left anterior descending artery, supplying the anterior wall and anterior septum): ST elevation in leads V1 through V4 localizes infarction to the anterior wall and septum, the territory of this vessel. Right coronary and posterior descending occlusions produce inferior lead changes, and circumflex occlusion produces lateral or posterolateral patterns. The acute marginal branch supplies right ventricular myocardium and does not generate this precordial distribution.
  24. The correct answer is D (Twelve months, with earlier shortening reserved for patients with bleeding concerns): after an acute coronary syndrome treated with a drug eluting stent, twelve months of aspirin plus a potent P2Y12 inhibitor remains the default for patients without elevated bleeding risk. Two week, one month, and three month regimens are abbreviated strategies reserved for bleeding concerns rather than defaults, and routine extension to thirty six months applies to selected high ischemic risk patients. His tolerability so far supports completing the standard year.
  25. The correct answer is E (It softens, because increased preload and afterload enlarge the cavity and reduce obstruction): squatting raises venous return and systemic resistance simultaneously, and handgrip raises afterload, so both maneuvers distend the left ventricle, pull the septum and mitral apparatus apart, and diminish the dynamic gradient. The murmur therefore quiets, the mirror image of its behavior with Valsalva and standing. Physical maneuvers reliably change this gradient without any drug provocation, and no mechanism converts it into a continuous murmur.
  26. The correct answer is A (A resting electrocardiogram without left bundle branch block, paced rhythm, or significant ST abnormality): exercise electrocardiography depends on interpretable exercise induced ST changes, which baseline bundle branch block, ventricular pacing, pre excitation, or marked resting ST depression obscure. No minimum resting heart rate, prior calcium score, or baseline echocardiogram is required before treadmill testing. Caffeine restriction matters for vasodilator stress agents rather than for exercise electrocardiography.
  27. The correct answer is A (Idiopathic, heritable, and drug or toxin induced pulmonary arterial hypertension): vasoreactivity testing exists to find the small subset of these specific patients whose disease responds to calcium channel blockade, and guidelines confine the test to them because acute responders in other etiologies do not achieve durable benefit from that therapy. Connective tissue and portopulmonary disease are group 1 conditions where testing is nonetheless not recommended, since responders are rare and calcium channel therapy can harm. Lung disease, thromboembolic disease, and left heart disease fall entirely outside the purpose of the test.
  28. The correct answer is C (Abnormal leaflet architecture creates turbulent stress that accelerates fibrosis and calcification): the asymmetric two leaflet geometry produces abnormal folding and turbulent shear across the valve, driving fibrocalcific remodeling one to two decades earlier than in trileaflet degeneration, so bicuspid patients commonly present with severe stenosis in their fifties and sixties. Rheumatic injury is an acquired process marked by commissural fusion, not a congenital leaflet property. Endocarditis risk is increased in bicuspid disease but healed vegetations are not the mechanism of progressive calcific stenosis. The raphe does not obstruct the coronary ostia, and annular size is typically normal rather than congenitally small.
  29. The correct answer is B (Stage D, marked symptoms interfering with daily life despite maximally tolerated guideline therapy): stage D, or advanced heart failure, describes refractory symptoms, recurrent hospitalizations, and escalating diuretic needs despite optimized treatment, prompting consideration of transplant, mechanical support, or palliative care. Inotrope dependence is one marker of advanced disease but is not a prerequisite for the stage. NYHA class IV describes his functional limitation and is documented alongside, not instead of, the stage.
  30. The correct answer is E (Enhanced ventricular interdependence with a septal bounce and respiratory variation in filling): a rigid pericardium fixes total cardiac volume, so inspiratory filling of the right ventricle displaces the septum toward the left ventricle, producing the septal bounce, marked respiratory variation in mitral inflow, and discordant rather than concordant ventricular pressure changes. Tissue Doppler velocities are typically preserved or increased in constriction and reduced in restriction. Severe biatrial enlargement and markedly elevated natriuretic peptides favor restrictive myocardial disease instead.
  31. The correct answer is C (A target of 3.0, generally maintained within a range of 2.5 to 3.5): mechanical valves in the mitral position carry higher thrombotic risk than aortic prostheses because of lower flow velocities, so the recommended warfarin intensity is an international normalized ratio of 3.0. A target of 2.5 applies to bileaflet aortic prostheses without additional risk factors, not to mitral valves. Values near 1.5 to 2.0 are subtherapeutic for mechanical prostheses, and a routine target of 4.0 adds bleeding risk without established benefit. Direct oral anticoagulants and aspirin substitution are not acceptable replacements for warfarin with mechanical valves.
  32. The correct answer is A (Irreversible acetylation of cyclooxygenase 1, blocking thromboxane A2 production for the platelet lifespan): aspirin permanently acetylates cyclooxygenase 1, and because platelets lack nuclei the effect lasts their entire 7 to 10 day lifespan. Reversible P2Y12 blockade describes ticagrelor, and glycoprotein IIb IIIa inhibition describes agents such as eptifibatide. Antithrombin potentiation is the mechanism of heparin, while phosphodiesterase 3 inhibition describes cilostazol.
  33. The correct answer is B (Reduced cardiovascular mortality and fewer hospital readmissions among program participants): participation after infarction is associated with lower cardiovascular mortality, fewer readmissions, and better functional capacity and quality of life. Exercise training does not shrink stenoses to normal caliber or regenerate infarcted myocardium. Rehabilitation complements rather than replaces statin and antiplatelet therapy, and no fixed 15 point ejection fraction gain is an expected outcome.
  34. The correct answer is B (Ticagrelor, a reversible direct acting oral antagonist of the P2Y12 receptor): ticagrelor needs no hepatic activation, binds reversibly, is dosed twice daily, and characteristically causes dyspnea without hypoxia. Clopidogrel and prasugrel are irreversible thienopyridine prodrugs taken once daily. Cangrelor is given only intravenously, and vorapaxar targets the protease activated receptor rather than the P2Y12 receptor.
  35. The correct answer is E (Heart failure with improved ejection fraction, a prior value of 40 or below rising above 40): HFimpEF describes a baseline ejection fraction of 40 percent or less that improves by at least 10 points to a value above 40 percent. The category exists specifically to signal that guideline directed therapy must continue, because withdrawal of treatment leads to relapse in a substantial share of patients. He is not reclassified as preserved ejection fraction, staging never moves backward to stage B, and no recovery category permits stopping medications.
  36. The correct answer is E (Class II, slight limitation with symptoms on ordinary exertion and comfort at rest): brisk walking and stair climbing are ordinary physical activities, and symptoms limited to that level of exertion with comfort at rest define class II. Class III requires symptoms with less than ordinary activity, such as walking across a room, and class IV requires symptoms at rest. The classification uses four discrete classes and does not record combined transitional grades.
  37. The correct answer is A (Diffuse concave upward elevation lacking reciprocal depression except in lead aVR): pericardial inflammation involves the whole subepicardium, producing widespread concave elevation with PR depression, and only aVR and sometimes V1 show reciprocal type changes. Convex elevation in contiguous leads with true reciprocal depression indicates regional infarction. Evolution into Q waves and hyperacute T waves are infarction features, and a shifting territorial pattern describes neither condition accurately.
  38. The correct answer is D (An orifice measuring 1.0 cm² or smaller on quantitative Doppler assessment): severe aortic stenosis is defined by a valve area of 1.0 cm² or less, together with a jet velocity of at least 4 m/s and a mean gradient of at least 40 mmHg in the high gradient form. Areas between about 1.0 and 1.5 cm² represent moderate stenosis, and values near 2.0 cm² or larger indicate mild narrowing of a valve whose normal area is 3.0 to 4.0 cm². Annular calcium without a reduced orifice or elevated gradient does not itself establish severity.
  39. The correct answer is A (At least 40 days after myocardial infarction, on guideline directed medical therapy with reassessment): ejection fraction frequently recovers after infarction and revascularization, and trials of very early implantation showed no survival benefit, so the guideline requires waiting at least 40 days with the ejection fraction remeasured on medical therapy. No exception waives the wait for lower ejection fractions. A wearable defibrillator may bridge selected high risk patients during the waiting period, but it does not shorten the required interval, and neither 7 days nor 6 months is the specified threshold.
  40. The correct answer is C (Left bundle branch block morphology with a QRS duration of 150 ms or greater): resynchronization benefit is greatest with left bundle branch block and a markedly wide QRS, so this combination in a patient with an ejection fraction of 35 percent or less, sinus rhythm, and NYHA class II to IV symptoms earns the class 1 recommendation. Right bundle branch block and nonspecific delays derive less benefit and carry weaker recommendations. Echocardiographic dyssynchrony and PR prolongation are not selection criteria, and a QRS under 120 ms is generally a situation where resynchronization can harm.
  41. The correct answer is D (Paroxysmal atrial fibrillation, defined by episodes terminating within seven days of onset): Episodes that stop spontaneously or with intervention within seven days meet the definition of paroxysmal atrial fibrillation, and his self terminating twelve hour episodes fit squarely within that window. Persistent disease requires continuous arrhythmia beyond seven days, and the long standing persistent label applies after twelve continuous months. Permanent describes a shared decision to stop pursuing rhythm control rather than an arrhythmia duration, and nothing in his history identifies a reversible noncardiac precipitant.
  42. The correct answer is D (Diffuse concave ST segment elevation accompanied by PR segment depression in multiple leads): stage one appears within hours to days and reflects diffuse subepicardial inflammation. Territorial convex elevation with reciprocal depression describes myocardial infarction rather than pericarditis. Normalization of segments and widespread T wave inversion belong to the later second and third stages, while PR elevation confined to aVR is only one component of stage one rather than its definition.
  43. The correct answer is C (Low near term event risk that supports deferring statin therapy with reassessment later): a score of zero in a borderline risk adult without diabetes or current smoking predicts very low near term event rates and supports deferring statin therapy with reassessment in five to ten years. It cannot establish freedom from soft plaque, because noncalcified disease is invisible to calcium scanning, and no six month confirmatory scan is needed. Blood pressure and lifestyle counseling must continue, and a negative scan in this setting is a valid result rather than an artifact.
  44. The correct answer is D (It inhibits cardiac myosin ATPase, reducing excess actin myosin cross bridge formation): hypertrophic cardiomyopathy involves hypercontractility from excessive cross bridge cycling, and mavacamten is a reversible cardiac myosin inhibitor that lowers the outflow gradient and improves symptoms, as shown in EXPLORER HCM. Because it reduces contractility, ejection fraction must be monitored and the drug paused if systolic function falls too far. It does not stimulate myosin, block sodium channels, act on ryanodine receptors, or vasodilate septal arteries.
  45. The correct answer is E (ACE inhibitors, because they cause fetal renal injury and are withheld until after delivery): ACE inhibitors, ARBs, ARNI, and mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists are fetotoxic, with renin angiotensin blockers causing fetal renal dysgenesis, oligohydramnios, and other injury, so they are introduced only postpartum. Beta 1 selective beta blockers such as metoprolol are generally continued during pregnancy. Loop diuretics can be used cautiously for congestion, hydralazine is actually a preferred vasodilator in pregnancy, and digoxin is considered compatible with gestation.
  46. The correct answer is A (Colchicine continued for at least six months alongside antiinflammatory therapy): recurrent pericarditis carries a substantially higher relapse risk, so colchicine duration is extended from the three months used for a first episode to at least six months. Repeating only the three month first episode course undertreats recurrence. Courses tied narrowly to pain resolution or marker normalization are too short, and indefinite full dose therapy without reassessment overshoots the guideline recommendation.
  47. The correct answer is E (A value below approximately 2.0 to 2.5 during adenosine induced hyperemia): coronary flow reserve below roughly 2.0 to 2.5 with adenosine indicates an impaired microcirculatory response and supports coronary microvascular dysfunction. Values above 2.8 or 4.0 reflect preserved or robust augmentation rather than disease, and a value of exactly 1.0 describes absent augmentation rather than the accepted diagnostic threshold. Intermediate values near 3.0 to 3.5 are considered normal.
  48. The correct answer is A (Cavotricuspid isthmus ablation interrupts the circuit with high single procedure success rates): Typical flutter depends on a macroreentrant circuit that must pass through the cavotricuspid isthmus, so a linear ablation lesion across that isthmus blocks the circuit with acute success in well over 90 percent of cases and low recurrence. Pulmonary vein isolation is the lesion set for atrial fibrillation rather than isthmus dependent flutter. Node ablation with pacing is a last resort for rate control, the circuit does not involve the sinus node, and a surgical maze is unnecessary for an arrhythmia so accessible to catheters.
  49. The correct answer is B (The murmur grows louder as inspiration increases venous return to the right heart): negative intrathoracic pressure during inspiration draws more blood into the right heart, increasing the regurgitant volume across the tricuspid valve, a response known as the Carvallo sign. This inspiratory augmentation is the classic bedside tool for separating tricuspid from mitral regurgitation, whose murmur stays the same or softens with inspiration. Inspiration augments rather than diminishes right heart filling, so softening reverses the physiology. The murmur remains holosystolic, and an inspiratory click is not a described feature of this lesion.
  50. The correct answer is B (Ischemia with signs or symptoms despite no obstructive epicardial coronary artery disease): INOCA denotes ischemia with no obstructive coronary arteries, meaning symptoms or objective ischemia despite stenoses below the obstructive threshold. It is not a recanalized infarction, an adventitial inflammatory syndrome, or a benign variant needing reassurance alone, and outcomes are worse than in asymptomatic people. A myocardial bridge is one specific mechanism, and bridges compress the artery in systole rather than diastole.
  51. The correct answer is D (Heart failure with mildly reduced ejection fraction, defined by values of 41 to 49 percent): an ejection fraction of 45 percent with symptoms falls within the HFmrEF band. Preserved ejection fraction requires a value of at least 50 percent, and reduced ejection fraction requires 40 percent or lower. Improved ejection fraction describes a rise above 40 percent from a previously reduced baseline, and advanced heart failure is a clinical stage rather than an ejection fraction band.
  52. The correct answer is C (The murmur grows louder because decreased venous return worsens dynamic outflow obstruction): Valsalva strain reduces preload, shrinking left ventricular cavity size and intensifying septal contact with the mitral apparatus. A smaller ventricle worsens the dynamic gradient, so the murmur intensifies, which is the opposite of fixed aortic stenosis, whose murmur softens as flow falls. Softening with reduced preload therefore describes fixed valvular obstruction, and a diastolic shift or diastolic accentuation belongs to filling murmurs such as mitral stenosis. Wide carotid radiation is a feature of aortic stenosis rather than dynamic subaortic obstruction.
  53. The correct answer is D (A narrow QRS tachycardia with a retrograde P wave shortly after each QRS): In orthodromic AVRT the ventricles activate over the His Purkinje system, keeping the QRS narrow, while the atria are activated retrogradely over the accessory pathway, producing a P wave that follows the QRS with a short RP interval. A wide QRS with a slurred upstroke describes antidromic conduction or resting preexcitation rather than the orthodromic form. P waves completely buried in the QRS are typical of AVNRT, and sawtooth waves or irregularly irregular fibrillatory activity indicate atrial flutter and atrial fibrillation.
  54. The correct answer is A (The infarcted right ventricle depends on preload, which venodilation from nitrates sharply reduces): ST elevation in V4R identifies right ventricular infarction, in which stroke volume depends on venous return, so venodilating agents can cause profound hypotension. Nitrates do not accelerate atrioventricular conduction, neutralize heparin, or constrict the pulmonary vasculature. Coronary steal is a concern with arteriolar dilators such as dipyridamole rather than with nitrate venodilation.
  55. The correct answer is E (Restriction from competitive and high intensity endurance exercise to slow disease progression): sustained intense exercise accelerates structural deterioration and markedly raises arrhythmic risk in this disease, so exercise restriction is a core disease specific intervention rather than generic advice. High intensity training would do the opposite of protecting him. Sodium limits, caffeine reduction, and positive airway pressure address other conditions and have no comparable role in altering the course of this cardiomyopathy.
  56. The correct answer is C (PR interval 120 to 200 ms with QRS duration under 120 ms): normal atrioventricular conduction takes 120 to 200 ms from atrial onset to ventricular onset, and normal ventricular depolarization completes in less than 120 ms, with 120 ms or more defining bundle branch level delay. Ranges starting at 80 ms or below describe preexcitation territory rather than normal conduction. A PR of 200 to 300 ms already meets first degree atrioventricular block criteria, and the widest listed ranges are clearly pathologic.
  57. The correct answer is A (A mean value of approximately 4 to 12 mmHg at rest): the wedge pressure estimates left atrial pressure, which normally runs 4 to 12 mmHg, and values above roughly 15 mmHg suggest left sided congestion. The 15 to 30 mmHg range describes normal pulmonary artery systolic pressure rather than wedge pressure. A near zero mean is below physiologic left atrial pressure, and the two highest ranges represent significant pulmonary venous hypertension.
  58. The correct answer is D (Ejection fraction ≤35 percent with class II or III symptoms, 40 days after infarction): The classic class I primary prevention criteria in ischemic cardiomyopathy are a left ventricular ejection fraction of 35 percent or less with NYHA class II or III symptoms on optimal medical therapy, measured at least 40 days after myocardial infarction and 90 days after revascularization. Ejection fractions of 45 or 50 percent are above the qualifying threshold, and measurements taken within a week of infarction are too early because function often recovers. Palpitations, isolated nonsustained runs with normal function, and frequent ectopy do not by themselves establish the indication.
  59. The correct answer is B (Atrial rate near 300 per minute with 2:1 conduction giving roughly 150 ventricular beats): Untreated typical flutter circulates at an atrial rate of about 250 to 350 per minute, most often near 300, and the AV node usually conducts every other impulse, yielding a ventricular rate around 150. One to one conduction of a flutter circuit is uncommon without drugs that slow the atrial rate, and rates of 100 or 150 in the atria are too slow for classic flutter. A regular tachycardia near 150 per minute should therefore always prompt a search for hidden flutter waves.
  60. The correct answer is B (Forty to sixty beats per minute, reflecting the junction's own intrinsic automaticity): Subsidiary pacemakers fire progressively more slowly the further they sit from the sinus node, and the atrioventricular junction typically escapes at 40 to 60 beats per minute when sinus input fails or slows. Ventricular escape from distal Purkinje tissue runs slower, roughly 20 to 40 per minute. Rates of 60 to 100 describe normal sinus rhythm, a junctional rhythm running above its intrinsic range is termed accelerated, and rates near 150 to 200 indicate a tachycardia mechanism rather than an escape.
  61. The correct answer is D (Free pulmonary regurgitation resulting from disruption of the pulmonic valve annulus): Transannular patching relieves outflow obstruction at the cost of valve competence, so severe regurgitation is nearly universal decades later. Chronic regurgitant volume dilates the right ventricle and drives decisions about valve replacement. Root dilation and residual septal defects occur but are far less frequent than regurgitation in this group.
  62. The correct answer is C (Treatment is reserved for bradycardia producing symptoms clearly attributable to the slow rate): Guideline recommendations for sinus node dysfunction hinge on the correlation between symptoms and documented bradycardia, and there is no numeric rate threshold that by itself mandates therapy. Trained athletes commonly have resting rates in the forties and fifties with sinus pauses during sleep, and these findings are benign. Prophylactic pacing for asymptomatic athletes and routine theophylline for slow nocturnal rates have no role in an asymptomatic healthy person.
  63. The correct answer is C (An NSAID or aspirin combined with colchicine continued for approximately three months): the antiinflammatory drug controls symptoms while adjunctive colchicine roughly halves the recurrence rate, and three months is the recommended colchicine duration for a first episode. Corticosteroids are reserved for contraindications or specific indications because they promote recurrence. Colchicine monotherapy for one month is too brief and omits the antiinflammatory backbone, interleukin 1 blockade is reserved for refractory recurrent disease, and acetaminophen with rest lacks antiinflammatory efficacy.
  64. The correct answer is D (Carvedilol, a beta blocker with proven mortality benefit in reduced ejection fraction): After myocardial infarction with a reduced ejection fraction, carvedilol, bisoprolol, or metoprolol succinate lowers blood pressure while reducing death and recurrent events, making a beta blocker the compelling indication choice. Amlodipine and thiazides lower pressure without survival benefit in this setting. Nondihydropyridine agents such as diltiazem depress contractility and worsen outcomes in systolic dysfunction, and alpha blockers add no mortality advantage.
  65. The correct answer is B (Atrioventricular dissociation with occasional fusion or capture beats between wide complexes): Independent atrial activity with fusion and capture beats proves that the ventricles are being driven by their own focus, which is essentially diagnostic of ventricular tachycardia. A rate under 150 and a longstanding palpitation history carry no discriminating power, since both arrhythmia types can behave that way. A QRS of 120 milliseconds in a typical bundle branch pattern actually favors aberrancy, and a response to carotid massage suggests a supraventricular mechanism dependent on the AV node.
  66. The correct answer is E (Ejection fraction of 35 percent or less with NYHA class II or III symptoms): primary prevention ICD therapy carries a class 1 recommendation for such patients on chronic guideline directed therapy with more than one year of expected meaningful survival, and his profile satisfies every element. Thresholds of 40 or 45 percent apply to no primary prevention indication in this guideline. Routine electrophysiology testing is not required at an ejection fraction of 30 percent, and syncope with a structurally normal heart raises a different evaluation pathway entirely.
  67. The correct answer is C (Aspirin started within six hours postoperatively and continued indefinitely thereafter): aspirin begun within six hours of surgery improves early vein graft patency and is continued indefinitely for secondary prevention. Warfarin is not standard graft protection, deferring therapy to the clinic visit forfeits the early patency window, and neither clopidogrel monotherapy nor time limited ticagrelor is the guideline default after surgery. Early administration is appropriate once surgical bleeding has been excluded.
  68. The correct answer is D (Coarctation of the aorta, often revealed by arm to leg blood pressure differences): roughly half of patients with aortic coarctation have a bicuspid valve, and the association runs in both directions, so every bicuspid valve patient deserves blood pressure measurement in the arms and legs and assessment of the femoral pulses. Bicuspid disease also travels with an aortopathy of the root and ascending aorta that requires imaging surveillance. Patent ductus, anomalous pulmonary venous return, and peripheral pulmonary stenosis belong to other congenital syndromes. Coronary fistulae are sporadic anomalies without a specific bicuspid association.
  69. The correct answer is A (Accumulation of bradykinin and substance P that sensitizes airway cough receptors): Angiotensin converting enzyme normally degrades bradykinin and substance P, so inhibiting it allows these peptides to accumulate and sensitize airway cough fibers, producing the classic dry cough. The cough is a class effect and resolves within days to weeks of stopping the drug. Histamine release, eosinophilic inflammation, and reflux do not explain the reaction, and angiotensin II levels fall rather than rise on therapy.
  70. The correct answer is E (Microorganisms demonstrated within a vegetation by culture or histologic examination): the Duke pathologic criteria establish definite endocarditis when organisms are shown by culture or histology in a vegetation, in an embolized vegetation, or in an intracardiac abscess, or when active endocarditis is confirmed histologically in such a lesion. Sterile fibrin platelet deposits describe nonbacterial thrombotic endocarditis, which lacks organisms and active inflammation. Myxomatous degeneration is a structural leaflet disorder without infection, and caseating granulomas suggest tuberculosis rather than typical infective endocarditis. Thrombus on a catheter tip, even when culture positive, does not demonstrate endocardial infection.
  71. The correct answer is E (It softens because greater venous return and afterload enlarge the ventricular cavity): squatting simultaneously raises preload and afterload, distending the left ventricle and pushing the septum away from the mitral apparatus, which lessens the dynamic gradient and quiets the murmur. Standing and Valsalva strain have the opposite effect, shrinking the ventricle and intensifying the murmur, which is the signature behavior distinguishing this lesion from fixed aortic stenosis. Squatting fills rather than empties the ventricle, so intensification is backwards physiology. The murmur remains systolic, and positional maneuvers strongly affect left sided dynamic obstruction.
  72. The correct answer is B (Sinus bradycardia, low voltage electrocardiogram, and a pericardial effusion accumulating slowly): thyroid hormone deficiency slows sinus node firing, reduces contractility, raises systemic vascular resistance, and allows a protein rich pericardial effusion that rarely causes tamponade because it collects gradually. Tachycardia, widened pulse pressure, high output states, and rapid atrial fibrillation describe thyrotoxicosis rather than hypothyroidism. Ventricular ectopy with a shortened QT interval does not fit either thyroid extreme, since hypothyroidism typically prolongs the QT interval.
  73. The correct answer is A (Tachycardia bradycardia syndrome, a common manifestation of underlying sinus node dysfunction): Alternating atrial tachyarrhythmias and symptomatic post conversion pauses define tachycardia bradycardia syndrome, in which a diseased sinus node recovers slowly after being suppressed by the tachycardia. The Ashman phenomenon is a benign aberrancy pattern rather than a pause producing disorder. Chronotropic incompetence refers to blunted rate response during exertion, physiologic overdrive suppression does not by itself produce prolonged symptomatic pauses in healthy nodes, and carotid sinus hypersensitivity requires a triggering reflex stimulus rather than arrhythmia offset.
  74. The correct answer is E (Delayed weak femoral pulses with lower systolic pressure in the legs): Coarctation narrows the aorta distal to the left subclavian artery, so femoral pulses arrive late and feel diminished while leg systolic pressure falls below arm pressure, reversing the normal gradient. Bounding femoral pulses with wide pulse pressure suggest aortic regurgitation, as does an early diastolic murmur. An abdominal bruit points toward renal artery disease, and a rise on standing has no localizing value for coarctation.
  75. The correct answer is C (Persistently positive blood cultures with an organism typical for infective endocarditis): the two Duke major criteria are blood cultures positive with typical organisms in a persistent pattern and evidence of endocardial involvement, such as a vegetation, abscess, new prosthetic dehiscence, or new valvular regurgitation. Viridans streptococci in two separate cultures drawn more than 12 hours apart satisfy the microbiologic major criterion. Fever, injection drug use, and immunologic phenomena such as glomerulonephritis are minor criteria. Painless palm and sole macules are Janeway lesions, a vascular phenomenon that also counts only as a minor criterion.
  76. The correct answer is E (Colonoscopy to evaluate for colorectal neoplasia associated with this organism): Streptococcus gallolyticus bacteremia and endocarditis are strongly associated with colonic adenomas and adenocarcinoma, and a mucosal lesion is presumed to be the portal of entry, so colonoscopy is indicated once the patient is stable. Dental imaging targets viridans streptococcal sources, not this gut organism. Screening for silent emboli does not address the source of infection, and neither urothelial nor hematologic malignancy carries the specific association that drives evaluation here.
  77. The correct answer is D (Long QT type 2, in which maintaining normal serum potassium is emphasized): KCNH2 mutations impair the rapid delayed rectifier potassium current, producing long QT type 2, whose events are classically provoked by sudden arousal and by the postpartum period, and keeping potassium replete supports the residual repolarizing current alongside beta blockade. Type 1 relates to KCNQ1 with exertional events, and type 3 relates to SCN5A with events at rest. Brugada syndrome and catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia arise from different genes and do not produce this notched T wave phenotype.
  78. The correct answer is C (Papillary muscle rupture causing acute severe mitral regurgitation and pulmonary edema): abrupt pulmonary edema with hypotension, a soft apical systolic murmur, and a flail leaflet several days after an inferior infarction indicates rupture of the posteromedial papillary muscle, which has a single blood supply. Septal rupture produces a loud parasternal murmur with a thrill, and free wall rupture presents as tamponade with pulseless electrical activity. Dressler syndrome appears weeks later, and an aneurysm causes gradual rather than sudden decline.
  79. The correct answer is B (Atorvastatin 80 mg daily, a high intensity regimen lowering LDL by ≥50 percent): after a myocardial infarction, high intensity therapy, meaning atorvastatin 40 to 80 mg or rosuvastatin 20 to 40 mg, is recommended regardless of the starting LDL value. Simvastatin 20 mg, pravastatin 40 mg, and rosuvastatin 5 mg are only low or moderate intensity regimens. Fenofibrate addresses triglycerides and is never a substitute for statin therapy in secondary prevention.
  80. The correct answer is D (Nondihydropyridine calcium channel blockers, whose negative inotropic effects worsen reduced ejection fraction): verapamil and diltiazem depress contractility and are listed as potentially harmful in HFrEF, where trials showed worse outcomes. Evidence based beta blockers remain foundational therapy in this population rather than a hazard, and digoxin is an acceptable rate control adjunct when dosed and monitored carefully. Amiodarone is among the antiarrhythmics considered safer in systolic dysfunction, and magnesium is used for specific arrhythmias without the harm described.
  81. The correct answer is C (The cumulative lifetime dose received, with risk climbing steeply at higher totals): anthracycline cardiotoxicity is dose related, with left ventricular dysfunction becoming substantially more frequent as cumulative doxorubicin exposure approaches and exceeds roughly 400 to 550 mg per square meter, which is why lifetime totals are tracked. Slower rather than faster infusion reduces peak exposure, so the infusion time option reverses the relationship. Tumor receptor status, baseline heart rate, and cycle spacing do not determine anthracycline risk in this way.
  82. The correct answer is B (NT proBNP, because neprilysin inhibition raises BNP while NT proBNP is not a substrate): sacubitril blocks neprilysin, the enzyme that degrades BNP, so BNP levels rise on therapy and no longer reflect filling pressures reliably. NT proBNP is not degraded by neprilysin, so it remains interpretable and typically falls as the patient improves. Troponin, soluble ST2, and galectin 3 provide prognostic information but none replaces natriuretic peptide monitoring in this setting.
  83. The correct answer is D (It binds antithrombin and greatly accelerates inactivation of thrombin and factor Xa): unfractionated heparin works indirectly by binding antithrombin and multiplying its inactivation of thrombin and factor Xa by roughly a thousandfold. Direct thrombin occupation without a cofactor describes bivalirudin, and selective factor Xa inactivation better describes fondaparinux. Vitamin K epoxide reductase inhibition is the mechanism of warfarin, while plasminogen activation describes fibrinolytics.
  84. The correct answer is A (Systolic dysfunction developing toward the end of pregnancy or in the months after delivery): peripartum cardiomyopathy is defined as new left ventricular systolic dysfunction, typically with an ejection fraction below 45 percent, arising in the last month of pregnancy or within about five months postpartum, once other causes are excluded. Her presentation at five weeks after delivery fits squarely within that window. First trimester onset, isolated diastolic dysfunction, intrapartum right ventricular failure, and onset years later all fall outside the definition.
  85. The correct answer is B (Oral anticoagulation is recommended, since his score indicates an elevated annual stroke risk): The 2023 guideline recommends oral anticoagulation for patients with atrial fibrillation and an annual thromboembolic risk of about 2 percent or more, which corresponds to a CHA2DS2-VASc score of 2 or greater in men and 3 or greater in women. Aspirin alone and dual antiplatelet therapy are inferior to anticoagulation for stroke prevention in atrial fibrillation and are not recommended substitutes. Waiting for a first stroke or for a score of four would leave a clearly elevated risk untreated.
  86. The correct answer is C (SGLT2 inhibitors, which reduced heart failure hospitalizations in dedicated preserved ejection fraction trials): EMPEROR Preserved demonstrated a reduction in the composite of cardiovascular death and heart failure hospitalization, making SGLT2 inhibition the best supported drug therapy in HFpEF and earning the 2a grade. Nitrates failed to improve activity in this population, and digoxin has no established role here. Neither beta blockers nor calcium channel blockers carry outcome evidence for preserved ejection fraction.
  87. The correct answer is B (Any prior intracerebral hemorrhage documented at any point in her lifetime): a history of intracerebral hemorrhage is an absolute contraindication to fibrinolysis no matter how remote. An ischemic stroke older than 3 months and ongoing anticoagulation are relative contraindications requiring risk assessment rather than prohibitions. A blood pressure of 165/95 mm Hg sits below the severe hypertension threshold, and menstruation does not preclude fibrinolytic therapy.
  88. The correct answer is E (A bicuspid aortic valve, present in roughly half of coarctation patients): Bicuspid valves accompany coarctation in about fifty to seventy percent of cases and carry lifelong risks of stenosis, regurgitation, and aortopathy. This association mandates careful imaging of the valve and the ascending aorta. The other listed lesions occur independently and are not characteristic companions of coarctation.
  89. The correct answer is A (Low limb lead voltage on ECG despite increased ventricular wall thickness on echocardiography): amyloid infiltration thickens the walls without adding electrical mass, so the discordance between an echocardiogram suggesting hypertrophy and an ECG showing low or normal voltage is the hallmark clue, especially alongside bilateral carpal tunnel syndrome and spinal stenosis. True hypertrophy from pressure overload usually produces tall voltage that matches the imaging. Diffuse ST elevation suggests pericarditis, delta waves indicate preexcitation, and dilated thin walled ventricles describe a different cardiomyopathy altogether.
  90. The correct answer is C (Patients with prior myocardial infarction or other significant structural heart disease): The CAST trial showed increased mortality when class Ic sodium channel blockers were given to patients after myocardial infarction, and this proarrhythmic risk extends to significant structural heart disease more broadly, so flecainide and propafenone are reserved for structurally normal hearts. Lone atrial fibrillation in a normal heart is precisely the setting where these agents are appropriate. Controlled hypertension without hypertrophy and trivial mitral prolapse are not exclusions, and pairing a class Ic drug with a nodal blocking agent is actually advised to prevent rapid conduction if flutter emerges.
  91. The correct answer is B (Interleukin 1 blockade for recurrences that are colchicine resistant and corticosteroid dependent): recurrent pericarditis is increasingly understood as autoinflammatory, and interleukin 1 antagonists such as rilonacept and anakinra achieve remission and permit steroid withdrawal in exactly this refractory population. They are not first line agents for an initial episode. They have no role in gastric protection, they are not antiviral drugs, and their use is intended to avoid rather than bridge toward pericardiectomy in most recurrent cases.
  92. The correct answer is C (Accessory atrioventricular pathways producing Wolff Parkinson White pattern preexcitation and tachycardia): Ebstein anomaly is the congenital lesion most strongly linked to accessory pathways, which are often right sided and sometimes multiple. Reentrant tachycardia through these pathways explains her palpitations and should prompt electrophysiologic evaluation. Progressive block characterizes congenitally corrected transposition, and baffle related sinus node dysfunction follows atrial switch surgery.
  93. The correct answer is B (Adenosine receptor agonist vasodilators such as adenosine and dipyridamole infusions): nonselective adenosine receptor stimulation provokes bronchoconstriction and should be avoided in severe or poorly controlled bronchospastic disease. Dobutamine stimulates beta receptors and is the usual pharmacologic alternative for such patients. Beta blockers, atropine, and saline are procedural adjuncts rather than stress agents, and none is the class responsible for vasodilator induced bronchospasm.
  94. The correct answer is E (A rise or fall of troponin with at least one value above the 99th percentile): the universal definition requires a dynamic troponin change with at least one value above the 99th percentile upper reference limit plus evidence of ischemia. A single detectable value or a flat persistent elevation indicates injury without proving an acute event. Creatine kinase MB and natriuretic peptides are not the required biomarkers in the current definition.
  95. The correct answer is D (Chronic myocardial injury, shown by elevated values without a significant serial change): patients on dialysis commonly have stable troponin elevation, and the flat pattern between measurements indicates chronic injury rather than an acute event. Acute infarction requires a significant rise or fall, not simply two elevated values. Unstable angina requires troponin below the reference limit, and kidney disease raises troponin through reduced clearance and chronic injury rather than assay error.
  96. The correct answer is A (Regurgitant systolic flow transmits backward through the vena cava into the hepatic veins): with each systole the regurgitant jet raises right atrial pressure, and that pressure wave travels down the inferior vena cava into the hepatic venous bed, expanding the liver. This produces the expansile systolic pulsation that accompanies the large venous cv waves of severe tricuspid regurgitation. Arterial transmission through the hepatic artery or from the aorta produces a transmitted rather than expansile impulse. Diaphragmatic motion follows respiration, not the cardiac cycle, so it cannot explain a beat by beat pulsation.
  97. The correct answer is C (Stage B, defined by structural heart disease without current or prior heart failure symptoms): a reduced ejection fraction in a patient who has never had symptoms is the classic pre heart failure, stage B, presentation. Stage C requires current or prior symptoms, which she has never experienced, and stage D describes refractory symptoms despite maximal therapy. Stage A would require the absence of any structural abnormality, which her imaging rules out.
  98. The correct answer is B (Every 6 to 12 months, with earlier imaging for any change in symptoms): severe asymptomatic aortic stenosis progresses unpredictably, so guidelines recommend echocardiography every 6 to 12 months to track velocity, gradient, and ventricular function, and any new symptom prompts immediate reassessment. Intervals of 3 to 5 years are reserved for mild disease, and 1 to 2 years for moderate disease. Stopping surveillance until symptoms declare themselves risks missing ventricular decompensation, which is itself an indication for intervention in severe stenosis.
  99. The correct answer is C (A time of 120 minutes or less from first medical contact to device): transfer for primary intervention is preferred when the anticipated interval from first medical contact to device is 120 minutes or less. The clock starts at first medical contact, not at ambulance departure or symptom onset. A 30 minute interval describes the fibrinolytic administration goal, and no 150 minute allowance exists even with anticoagulation started before transfer.
  100. The correct answer is C (The reaction can first appear months or years after starting the medication): Angioedema from this class is bradykinin mediated and can surface at any point in therapy, including long after an uneventful start, which is why counseling cannot stop at the first refill. Because histamine is not the driver, antihistamines and corticosteroids work poorly, and management centers on stopping the drug and protecting the airway. Black patients have higher rates than White patients, and the reaction is a class effect, so switching within the class is unsafe.
  101. The correct answer is D (Class III, drugs that block potassium channels and prolong repolarization and refractoriness): Amiodarone, sotalol, dofetilide, and ibutilide are classified as class III agents because their dominant shared action is potassium channel blockade, which lengthens the action potential duration, the refractory period, and the QT interval. Sodium channel blockade with slowed phase zero defines class I, beta receptor antagonism defines class II, and calcium channel blockade defines class IV. Amiodarone and sotalol each carry additional properties from other classes, but their primary classification rests on repolarization prolongation.
  102. The correct answer is E (An echo free diastolic space measuring greater than 20 millimeters in width): standard grading calls an effusion mild below 10 millimeters, moderate from 10 to 20 millimeters, and large above 20 millimeters, measured as the diastolic echo free space. The 5 to 10 and 10 to 20 millimeter ranges therefore describe mild and moderate effusions. Mere circumferential visibility does not size an effusion, and a swinging heart suggests hemodynamic significance rather than defining the size category.
  103. The correct answer is A (Plasma volume rising 40 to 50 percent, higher cardiac output, and lower systemic vascular resistance): normal gestation expands plasma volume dramatically, raises cardiac output through both stroke volume and heart rate, and drops vascular resistance under progesterone and placental vasodilation, which explains mild tachycardia and flow murmurs. Plasma expands faster than red cell mass, producing the dilutional anemia of pregnancy rather than a rising hematocrit. Falling output and climbing resistance describe pathologic states, not healthy adaptation.
  104. The correct answer is B (QRS ≥150 milliseconds with a left bundle branch block morphology on ECG): The class I indication for resynchronization requires an ejection fraction of 35 percent or less, persistent symptoms on optimal therapy, sinus rhythm, and a left bundle branch block with a QRS of at least 150 milliseconds, the combination in which trials showed the clearest benefit. Right bundle branch block and nonspecific conduction delay patterns derive less benefit and carry weaker recommendations. Ejection fraction alone without QRS prolongation is not an indication, and isolated PR prolongation with a narrow QRS does not create dyssynchrony that resynchronization corrects.
  105. The correct answer is A (Liraglutide, subcutaneous semaglutide, and dulaglutide, each tested in large randomized trials): LEADER, SUSTAIN-6, and REWIND respectively showed these three agents lower major adverse cardiovascular events, driving guideline preference in atherosclerotic disease. Lixisenatide and short acting exenatide were neutral in their trials, so they carry no comparable indication. Dipeptidyl peptidase 4 inhibitors have not reduced cardiovascular events, and registry data on exenatide with insulin cannot establish outcome benefit.
  106. The correct answer is E (Age seventy five years or older, and prior stroke or transient ischemic attack): The two double weighted elements of CHA2DS2-VASc are age of at least 75 years and a history of stroke, transient ischemic attack, or systemic thromboembolism, each contributing two points. Hypertension, diabetes, heart failure, and female sex each contribute a single point however they are treated or characterized. The number of antihypertensive agents and the use of insulin do not change the points those conditions carry.
  107. The correct answer is B (A PR interval consistently longer than 200 milliseconds with every P wave conducted): First degree atrioventricular block is defined as a PR interval above 200 milliseconds with preserved one to one conduction, so it is a conduction delay rather than a true block of impulses. A PR shorter than 120 milliseconds suggests preexcitation or a junctional origin. Variable PR intervals with dropped complexes indicate second degree block, and nonconducted P waves of any kind move the diagnosis beyond first degree regardless of the measured PR value on conducted beats.
  108. The correct answer is A (It is the required confirmatory study, since echocardiography estimates rather than measures pulmonary hemodynamics): guidelines mandate right heart catheterization before diagnosing pulmonary arterial hypertension because direct measurement of mean pressure, wedge pressure, resistance, and output defines the hemodynamic subtype and anchors therapy, while echocardiographic pressure estimates can err in either direction. Delaying the study until oral therapies fail reverses the correct order, since treatment should never start on an unconfirmed diagnosis. Magnetic resonance characterizes the right ventricle but cannot replace pressure measurement, and catheterization has nothing to do with diuretic initiation.
  109. The correct answer is C (Dynamic subvalvular outflow obstruction caused by asymmetric septal thickening of the ventricle): a murmur that intensifies with standing and Valsalva strain yet softens with squatting and handgrip identifies obstruction that worsens when the ventricle is small, and the thickened septum with systolic anterior motion and a labile gradient completes the picture of hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy. Fixed valvular, subvalvular membrane, and supravalvular obstructions all soften when preload falls because flow decreases across a constant orifice. Prolapse produces a click murmur complex at the apex rather than a harsh sternal border ejection murmur, and no click is described here.
  110. The correct answer is A (Dilated intercostal collateral arteries eroding the undersurface of the adjacent ribs): Blood bypasses the aortic narrowing through internal mammary and intercostal collaterals, and the pulsatile enlarged intercostal vessels erode the inferior rib margins over years. The first two ribs are typically spared because their intercostal arteries arise proximal to the obstruction. Venous pressure, bronchial arteries, and osteopenia do not create this pattern.
  111. The correct answer is A (A morning plasma aldosterone concentration paired with plasma renin activity measurement): Screening for primary aldosteronism starts with the aldosterone to renin ratio, where an elevated aldosterone with suppressed renin suggests autonomous secretion and prompts confirmatory testing. Adrenal venous sampling and adrenal imaging are localizing steps reserved for biochemically proven disease. Metanephrine collection screens for pheochromocytoma and dexamethasone suppression screens for cortisol excess, neither of which explains hypokalemic hypertension as directly.
  112. The correct answer is D (Elevated blood pressure, systolic 120 to 129 with a diastolic remaining below 80): Her systolic of 124 with a diastolic of 76 fits the elevated category exactly as the 2017 ACC/AHA guideline defines it. Normal requires a systolic under 120, while stage 1 begins at systolic 130 or diastolic 80, so neither label applies. A white coat effect cannot be invoked without out of office readings showing lower values than the office produced.
  113. The correct answer is B (Reentry using a slow pathway anterogradely and a fast pathway retrogradely within nodal tissue): Typical AVNRT depends on dual AV nodal physiology, with the impulse conducting down the slow pathway and back up the fast pathway so that atria and ventricles activate nearly simultaneously and the retrograde P wave hides within the QRS. Reentry that returns up an accessory atrioventricular pathway describes orthodromic AVRT, a different circuit. Focal automaticity near the crista terminalis and macroreentry around the tricuspid annulus describe focal atrial tachycardia and typical atrial flutter respectively.
  114. The correct answer is C (Cytochrome P450 2C19, which performs the required two step oxidative bioactivation): clopidogrel is a prodrug, and loss of function 2C19 alleles reduce generation of the active metabolite and weaken platelet inhibition. Cytochrome 3A4 contributes to statin metabolism but is not the key determinant tested for clopidogrel response. Glucuronosyltransferase, plasma esterases, and thiopurine methyltransferase do not produce the active antiplatelet metabolite.
  115. The correct answer is B (A fall of ≥20 mm Hg systolic or ≥10 diastolic within three minutes): The consensus definition requires a sustained drop of at least 20 mm Hg systolic or 10 mm Hg diastolic within three minutes of standing, regardless of symptoms. Symptoms alone without the numeric fall, or arbitrary absolute values such as 100 mm Hg, do not define the condition. A heart rate rise above 30 beats per minute without hypotension defines postural tachycardia syndrome instead.
  116. The correct answer is A (Persistent atrial fibrillation, meaning continuous arrhythmia sustained for more than seven days): Nine months of continuous atrial fibrillation exceeds the seven day threshold for persistent disease but has not yet reached the twelve continuous months required for the long standing persistent category. The paroxysmal label depends on episode duration, not on whether rhythm control is being pursued. Permanent describes a joint decision to abandon rhythm control, which contradicts her planned ablation, and subclinical refers to device detected arrhythmia without clinical documentation, the opposite of her situation.
  117. The correct answer is C (A rise in jugular venous pressure with inspiration and an early diastolic knock): the rigid pericardium prevents the right heart from accepting the inspiratory increase in venous return, producing the Kussmaul sign, and abrupt cessation of early diastolic filling generates the pericardial knock. A fourth heart sound with a sustained apex suggests hypertensive or hypertrophic disease. A click with late systolic murmur indicates mitral prolapse, bounding pulses indicate aortic regurgitation, and a murmur augmented by leg raising reflects flow dependent valvular lesions.
  118. The correct answer is D (Elevated cardiac output with low systemic vascular resistance and congestive symptoms): severe anemia lowers blood viscosity and tissue oxygen delivery, so vasodilation and reflex increases in stroke volume and heart rate raise output while congestion develops despite a hyperdynamic circulation. Cold extremities with high resistance and low output describe cardiogenic shock physiology, and impaired relaxation with normal output describes diastolic dysfunction. Iron excess injuring the myocardium is a feature of hemochromatosis, not of anemia driven high output states.
  119. The correct answer is E (Transfer for coronary angiography, ideally performed within 2 to 24 hours): the pharmacoinvasive strategy calls for routine transfer and angiography within 2 to 24 hours even after apparently successful fibrinolysis. Rescue angioplasty is reserved for failed reperfusion, and waiting for recurrent pain forfeits the benefit of early definitive treatment. Repeat fibrinolytic dosing and discharge with deferred stress testing are not recommended approaches after fibrinolysis for ST elevation infarction.
  120. The correct answer is A (Non ST elevation myocardial infarction with a dynamic rise in troponin values): the rising troponin pattern with ischemic ST depression and no ST elevation defines this diagnosis. Unstable angina requires troponin values that stay below the 99th percentile, which these results exclude. ST elevation infarction is excluded by the electrocardiogram, while stable angina and pericarditis do not produce this dynamic biomarker rise.
  121. The correct answer is E (Gain of function in the cardiac sodium channel with persistent late inward current): Long QT type 3 arises from SCN5A gain of function mutations that impair sodium channel inactivation, allowing a persistent late inward current that prolongs the action potential plateau and lengthens the QT interval, with events typically at rest or during sleep. Loss of function in the slow and rapid delayed rectifier potassium currents defines types 1 and 2 respectively. Reduced L type calcium current would shorten rather than lengthen repolarization, and inward rectifier gain of function underlies short QT physiology.
  122. The correct answer is E (Short PR interval, slurred initial QRS upstroke, and a widened QRS complex): Anterograde conduction over an accessory pathway bypasses normal AV nodal delay, shortening the PR interval, and early eccentric ventricular activation inscribes the slurred delta wave that widens the QRS. Epsilon waves with right precordial T inversion suggest arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy, and deep septal Q waves with high voltage suggest hypertrophic cardiomyopathy. A prolonged PR interval with a narrow QRS is the opposite of preexcitation, and a short QT with peaked T waves points toward hyperkalemia or short QT syndrome.
  123. The correct answer is D (Retrograde vertebral artery flow diverting blood from the brainstem to the exercising arm): a proximal subclavian stenosis upstream of the vertebral origin causes the exercising arm to recruit blood retrograde down the ipsilateral vertebral artery, stealing flow from the posterior circulation and producing vertebrobasilar symptoms. The interarm pressure gradient localizes the obstruction, and symptoms tied specifically to arm work distinguish steal from embolization, which occurs unpredictably. Osteophyte compression relates to neck position rather than arm use, and neither orthostasis nor carotid sinus sensitivity explains the pressure differential.
  124. The correct answer is B (Therapy targets the underlying left heart disease, because pulmonary arterial hypertension drugs can worsen congestion): her wedge pressure above 15 mmHg marks group 2 postcapillary disease, where trials of endothelin antagonists, phosphodiesterase inhibitors, and prostanoids have shown no benefit and sometimes harm, since dilating the pulmonary bed increases flow into a congested left heart. Management therefore centers on decongestion, blood pressure control, and treating the preserved ejection fraction syndrome itself. Pressure magnitude alone does not trigger vasodilator use, and none of the vasodilator strategies listed is appropriate here.
  125. The correct answer is B (Fever with a documented body temperature higher than 38 degrees Celsius on admission): fever is one of the Duke minor criteria, alongside a predisposing condition or injection drug use, vascular phenomena, immunologic phenomena, and microbiologic evidence not meeting the major criterion. An oscillating mass and new valvular regurgitation are echocardiographic major criteria demonstrating endocardial involvement. Two cultures of a typical organism such as Streptococcus gallolyticus satisfy the microbiologic major criterion. Coxiella burnetii is a special case in which a single positive culture or a high phase one antibody titer also counts as a major criterion.
  126. The correct answer is D (Regurgitation caused by intrinsic disease of the leaflets, chordae, or other valve components): primary mitral regurgitation means the valve apparatus itself is diseased, as with myxomatous degeneration, flail segments, chordal rupture, rheumatic injury, or endocarditis. Correcting the valve corrects the disease in this form, which is why surgical repair is favored. Regurgitation from ventricular dilation, ischemic tethering of an intact leaflet, or atrial annular enlargement describes secondary disease, where the valve is a bystander to chamber pathology. Transient ischemic regurgitation likewise reflects myocardial rather than valvular disease.
  127. The correct answer is A (Expansion greater than 0.5 centimeters within six months, even below the diameter threshold): expansion exceeding 0.5 centimeters over six months marks a biologically unstable aneurysm with elevated rupture risk, so rapid growth is an accepted repair indication independent of reaching 5.5 centimeters. Small measurement differences between studies often reflect technique variability, so minor enlargement or slow growth over years does not justify surgery. A fixed fraction of the diameter threshold has no role in timing repair decisions.
  128. The correct answer is B (Reassess ejection fraction after at least 3 months of guideline directed medical therapy): newly diagnosed nonischemic cardiomyopathy often improves substantially on the four pillars, so the guideline requires a trial of optimized therapy, generally at least 3 months, with repeat imaging before committing to a primary prevention device. Immediate implantation would expose many patients whose ejection fraction recovers to unnecessary hardware. Electrophysiology studies do not reliably risk stratify nonischemic disease, and neither genetic testing nor biopsy is a mandatory gate, although selected findings such as lamin variants can influence decisions.
  129. The correct answer is A (NYHA class reflects current symptom severity while ACC/AHA stages advance without moving backward): stages describe disease development and progression, so a patient who has ever had symptomatic heart failure stays at least stage C even when treatment renders them asymptomatic. NYHA class, by contrast, grades present functional limitation and can improve or worsen over time. The two systems measure different things, are used together within stages C and D, and neither is restricted to one ejection fraction category.
  130. The correct answer is A (Obesity lowers measured natriuretic peptide levels, so true heart failure may be underrecognized): obese patients have lower circulating natriuretic peptide concentrations, likely through reduced production and increased clearance, so a value below the usual rule out threshold cannot exclude heart failure when the examination shows clear congestion. His bedside findings make heart failure likely despite the number. Pericardial constriction is not suggested by an S3 with orthopnea in a patient with severe obesity, lipemia interference is not a recognized cause of this pattern, and age does not suppress peptide synthesis.
  131. The correct answer is E (Large stroke volume raising systolic pressure while diastolic runoff into the ventricle lowers diastolic pressure): the volume loaded ventricle ejects an augmented stroke volume, elevating systolic pressure, and regurgitation back through the incompetent valve drains the aorta during diastole. This combination produces the wide pulse pressure and the bounding peripheral pulses that underlie the classic named signs. Catecholamine excess and medication related vasodilation are not the primary valve mediated mechanisms. Isolated aortic stiffening raises systolic pressure in elderly hypertension but does not create the profound diastolic runoff seen here.
  132. The correct answer is A (A lipoprotein(a) concentration of 50 mg/dL or higher measured on a validated assay): guidelines list a lipoprotein(a) of 50 mg/dL or 125 nmol/L or higher as a risk enhancing factor that can tip a borderline risk patient toward statin therapy. A modestly favorable high density lipoprotein level, a normal home blood pressure, and a grandparent with infarction at 78 do not qualify, since the family history enhancer requires premature disease in a first degree relative. Resting heart rate is not part of the risk enhancer list.
  133. The correct answer is A (The right coronary artery, which supplies the inferior wall in most patients): elevation in leads II, III, and aVF localizes injury to the inferior wall, which the right coronary artery perfuses in roughly 85 percent of people. The accompanying bradycardia fits this vessel because it usually supplies the sinoatrial and atrioventricular nodes. Left anterior descending and left main occlusions produce anterior or widespread precordial changes, while obtuse marginal and ramus occlusions produce lateral patterns.
  134. The correct answer is C (The click arrives earlier in systole as the smaller ventricle prolapses sooner): Valsalva strain lowers venous return, and in a smaller ventricle the chordae and redundant leaflet reach their tension point earlier in ejection, moving the click toward S1 and lengthening the murmur that follows it. A later click reflects maneuvers that enlarge the ventricle, such as squatting or passive leg raising. The click is a systolic event and does not convert to an opening sound, which belongs to mitral stenosis. Movement is toward the first heart sound rather than into S2, and a doubled click is not the expected maneuver response.
  135. The correct answer is A (The water hammer pulse, also called the Corrigan pulse in classic descriptions): the augmented stroke volume produces a slapping rapid upstroke, and diastolic runoff through the incompetent valve causes the abrupt collapse, a combination accentuated by elevating the arm. Pulsus parvus et tardus is the weak delayed pulse of severe aortic stenosis, the hemodynamic opposite.
