Pulsus paradoxus points toward tamponade or severe airway obstruction, and pulsus alternans indicates advanced ventricular systolic failure. A dicrotic pulse with a palpable diastolic wave occurs in low output states with compliant vessels rather than regurgitant runoff.
  136. The correct answer is D (Ventricular bigeminy with a premature wide complex following each sinus beat): The strip shows an alternating pattern in which every conducted sinus beat is followed by an early, wide, bizarre complex without a preceding premature P wave, the definition of ventricular bigeminy. Atrial bigeminy also alternates but produces narrow early complexes preceded by premature P waves. Grouped beating from second degree block features dropped beats rather than added early ones, aberrancy would follow premature atrial activity, and atrial fibrillation would show an irregularly irregular rhythm without a repeating paired pattern.
  137. The correct answer is C (It distinguishes truly severe stenosis from pseudosevere disease by augmenting forward flow): in classical low flow, low gradient stenosis with reduced ejection fraction, dobutamine increases stroke volume, and a truly severe valve shows a rising gradient with a persistently small orifice while a pseudosevere valve opens further as flow improves. The test also identifies contractile reserve, which informs operative risk discussion. It is not designed to measure coronary flow reserve or provoke vasospasm, which involve different pharmacologic protocols. Stress evaluation of mitral regurgitation and functional capacity testing are separate indications that do not address this diagnostic question.
  138. The correct answer is D (It becomes louder, because reduced preload shrinks the cavity and worsens dynamic obstruction): Valsalva strain and abrupt standing both decrease venous return, and a smaller left ventricular cavity brings the hypertrophied septum and the anteriorly moving mitral apparatus closer together, intensifying the gradient and the murmur. This behavior is the opposite of aortic stenosis, whose fixed obstruction softens with these maneuvers. Venous return falls rather than rises during both actions, and the obstruction in this disease is dynamic, not fixed.
  139. The correct answer is B (It grows louder because increased afterload drives more backward flow into the atrium): handgrip raises systemic vascular resistance, so a larger fraction of the stroke volume regurgitates through the mitral valve and the murmur intensifies. The same maneuver softens the murmurs of aortic stenosis and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy because forward ejection and the dynamic gradient both fall. Softening with afterload describes those outflow lesions rather than regurgitation. A shift of a click later in systole is the response of mitral valve prolapse to maneuvers that enlarge the ventricle, not a defining handgrip response of established regurgitation.
  140. The correct answer is D (Heart rate, contractility, and ventricular wall tension are the principal determinants): oxygen demand rises with faster rates, stronger inotropy, and greater wall stress, which itself scales with chamber pressure and radius by the Laplace relationship, and beta blockade lowers all three. Hemoglobin, saturation, and perfusion pressure govern oxygen supply rather than demand. Diastolic filling time and collaterals also belong to the supply side, catecholamine clearance and thyroid state act only through the primary determinants, and the viscosity based option lists no cardiac demand variable.
  141. The correct answer is D (Ostial and proximal stenoses, often involving the left main, appearing years after exposure): radiation injures vessels within the treated field, producing ostial and proximal stenoses, frequent left main involvement, and a latency of a decade or more after exposure. The disease is proximal rather than distal small vessel, is fibrotic and fixed rather than spastic, and does not present as triple vessel ectasia or acquired bridging. Young survivors can therefore develop surgical anatomy despite few conventional risk factors.
  142. The correct answer is C (Renin angiotensin system inhibitor, evidence based beta blocker, mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist, and SGLT2 inhibitor): the four pillars are an ARNI, ACE inhibitor, or ARB, one of the three proven beta blockers, a mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist, and an SGLT2 inhibitor, each carrying mortality or hospitalization benefit. Loop diuretics relieve congestion but do not improve survival, so they sit outside the pillars. Ivabradine, hydralazine with isosorbide dinitrate, digoxin, statins, and potassium binders are adjuncts for selected patients rather than foundational therapy.
  143. The correct answer is D (Atrial fibrillation, which elevates natriuretic peptide levels even without ventricular decompensation): atrial stretch and the arrhythmia itself raise BNP and NT proBNP, so atrial fibrillation is a recognized noncardiac failure confounder alongside renal impairment and advancing age. Asymptomatic patients can have modest elevations without any laboratory error. A structurally normal echocardiogram argues against hidden valvular disease, and thyroid disease is a possible but far less common explanation than the arrhythmia she already carries.
  144. The correct answer is D (Estimated GFR above 30 mL/min/1.73 m² with serum potassium below 5.0 mEq per liter): the guideline restricts mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist initiation to patients meeting both thresholds, with potassium and renal function rechecked after starting and after dose changes because hyperkalemia is the principal danger. An estimated GFR of 60 and a potassium of 4.0 are stricter than anything the guideline requires. Creatinine based cutoffs of 2.5 mg/dL in men and 2.0 mg/dL in women are the traditional equivalents, not 3.0 mg/dL, and sodium or magnesium levels are not gating criteria.
  145. The correct answer is E (Radiation upward into both carotid arteries in the neck during systole): the aortic stenosis murmur follows the direction of turbulent flow into the ascending aorta and great vessels, so it is heard over the carotids. Axillary radiation characterizes mitral regurgitation, whose jet is directed into the left atrium. Inspiratory augmentation at the lower sternal border points to right sided lesions such as tricuspid regurgitation, and interscapular radiation is classically described with coarctation of the aorta.
  146. The correct answer is E (Heart failure of any severity, given increased mortality seen with related phosphodiesterase inhibitors): cilostazol is a phosphodiesterase three inhibitor, and oral agents of this class increased mortality in patients with heart failure, so the drug carries a boxed contraindication in heart failure of any severity. Pulmonary disease, moderate kidney disease, diabetes, and prior stroke do not preclude its use, although dose adjustment or caution may apply in some situations. Screening for a heart failure history is therefore a required step before this medication is prescribed.
  147. The correct answer is D (Chronic limb threatening ischemia, defined by rest pain with nonhealing tissue loss): ischemic rest pain relieved by dependency, together with tissue loss lasting more than two weeks, defines chronic limb threatening ischemia, the most advanced stage of chronic peripheral artery disease. Claudication produces exertional pain with preserved resting perfusion and no ulceration. Neuropathy causes numbness and dysesthesia but not positional relief through gravity assisted perfusion, and acute limb ischemia presents over hours with abrupt pulse loss rather than weeks of ulceration.
  148. The correct answer is D (Right heart enlargement with a measured Qp:Qs shunt ratio ≥ 1.5): Closure is recommended when the shunt is large enough to enlarge the right atrium and ventricle, reflected by a ratio ≥ 1.5, provided pulmonary pressures permit. Flow with normal chamber dimensions does not by itself establish hemodynamic significance. Defect diameter and septal motion are supportive observations rather than the criteria that drive the decision.
  149. The correct answer is E (A mean pressure fall of ≥10 mmHg to below 40 mmHg with preserved cardiac output): the accepted positive response requires the mean pulmonary arterial pressure to drop by at least 10 mmHg, reach an absolute value under 40 mmHg, and do so with unchanged or improved output, identifying the small group who may respond durably to calcium channel blockade. Symptom change and modest resistance falls do not meet the definition. Wedge pressure behavior is not part of the criteria, and an isolated output rise without the required pressure fall does not qualify.
  150. The correct answer is C (Rupture of the left ventricular free wall with hemopericardium and tamponade): sudden pulseless electrical activity with a large effusion and right ventricular diastolic collapse within the first week of an unreperfused infarction is the classic presentation of free wall rupture. Pulmonary embolism can cause pulseless electrical activity but not an acute pericardial effusion. Stent thrombosis is impossible without a stent, and papillary muscle or septal rupture produces pulmonary edema or a shunt rather than tamponade physiology.
  151. The correct answer is C (Reduce dosing to 1 mg/kg once daily when creatinine clearance falls below 30 mL/min): enoxaparin is renally cleared and accumulates in severe impairment, so the therapeutic dose drops from twice daily to once daily below this clearance threshold. Increasing the dose or continuing the standard regimen with monitoring alone invites bleeding from accumulation. Fondaparinux is itself contraindicated at this level of renal dysfunction, so switching to it would not be safe.
  152. The correct answer is A (Masked hypertension, normal office readings with elevated out of office averages): His office values look acceptable while a properly conducted week of home monitoring shows sustained elevation, which defines masked hypertension. This pattern carries cardiovascular risk similar to sustained hypertension and is easy to miss when decisions rest on office numbers alone. White coat hypertension is the mirror image, pseudohypertension involves rigid calcified vessels, and nondipping describes nocturnal ambulatory behavior rather than this office versus home discrepancy.
  153. The correct answer is C (Emergency open surgical repair, because ascending aortic involvement carries hourly mortality risk): ascending aortic involvement defines a type A dissection, which kills untreated patients at a rate of roughly one to two percent per hour from tamponade, rupture, or coronary compromise, so emergency open repair is the standard of care. A medical first strategy applies to uncomplicated type B disease, not to the ascending aorta. Endovascular grafting is not established therapy for the ascending segment, and coronary angiography delays lifesaving surgery while the computed tomography diagnosis is already secure.
  154. The correct answer is B (Unexplained left ventricular wall thickness of 15 mm or more in an adult): hypertrophic cardiomyopathy is diagnosed when maximal left ventricular wall thickness reaches 15 mm without another cause such as hypertension or aortic stenosis, and a lower threshold of 13 to 14 mm applies when a family history or a pathogenic sarcomere variant is present. A measurement of 11 mm sits within normal limits. Thickening explained by hypertension does not qualify, 20 mm is not the diagnostic floor, and right ventricular measurements are not the defining criterion.
  155. The correct answer is E (Large fused cv waves produced by systolic flow regurgitating into the right atrium): during ventricular systole the incompetent tricuspid valve transmits ventricular pressure and volume directly into the right atrium, merging the c and v waves into a tall systolic wave with a rapid y descent. Prominent a waves and a slow y descent characterize tricuspid stenosis or impaired right ventricular filling. Cannon a waves reflect atrioventricular dissociation, as in complete heart block. Venous pulsations are exaggerated rather than absent, since regurgitant flow distends the jugular column.
  156. The correct answer is D (An orifice of 1.5 cm² or smaller, typically with a prolonged pressure half time): the guideline defines severe rheumatic mitral stenosis at a valve area of 1.5 cm² or less, usually corresponding to a pressure half time of 150 msec or more. The normal mitral orifice measures about 4.0 to 6.0 cm², so areas near 2.0 to 3.0 cm² represent progressive but not severe narrowing. Symptoms can occur at larger areas with exertion or tachycardia, but the severity classification itself rests on the 1.5 cm² threshold.
  157. The correct answer is D (Lisinopril plus spironolactone with regular ibuprofen use in stage 3 kidney disease): This regimen stacks four independent potassium raising influences, angiotensin blockade, aldosterone antagonism, prostaglandin inhibition that suppresses renin and reduces renal perfusion, and impaired baseline excretion from kidney disease. Diuretic containing combinations tend to lower potassium instead. Losartan alone with excellent filtration, vasodilator combinations, and beta blockade with a thiazide each pose far smaller risks of a dangerous rise.
  158. The correct answer is A (Quincke sign, which reflects transmission of the exaggerated pulse into the capillary circulation): visible nail bed capillary pulsation with gentle nail tip compression is the Quincke sign of chronic aortic regurgitation. Head bobbing with each systole is the de Musset sign, and the femoral findings are Duroziez sign, a double sound with partial compression, and Traube sign, a pistol shot sound without compression. The abrupt rise and collapse of the carotid pulse is the Corrigan sign, a large vessel rather than capillary finding.
  159. The correct answer is C (Plasma free metanephrines or urinary fractionated metanephrines collected over 24 hours): The triad of episodic headache, sweating, and palpitations with paroxysmal severe hypertension suggests pheochromocytoma, and metanephrines are the recommended screen because tumors methylate catecholamines continuously, keeping these metabolites elevated even between spells. Random catecholamines drawn during a quiet period are frequently normal and insensitive. Aldosterone testing targets sustained hypokalemic hypertension, and cortisol and thyroid studies address different syndromes.
  160. The correct answer is E (Holodiastolic flow reversal in the proximal descending aorta with vena contracta above 0.6 cm): severe aortic regurgitation is supported by a vena contracta wider than 0.6 cm, a regurgitant volume of 60 mL or more, a regurgitant fraction of 50 percent or more, an effective orifice of 0.3 cm² or more, and flow reversal throughout diastole in the descending aorta. A 0.2 cm vena contracta, a 20 percent fraction, and a 25 mL volume all sit in the mild range. Brief early diastolic reversal is a normal or mild finding, while the holodiastolic pattern reflects substantial runoff back into the ventricle.
  161. The correct answer is A (Young age, symmetric attacks, spared thumbs, and absence of any tissue injury): primary Raynaud phenomenon typically begins in young women, produces symmetric attacks that spare the thumbs, and never causes tissue damage, with normal nailfold capillaries and negative serologies. Older onset, asymmetry, digital pitting, ulceration, abnormal capillaroscopy, and high titer autoantibodies all point toward a secondary cause such as systemic sclerosis. Recognizing the benign pattern spares a young patient unnecessary invasive evaluation while identifying who genuinely needs serologic workup.
  162. The correct answer is B (An acute, rapidly progressive infection that can destroy previously normal valve tissue): Staphylococcus aureus is the classic cause of acute endocarditis, producing high fever, rapid valve destruction, abscess formation, and embolic complications within days, and it readily infects structurally normal valves, including the tricuspid valve in people who inject drugs. The indolent months long course with constitutional symptoms describes subacute disease from viridans streptococci seeded onto previously damaged valves. Persistently sterile cultures suggest fastidious or serology dependent organisms rather than this pathogen. A silent interval followed by isolated conduction disease is not a recognized presentation of staphylococcal endocarditis.
  163. The correct answer is C (Marked biatrial enlargement with reduced tissue Doppler velocities and nondilated ventricles): stiff, noncompliant ventricles in restrictive cardiomyopathy transmit chronically high filling pressures backward, dilating both atria while the ventricles themselves stay normal in size with preserved ejection fraction, and intrinsic myocardial disease depresses tissue Doppler velocities. Dilated thin walled ventricles describe dilated cardiomyopathy. Systolic anterior motion belongs to hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, and isolated right ventricular dilation with regional aneurysms suggests arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy.
  164. The correct answer is D (5.5 centimeters, the diameter at which repair benefit outweighs procedural risk in men): randomized trials of early surgery versus surveillance showed no survival advantage for repairing smaller aneurysms in men, so 5.5 centimeters is the accepted elective threshold at average risk. A 4.5 or 5.0 centimeter trigger would expose men to operative risk without proven benefit, and 5.0 centimeters is the threshold used for women rather than for both sexes. Waiting until 6.0 or 6.5 centimeters accepts a substantially higher annual rupture risk than modern repair carries.
  165. The correct answer is D (Infranodal block or a transplanted denervated heart, where vagolytic effects accomplish little): Atropine works by removing vagal influence on the sinus and AV nodes, so it cannot improve conduction in His Purkinje disease and has no substrate to act on in a denervated transplanted heart, and in infranodal block it may even worsen the ratio by accelerating atrial rates. Vagally mediated sinus bradycardia after inferior infarction and nodal Wenckebach are the settings where atropine is most effective. Sinus node dysfunction and beta blocker effect usually retain some response to vagolysis while other measures are arranged.
  166. The correct answer is A (Asynchronous pacing at a fixed magnet rate without sensing of intrinsic beats): A magnet over a pacemaker closes a reed switch that disables sensing and forces asynchronous pacing at a device specific magnet rate, which protects against inappropriate inhibition when electrocautery noise is misread as intrinsic beats. Pacing output is not shut off, and the device does not deliver shocks or switch into a rate responsive sensing mode. Suspension of therapy with continued detection describes the magnet response of a defibrillator, whose tachyarrhythmia therapies are disabled while pacing behavior is unchanged.
  167. The correct answer is D (An inspiratory fall in systolic blood pressure exceeding 10 mmHg during quiet breathing): tamponade exaggerates the normal inspiratory decline in systolic pressure beyond 10 mmHg because ventricular interdependence lets inspiratory right heart filling compress left ventricular stroke volume. A diastolic rise with expiration is not the defined measurement. Interarm pressure differences suggest aortic or subclavian disease, positional pulse loss suggests arterial obstruction, and an orthostatic heart rate rise reflects volume status or dysautonomia.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Within 30 minutes of arrival, the guideline door to needle performance goal): when fibrinolysis is the chosen reperfusion strategy, the drug should be given within 30 minutes of hospital arrival. The 90 and 120 minute intervals are device goals for direct presentation and transfer, and the 10 minute target applies to acquiring the first electrocardiogram. Waiting for serial troponin results delays reperfusion and is never required once the electrocardiogram is diagnostic.
  2. The correct answer is B (Expanded plasma volume, epicardial fat inflammation, and increased ventricular stiffness with microvascular dysfunction): obesity raises circulating volume, and inflamed visceral and epicardial adipose tissue promotes myocardial fibrosis, coronary microvascular disease, and the stiff ventricle that defines the obese phenotype of heart failure with preserved ejection fraction. Silent infarction from plaque rupture would produce a reduced ejection fraction picture instead. Valvular lipid deposition, sinus node denervation, and fatty right ventricular replacement are not the mechanisms tying obesity to this syndrome.
  3. The correct answer is D (Acute cardiac dysfunction produces acute kidney injury through congestion and reduced renal perfusion): type 1 cardiorenal syndrome describes worsening renal function driven by acute heart failure, where elevated venous pressure and low forward flow injure the kidney. Acute kidney injury provoking cardiac decompensation describes type 3, chronic heart failure causing gradual nephron loss describes type 2, and a systemic insult injuring both organs describes type 5. Chronic kidney disease driving hypertrophy and vascular calcification is the type 4 pattern.
  4. The correct answer is D (A midsystolic crescendo decrescendo murmur whose peak moves later as severity increases): aortic stenosis produces an ejection murmur that rises and falls with systolic flow, and as obstruction worsens the peak shifts later in systole while A2 softens and the carotid upstroke becomes small and delayed. A uniform holosystolic murmur indicates regurgitation through the mitral or tricuspid valve or a ventricular septal defect. An early diastolic decrescendo murmur is aortic regurgitation, and an apical middiastolic rumble with presystolic accentuation is mitral stenosis. A continuous murmur beneath the left clavicle suggests a patent ductus arteriosus.
  5. The correct answer is B (Chlorthalidone, a thiazide type diuretic with long duration and outcome trial support): The four preferred first line classes are thiazide type diuretics, angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors, angiotensin receptor blockers, and calcium channel blockers. Beta blockers are reserved for compelling indications such as ischemic heart disease or reduced ejection fraction rather than uncomplicated hypertension. Alpha blockers, central agonists, and loop diuretics lack comparable outcome evidence and sit further down the treatment sequence.
  6. The correct answer is D (Start high dose glucocorticoids immediately, because biopsy findings persist for about two weeks): treatment should never wait for tissue confirmation because irreversible vision loss can occur while the biopsy is pending, and characteristic histologic changes remain detectable for roughly two weeks after glucocorticoids begin. Starting after biopsy or after imaging leaves the patient unprotected during the highest risk window. Low dose therapy is inadequate for active cranial arteritis, and tocilizumab is an adjunct for maintaining remission rather than a substitute for initial high dose glucocorticoids.
  7. The correct answer is C (The internal mammary artery shows roughly 90 percent patency at ten years): the left internal mammary artery resists atherosclerosis and remains patent in roughly 90 percent of patients at ten years, clearly exceeding vein graft performance. Vein grafts degenerate rather than improve with time, radial grafts generally outperform veins to suitable targets, and antiplatelet therapy narrows but does not erase the conduit difference. This patency advantage underlies the survival benefit of mammary grafting to the anterior descending.
  8. The correct answer is E (Bisoprolol, carvedilol, and sustained release metoprolol succinate, the agents with proven mortality benefit): these three reduced death in CIBIS II, COPERNICUS and related trials, and MERIT HF respectively, so the guideline names them specifically rather than endorsing the class. Metoprolol tartrate has not shown equivalent survival benefit and is not an accepted substitute. Atenolol, propranolol, nadolol, labetalol, and sotalol lack HFrEF mortality evidence, and sotalol adds proarrhythmic risk.
  9. The correct answer is A (Enalapril 10 to 20 mg twice daily, the dosing range proven in mortality trials): the target doses in the guideline reflect what the survival trials actually used, such as enalapril 10 to 20 mg twice daily and lisinopril 20 to 40 mg daily. The 2.5 mg and 6.25 mg regimens listed are starting doses, not maintenance targets. Higher doses of ACE inhibitors outperformed very low doses for clinical outcomes, so deliberately staying at the lowest dose sacrifices benefit.
  10. The correct answer is C (An ostium secundum defect, the most common form seen in adult patients): Secundum defects arise at the fossa ovalis and account for roughly seventy percent of atrial septal defects. Primum defects lie low in the septum next to the atrioventricular valves, often with a cleft mitral valve. Sinus venosus and coronary sinus defects sit outside the fossa ovalis region, near the caval veins or the coronary sinus.
  11. The correct answer is C (Intravenous ferric carboxymaltose, because oral iron failed to improve outcomes in trials): intravenous iron improved symptoms, functional capacity, and quality of life in FAIR HF and related studies, and the guideline gives it a 2a recommendation for iron deficient patients with HFrEF. High dose oral iron did not replete iron stores or improve exercise capacity in the IRONOUT HF trial, so tablets and diet are inadequate. Erythropoiesis stimulating agents increased thromboembolic events without benefit in heart failure, and transfusion is reserved for severe symptomatic anemia.
  12. The correct answer is B (A very low baseline concentration combined with an absent or minimal 1 hour change): rapid rule out requires a very low starting concentration together with a minimal delta on the 1 hour repeat. A baseline above the 99th percentile or a large upward delta moves the patient toward rule in rather than rule out. Symptom resolution and a normal electrocardiogram do not replace the serial biomarker measurements the algorithm requires.
  13. The correct answer is A (Dental work that manipulates gingival tissue or perforates the oral mucosa): for high risk patients, prophylaxis is recommended before dental procedures involving manipulation of gingival tissue, the periapical region of teeth, or perforation of the oral mucosa, because these reliably produce bacteremia with viridans streptococci. Routine gastrointestinal and genitourinary procedures, including colonoscopy and cystoscopy without active infection, do not warrant prophylaxis under current recommendations. Transesophageal echocardiography and uncomplicated vaginal delivery are likewise excluded when no established infection is present.
  14. The correct answer is E (Complete atrioventricular block, acquired at a rate of roughly two percent yearly): The abnormally positioned and fragile conduction axis in congenitally corrected transposition degenerates over time, producing an incremental annual risk of complete heart block. Many patients ultimately require pacing, and pacing choices must account for the systemic right ventricle. Sinus node dysfunction is instead the hallmark of atrial switch survivors.
  15. The correct answer is C (Serial imaging of the aortic root and ascending aorta, with interval guided by size): bicuspid valves carry an intrinsic aortopathy, and a diameter of 4.2 cm warrants ongoing surveillance with echocardiography, or with computed tomography or magnetic resonance when the aorta is not well seen, at intervals determined by dimension and growth rate. Waiting until the surgical threshold would miss the growth trajectory that guides timing and family counseling. Normal valve function does not remove the aortic risk, since dilation progresses independently of stenosis or regurgitation. Invasive angiography and plain radiographs are not appropriate tools for measuring aortic size.
  16. The correct answer is B (Complete occlusion with TIMI 0 flow estimated to be at least three months old): a chronic total occlusion is defined as complete occlusion with TIMI 0 antegrade flow and an estimated duration of at least three months. High grade stenoses with delayed filling and lesions with faint TIMI 1 flow are functional occlusions rather than true chronic total occlusions, and neither territory viability nor prior stenting is part of the definition. Duration is usually estimated from symptom history or prior imaging.
  17. The correct answer is D (Dobutamine infusion titrated upward, with atropine added if heart rate response is inadequate): dobutamine raises heart rate and contractility through beta stimulation, providing diagnostic stress without adenosine receptor activation, and atropine supplements an insufficient chronotropic response. Adenosine and a routine regadenoson protocol both carry bronchospasm risk in severe reactive airway disease with prior vasodilator intolerance. A single treadmill stage gives inadequate workload in a patient limited by claudication, and cold pressor testing is not a diagnostic stress modality.
  18. The correct answer is A (Focal atrial tachycardia arising from an automatic or microreentrant atrial site): A warm up in rate at onset, a P wave axis different from sinus, and persistence of atrial activity despite AV nodal slowing all point to a focal atrial source that does not need the AV node to sustain itself. Nodal reentry and accessory pathway reentry both depend on the AV node, so vagal maneuvers typically terminate them rather than merely slowing conduction. Sinus tachycardia produces a normal P wave axis, and junctional ectopic tachycardia arises below the atria rather than from an ectopic atrial focus.
  19. The correct answer is D (Supervised exercise training with risk factor modification, education, and psychosocial support): comprehensive cardiac rehabilitation combines structured exercise with counseling on lipids, blood pressure, diabetes, smoking, nutrition, and psychological health. Telephone refill reminders and an unsupervised walking diary omit the supervised training and multidisciplinary components that define the program. Serial stress testing and passive joint focused therapy are not rehabilitation program elements.
  20. The correct answer is E (They carry weaker class 2b recommendations, mainly to reduce hospitalizations in selected patients): TOPCAT for spironolactone and PARAGON HF for sacubitril valsartan each missed their primary endpoints but suggested benefit in subgroups, particularly those toward the lower end of the preserved ejection fraction range, so both drugs earned 2b grades for decreasing hospitalizations. Neither reached class 1 strength nor showed harm meriting class 3. If anything, benefit appears greater at lower rather than higher ejection fractions, the opposite of a 65 percent restriction.
  21. The correct answer is D (Acetylcholine, which provokes focal or diffuse spasm in susceptible epicardial segments): intracoronary acetylcholine is the standard provocative agent, producing spasm in susceptible segments through muscarinic stimulation of dysfunctional endothelium and smooth muscle. Adenosine and regadenoson are hyperemic agents used for flow reserve and perfusion imaging rather than spasm provocation. Dobutamine raises demand rather than testing vasomotor tone, and nitroglycerin relieves spasm rather than provoking it.
  22. The correct answer is C (Loeys Dietz syndrome, in which dissection can occur at relatively small diameters): Loeys Dietz syndrome, caused by transforming growth factor beta pathway variants, produces the most aggressive aortopathy, with documented dissections at small diameters, so repair is considered around 4.0 to 4.5 centimeters depending on the specific gene and family history. Uncomplicated Marfan syndrome is repaired at 5.0 centimeters, while bicuspid aortopathy without risk factors uses 5.5 centimeters. Turner syndrome uses body size indexed measurements, and gene negative familial disease follows standard rather than accelerated thresholds.
  23. The correct answer is B (A prosthetic cardiac valve, including one implanted by a transcatheter approach): prophylaxis is reserved for the highest risk conditions, namely prosthetic valves or prosthetic material used in valve repair, prior infective endocarditis, specific forms of congenital heart disease, and transplant recipients with valvulopathy. Bicuspid valves, mitral valve prolapse, and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy no longer qualify under current recommendations even when a murmur is present. An isolated secundum atrial septal defect is likewise excluded, since unrepaired defects of this type carry very low endocarditis risk.
  24. The correct answer is C (A 24 hour mean of 125/75 mm Hg or higher on the report): The guideline treats an office value of 130/80 as corresponding to a 24 hour ambulatory mean of 125/75, a daytime mean of 130/80, and a nighttime mean of 110/65. A full day cutoff of 130/80 is too high because lower nocturnal pressures are averaged into the 24 hour figure. Diagnosis rests on averaged values, so a single daytime reading proves nothing on its own.
  25. The correct answer is A (Coagulase negative staphylococci, particularly Staphylococcus epidermidis introduced at the time of surgery): early prosthetic valve endocarditis is dominated by organisms acquired perioperatively, and coagulase negative staphylococci adhere to the sewing ring, form biofilm, and characteristically produce perivalvular abscess and dehiscence rather than large leaflet vegetations. Staphylococcus aureus is also an important early pathogen but the coagulase negative species are the classic answer for this window. Viridans streptococci and enterococci become more prominent in late prosthetic infection, which resembles native valve disease. Pneumococcal seeding and catheter related Candida infection are far less common causes in this setting.
  26. The correct answer is B (Spironolactone, which outperformed alpha and beta blockade as fourth line therapy): The PATHWAY 2 trial randomized patients with confirmed resistant hypertension to a fourth agent and found spironolactone lowered blood pressure substantially more than doxazosin, bisoprolol, or placebo, implicating occult aldosterone driven sodium retention. His normal potassium and preserved filtration make the drug safe with monitoring. Alpha blockers, beta blockers, hydralazine, and clonidine remain later options after mineralocorticoid antagonism.
  27. The correct answer is E (A ratio of ST elevation to T wave amplitude of 0.25 or greater): in V6, dividing the height of the ST segment by the height of the T wave yields a ratio that favors pericarditis when it reaches 0.25, because early repolarization produces proportionally taller T waves. A J point notch with tall T waves is the typical early repolarization appearance. Normalization with exercise also suggests early repolarization, and an isoelectric ST segment or a tall T wave with normal ST does not indicate pericarditis.
  28. The correct answer is B (Popliteal artery entrapment caused by abnormal gastrocnemius anatomy compressing the vessel): loss of pedal pulses with provocative plantarflexion is the hallmark of popliteal artery entrapment, in which an anomalous relationship between the artery and the gastrocnemius compresses the vessel during muscle contraction. Compartment syndrome causes pressure related pain but leaves pulses intact during maneuvers. Atherosclerosis is implausible without risk factors at 24, cystic adventitial disease produces fixed narrowing rather than maneuver dependent pulse loss, and venous claudication causes bursting fullness with preserved arterial pulses.
  29. The correct answer is C (White coat hypertension, elevated office values with normal out of office averages): An untreated patient whose office readings meet hypertension thresholds while home or ambulatory averages remain normal has white coat hypertension, a pattern often driven by situational anxiety. Masked hypertension is the reverse pattern, with normal office values hiding elevated daily pressures. Resistance refers to failure of an adequate multidrug regimen, and lability implies unpredictable swings rather than a consistent setting dependent difference.
  30. The correct answer is C (Inhibiting HMG CoA reductase, which increases hepatic LDL receptor expression and clearance): statins block the rate limiting enzyme of hepatic cholesterol synthesis, and the resulting upregulation of LDL receptors pulls LDL particles out of the circulation. Bile acid binding describes resins such as cholestyramine, and blockade of the intestinal sterol transporter describes ezetimibe. PCSK9 directed therapy uses antibodies or small interfering RNA, while lipoprotein lipase activation relates to fibrate effects on triglycerides.
  31. The correct answer is D (Tafamidis, a transthyretin stabilizer that reduced mortality and cardiovascular hospitalizations in trials): tafamidis binds and stabilizes the transthyretin tetramer, preventing its dissociation into amyloidogenic monomers, and the ATTR ACT trial showed lower all cause mortality and fewer cardiovascular hospitalizations, earning it a guideline recommendation in NYHA class I to III disease. Melphalan and transplantation treat light chain amyloidosis, a different disease. Colchicine, corticosteroids, and sacubitril valsartan neither stabilize transthyretin nor remove existing deposits.
  32. The correct answer is D (Adding metolazone to block distal tubule sodium reabsorption that limits loop diuretic response): chronic loop diuretic exposure causes hypertrophy of distal tubular cells that reclaim the sodium escaping the loop, and adding a thiazide type agent such as metolazone blocks that downstream segment. This combination requires close potassium and sodium monitoring because the diuresis can be profound. Rotating loop agents does not address the distal adaptation, mannitol is not a decongestion strategy in heart failure, and stopping diuretics in a congested patient invites worsening decompensation.
  33. The correct answer is E (Inhibition of the late inward sodium current, reducing calcium overload in ischemic myocytes): ranolazine blocks the late sodium current, which lowers intracellular calcium accumulation and diastolic wall tension without meaningfully changing heart rate or blood pressure, making it attractive when both are already low. Beta receptor blockade and venodilation describe the beta blocker and nitrate this patient already takes. Calcium channel blockade and potassium channel activation describe other vasodilator classes.
  34. The correct answer is A (Stage 2 hypertension, because systolic ≥140 mm Hg or diastolic ≥90 mm Hg): Both his systolic of 146 and his diastolic of 94 sit at or above the stage 2 cutoffs, and two properly performed visits already satisfy the confirmation requirement. Stage 1 covers systolic 130 to 139 or diastolic 80 to 89, which his readings exceed. Urgency describes severe elevation above 180/120, and masked hypertension means normal office values with high out of office values, the opposite pattern.
  35. The correct answer is A (A left ventricular ejection fraction of 50 percent or higher with other heart failure evidence): HFpEF requires symptoms, an ejection fraction of at least 50 percent, and objective evidence such as elevated natriuretic peptides or raised filling pressures. Values between 41 and 49 percent belong to the mildly reduced category, not the preserved one. Neither a 60 percent floor, a stress study, nor confirmation on two studies months apart appears anywhere in the definition.
  36. The correct answer is D (Mitral valve prolapse with regurgitation in a patient with no prior endocarditis): prolapse, even with a murmur or regurgitation, was removed from prophylaxis recommendations because the lifetime risk of endocarditis is low and procedure related bacteremia is a minor contributor compared with daily activities. Prior endocarditis, prosthetic valves including transcatheter devices, unrepaired cyanotic congenital heart disease, and transplant recipients with valvulopathy all remain high risk conditions for which prophylaxis before qualifying dental procedures is still advised. The distinction rests on the risk of a severe or fatal outcome from endocarditis, which is concentrated in those four groups.
  37. The correct answer is A (An adult aged 18 to 60 with an embolic appearing cryptogenic ischemic stroke): Randomized trials supporting closure enrolled patients aged 18 to 60 whose strokes remained cryptogenic after a comprehensive workup. Atrial fibrillation, small vessel lacunes, and large artery carotid disease each provide an alternative mechanism that closure would not address. Migraine without cerebral ischemia is not an accepted indication for the device.
  38. The correct answer is D (The Gallavardin phenomenon, in which high frequency components radiate selectively to the apex): in calcific aortic stenosis the musical high frequency elements of the murmur can transmit to the apex while the harsh noisy elements stay at the base, mimicking mitral regurgitation. The apical murmur remains midsystolic and ejection shaped, which helps separate it from true holosystolic regurgitation. The Austin Flint murmur is a diastolic apical rumble of aortic regurgitation, and the Graham Steell murmur is diastolic pulmonic regurgitation. Inspiratory augmentation of right sided murmurs and the vibratory innocent murmur of childhood are unrelated to this transmission pattern.
  39. The correct answer is E (A benign fluid filled structure most often located at the right cardiophrenic angle): pericardial cysts are congenital, thin walled, fluid filled lesions, roughly 70 percent of which sit at the right cardiophrenic angle, and asymptomatic cysts are simply followed. They are not malignant mesothelial tumors and are not solid fibrous masses, so resection is reserved for symptoms or diagnostic uncertainty. A purulent collection would show clinical infection with complex fluid, and a traumatic pleuropericardial communication is a different, acquired entity.
  40. The correct answer is C (New ST elevation of ≥1 mm at the J point in two contiguous leads): the universal definition requires new ST elevation in two contiguous leads, with higher thresholds applying only in leads V2 and V3. Isolated lateral ST depression and deep T wave inversion indicate ischemia but do not meet elevation criteria for infarction. Diffuse concave elevation with PR depression suggests pericarditis, and an isolated premature ventricular complex has no diagnostic weight for infarction.
  41. The correct answer is E (Atrial flutter with organized sawtooth atrial waves conducting to the ventricles): The strip shows continuous, organized, repeating atrial deflections without an isoelectric baseline, the sawtooth appearance that defines flutter, with QRS complexes conducted at a fixed ratio. Coarse atrial fibrillation produces chaotic undulations that vary in shape and timing rather than a uniform repeating wave. Nonconducted premature atrial complexes and Wenckebach conduction both preserve discrete sinus P waves with pauses, and multifocal atrial tachycardia requires multiple distinct P wave morphologies rather than one repeating waveform.
  42. The correct answer is B (Apply the bell lightly at the apex with the patient in left lateral decubitus): the mitral stenosis rumble is a low frequency sound best captured by the bell applied with light pressure, and turning the patient onto the left side brings the apex against the chest wall, while brief exercise can further accentuate the murmur by increasing transmitral flow. Firm diaphragm pressure filters out low frequencies and would suppress the rumble. The right upper sternal border and carotids are positions for aortic stenosis, and the leaning forward diaphragm technique at the sternal border targets the high pitched murmur of aortic regurgitation.
  43. The correct answer is A (Blockade of the sodium potassium chloride cotransporter in the thick ascending limb): loop diuretics inhibit the NKCC2 transporter, preventing reabsorption of a large fraction of filtered sodium and producing the most powerful diuresis of any class. Sodium chloride cotransporter inhibition in the distal tubule describes thiazides, and aldosterone receptor antagonism in the collecting duct describes mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists. Osmotic agents and carbonic anhydrase inhibitors act in the proximal nephron and are not loop mechanisms.
  44. The correct answer is E (Wait at least 36 hours after the last enalapril dose before starting sacubitril valsartan): both ACE inhibitors and neprilysin inhibitors raise bradykinin levels, and overlapping them markedly increases angioedema risk, so a 36 hour washout is mandatory. Starting at the next scheduled dose would create exactly that dangerous overlap. No ARB bridge is required because ARBs do not carry the same bradykinin mediated interaction, and tryptase testing plays no role in predicting angioedema here.
  45. The correct answer is C (Right ventricular structural and functional alteration caused by lung disease and its pulmonary hypertension): cor pulmonale describes right ventricular hypertrophy, dilation, or failure arising from disorders of the lungs, their vasculature, or ventilation, with hypoxic vasoconstriction and vascular remodeling raising the afterload the right ventricle must face. Left ventricular failure coexisting with lung disease is a separate entity and is specifically excluded from the definition. Valvular endocarditis, viral biventricular dilation, and pericardial constriction each produce venous congestion through mechanisms unrelated to pulmonary disease.

The correct answer is E (A target of 2.5, typically maintained within a range of 2.0 to 3.0): a current generation bileaflet or single tilting disc aortic prosthesis without atrial fibrillation, prior embolism, ventricular dysfunction, or hypercoagulability is managed with a warfarin target of 2.5. The higher target of 3.0 is reserved for mechanical mitral valves, older generation prostheses, or aortic valves with added risk factors. Mechanical prostheses require lifelong warfarin, so stopping after endothelialization is not an option, and values below 2.0 leave the valve unprotected. Combination regimens with clopidogrel or scheduled heparin are not part of routine maintenance therapy.
  47. The correct answer is D (Mobitz type II block with abruptly nonconducted P waves and fixed PR intervals): The strip shows on time sinus P waves that intermittently fail to conduct while the PR interval of every conducted beat remains constant, the defining feature of Mobitz type II and a marker of infranodal disease with risk of progression to complete block. Wenckebach conduction would show PR intervals stretching progressively before the dropped beat. Complete block would show no relationship at all between P waves and QRS complexes, first degree block drops nothing, and blocked premature atrial complexes arrive early rather than on time.
  48. The correct answer is C (A normal perfusion pattern effectively excludes chronic thromboembolic disease, which computed tomography can miss): ventilation perfusion scintigraphy is the recommended screening test because its sensitivity for chronic thromboembolic pulmonary hypertension approaches 96 to 97 percent, while organized, webbed, and distal disease can escape detection on computed tomography angiography read by nonexpert eyes. The scan does not image arteries at higher resolution, and it provides no ventricular or resistance measurement. It also involves radiation, so the safety claim misstates its advantage.
  49. The correct answer is D (Fastidious gram negative oropharyngeal bacteria that grow slowly in standard blood cultures): the HACEK group comprises Haemophilus, Aggregatibacter, Cardiobacterium, Eikenella, and Kingella species, oropharyngeal residents that historically explained a portion of apparently culture negative endocarditis because of slow growth. Modern automated culture systems usually recover them within about five days. Skin origin gram positive cocci describe coagulase negative staphylococci of early prosthetic valve infection, and truly serology dependent organisms are pathogens such as Coxiella and Bartonella. Fungal endocarditis with bulky vegetations occurs in parenteral nutrition and catheter settings but is unrelated to this acronym.
  50. The correct answer is E (Category IIa, marginally threatened, salvageable with prompt rather than immediate revascularization): minimal sensory loss confined to the toes without motor deficit, inaudible arterial signals, and audible venous signals define Rutherford category IIa, a marginally threatened limb that is salvageable if treated promptly. Category I requires intact sensation and audible arterial signals, so preserved motor function alone does not make a limb viable. Category IIb requires sensory loss beyond the toes or any motor weakness, and category III shows profound paralysis, absent venous signals, and fixed skin changes.
  51. The correct answer is B (Rupture of the interventricular septum creating a left to right shunt): a harsh holosystolic murmur with a thrill days after infarction, together with an oxygen saturation step up from right atrium to right ventricle, is diagnostic of septal rupture shunting oxygenated blood into the right ventricle. Papillary muscle rupture produces regurgitation into the left atrium without a right sided step up, and its murmur is often soft. Free wall rupture causes tamponade, and neither pericarditis nor right ventricular infarction produces a saturation step up.
  52. The correct answer is A (Events cluster with exertion, classically swimming, and beta blockers are first line therapy): KCNQ1 mutations cause long QT type 1, in which the slow delayed rectifier potassium current fails to augment during sympathetic stimulation, so arrhythmic events occur with physical activity and swimming is a classic trigger, and beta blockade is the cornerstone of treatment. Auditory startle points to type 2, and events during rest or sleep point to type 3, where late sodium current blockade has a role. Fever related events with quinidine therapy describe Brugada syndrome rather than any long QT subtype.
  53. The correct answer is D (Thiazolidinediones cause fluid retention and can precipitate or worsen heart failure episodes): these agents promote renal sodium reabsorption and plasma volume expansion, producing edema and heart failure exacerbations, which is why the guideline lists them as potentially harmful in symptomatic heart failure and why an SGLT2 inhibitor is the far better diabetes choice here. The mechanism is volume related rather than direct myocyte death. They do not block SGLT2 receptors, meaningfully prolong the QT interval, or drive lipid changes severe enough to explain the warning.
  54. The correct answer is E (Paroxysmal atrial tachycardia with block, often accompanied by accelerated junctional rhythms): Digoxin increases automaticity through calcium loading and delayed afterdepolarizations while simultaneously enhancing vagal tone at the AV node, so atrial tachycardia with atrioventricular block is the classic toxic rhythm, and accelerated junctional rhythms and bidirectional ventricular tachycardia are also characteristic. Torsades de pointes reflects QT prolonging drugs rather than digoxin, which actually shortens the QT interval. One to one flutter, preexcited atrial fibrillation, and sinus tachycardia with aberrancy have no specific association with cardiac glycoside excess.
  55. The correct answer is B (The morphologic right ventricle, which continues ejecting into the aorta lifelong): Atrial switch procedures redirect venous inflow at the atrial level while leaving the discordant ventriculoarterial connections untouched, so the right ventricle keeps supporting the systemic circulation. Progressive failure of this chamber with tricuspid regurgitation is the dominant late problem. The later arterial switch operation is what restores the left ventricle as the systemic pump.

The correct answer is D (Paradoxical low flow, low gradient severe stenosis despite a preserved ejection fraction): a small, stiff, hypertrophied ventricle can generate a normal ejection fraction yet a low stroke volume index, typically under 35 mL/m², so the gradient stays below 40 mmHg even though the orifice is severely narrowed. The classical low flow form requires a reduced ejection fraction, which this patient does not have. Pseudosevere disease refers to a moderately diseased valve whose calculated area underestimates its true opening at low flow. Dynamic subvalvular obstruction produces a labile gradient with systolic anterior motion rather than a fixed calcified valvular lesion.

Diet has little effect on the level, statins do not lower it, and a clearly elevated value alongside normal conventional lipids does not suggest an artifact. Its prognostic significance does not depend on the triglyceride concentration.
  58. The correct answer is B (5.0 centimeters, reflecting rupture at smaller aortic diameters among women): women have smaller native aortas and rupture at smaller absolute diameters, so elective repair is recommended at 5.0 centimeters rather than the 5.5 centimeter threshold used for men. A 4.0 or 4.5 centimeter trigger would operate on aneurysms whose rupture risk remains lower than procedural risk, and pulmonary comorbidity does not lower the diameter threshold. Deferring repair to 6.0 centimeters would leave a woman exposed to a high annual rupture hazard.
  59. The correct answer is A (She meets criteria because ferritin between 100 and 299 with transferrin saturation below 20 percent qualifies): iron deficiency in heart failure is defined as a ferritin below 100 ng/mL, or a ferritin of 100 to 299 ng/mL when transferrin saturation is under 20 percent, and her values of 150 and 14 percent satisfy the second arm. A normal laboratory ferritin range does not exclude the diagnosis because ferritin behaves as an acute phase reactant in heart failure. Anemia is not required for the definition, and bone marrow examination is unnecessary.
  60. The correct answer is E (Take the final dose at least four hours before lying down for the night): Midodrine is an alpha agonist prodrug whose pressor effect lasts several hours, so a dose taken too close to bedtime produces supine hypertension while the patient sleeps. Standard counseling schedules doses during upright daytime hours, with the last dose no later than four hours before lying down. Bedtime dosing, symptom triggered redosing, and unsupervised dose doubling each raise the supine pressure hazard.
  61. The correct answer is B (A left ventricular ejection fraction of 40 percent or lower with heart failure symptoms): the guideline defines HFrEF as symptomatic heart failure with an ejection fraction of 40 percent or less. Values between 41 and 49 percent define the mildly reduced category, while 50 percent or higher with evidence of congestion defines preserved ejection fraction. Thresholds of 45 or 55 percent do not correspond to any guideline category.
  62. The correct answer is B (Nonvasodilating beta blockers, titrated to blunt heart rate and reduce the dynamic gradient): slowing the heart rate lengthens diastolic filling and reduces contractility, both of which enlarge the cavity and lessen obstruction, making beta blockers first line, with verapamil or diltiazem as alternatives when beta blockade fails or is not tolerated. Vasodilators such as dihydropyridines and nitrates drop afterload and worsen the gradient. Aggressive diuresis shrinks preload and can provoke severe obstruction, and ACE inhibitors do not regress sarcomeric hypertrophy.
  63. The correct answer is C (They are associated with a higher rate of recurrent pericarditis after tapering): observational and trial data show corticosteroid exposure, particularly at high doses with rapid withdrawal, independently predicts recurrence, likely by impairing viral clearance and fostering dependence. Masking of fever and blunting of markers are practical concerns but not the principal guideline rationale. A clinically meaningful pharmacokinetic interaction with colchicine is not the issue, and corticosteroids have not been shown to drive constrictive transformation in viral disease.
  64. The correct answer is B (Endarterectomy is generally favored over transfemoral stenting in patients older than about 70): randomized trial analyses show periprocedural stroke risk with transfemoral stenting rises steeply after about age 70, while endarterectomy outcomes remain relatively stable, so surgery is generally favored in older patients. The claim that stenting is safer beyond 75 inverts the trial data, and outcomes are not age neutral for the two strategies. Arch tortuosity and calcification actually make catheter delivery harder in older patients, and endarterectomy is not restricted to the young.
  65. The correct answer is C (Give supplemental unfractionated heparin during the procedure to prevent catheter thrombosis): fondaparinux alone provides inadequate procedural anticoagulation and has been associated with catheter thrombosis, so an agent with antithrombin activity such as unfractionated heparin is added during intervention. Protamine does not reverse fondaparinux, and doubling its dose does not solve the catheter thrombosis problem. Relying on the subcutaneous dose alone or substituting a fibrinolytic bolus would leave the procedure unprotected.
  66. The correct answer is D (Every 1 to 2 years, sooner if symptoms or examination findings change): moderate aortic stenosis, generally a velocity of 3.0 to 3.9 m/s, is reimaged every 1 to 2 years because progression to severe disease is common in calcified valves. The 3 to 5 year schedule applies to mild stenosis with velocities of 2.0 to 2.9 m/s. Six month imaging with routine biomarkers exceeds what guidelines require at this stage, while a 10 year gap or purely symptom driven imaging would miss silent progression.
  67. The correct answer is D (Its high negative predictive value reliably excludes obstructive coronary artery disease): a normal coronary computed tomography angiogram makes obstructive disease very unlikely, which is why the test is favored for intermediate risk patients without known disease. The study is anatomic rather than functional, so it neither provokes ischemia nor measures oxygen consumption, and invasive fractional flow reserve requires catheterization. The examination does use ionizing radiation and iodinated contrast.
  68. The correct answer is E (A mean pulmonary arterial pressure above 20 mmHg measured at rest): the 2022 European Society of Cardiology and European Respiratory Society guidelines lowered the defining threshold from the historical 25 mmHg to above 20 mmHg, reflecting evidence that mortality risk rises above the normal mean of about 14 mmHg. The older 25 mmHg cutoff no longer defines the disease. Systolic estimates do not define the condition, exercise values are not part of the resting definition, and wedge pressure serves to classify rather than establish pulmonary hypertension.
  69. The correct answer is D (An ostium secundum defect centered within the fossa ovalis with sufficient surrounding rim): Device closure requires a defect surrounded by septal tissue that can anchor the occluder, which describes secundum anatomy. Primum defects need surgery because valve repair accompanies patch closure. Sinus venosus and coronary sinus defects lack adequate rims and often involve anomalous veins, so they are repaired surgically.
  70. The correct answer is B (Giant cell myocarditis, which causes rapid deterioration with ventricular arrhythmias and heart block): this rare subtype strikes younger adults with rapidly progressive heart failure, sustained ventricular tachycardia, and conduction block, and survival depends on early biopsy diagnosis followed by combined immunosuppression, with many patients needing mechanical support or transplant. Ordinary lymphocytic myocarditis is usually self limited and does not routinely warrant immunosuppression. Loeffler disease is an eosinophilic endomyocardial process, Chagas disease requires exposure in endemic regions, and radiation injury develops slowly over years.
  71. The correct answer is A (Baseline conduction abnormality makes exercise induced ST changes uninterpretable for ischemia): left bundle branch block distorts repolarization so that exercise ST changes cannot be read, which removes the diagnostic value of treadmill electrocardiography alone. Imaging still requires an adequate stress level, so no lower heart rate standard applies. Bundle branch block does not commonly progress to complete block with exercise, perfusion imaging does not quantify calcium, and supervision requirements are unchanged by echocardiography.
  72. The correct answer is E (Bumetanide 1 mg, which provides diuresis roughly equivalent to furosemide 40 mg): the standard oral equivalence is furosemide 40 mg, bumetanide 1 mg, and torsemide 20 mg, making bumetanide about forty times more potent per milligram. A 10 mg dose would be a massive overdose relative to her current regimen, and 0.25 mg would underdose her fourfold. The 5 mg and 2 mg conversions do not correspond to any accepted equivalence table.
  73. The correct answer is E (Type 1 is iodine driven overproduction in abnormal glands, while type 2 is destructive thyroiditis): the iodine load in amiodarone drives true hormone synthesis in nodular goiter or latent Graves disease in type 1, while type 2 is a drug induced destructive process releasing preformed hormone from a previously normal gland. This mechanistic split explains why thionamides treat type 1 and glucocorticoids treat type 2. Pituitary overproduction, receptor blockade, tissue resistance, and binding protein excess do not describe either form.
  74. The correct answer is B (Fibrofatty replacement of right ventricular myocardium with epsilon waves on the ECG): ARVC is a desmosomal gene disease in which right ventricular muscle is progressively replaced by fibrous and fatty tissue, producing ventricular arrhythmias of left bundle branch morphology, T wave inversions in the right precordial leads, and the characteristic epsilon wave. Concentric thickening and dagger shaped gradients describe hypertrophic cardiomyopathy. Noncompaction is a separate trabeculation disorder, and pericardial thickening with alternans points toward pericardial disease with effusion.
  75. The correct answer is B (Catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia, most often from ryanodine receptor mutations): Exertional or emotional syncope in a child with a normal resting ECG and normal cardiac imaging, plus exercise induced ectopy that evolves into bidirectional ventricular tachycardia, is the classic presentation of CPVT, usually caused by RYR2 mutations that produce calcium leak from the sarcoplasmic reticulum. Long QT type 1 also causes exertional events but shows a prolonged QT at rest. Arrhythmogenic cardiomyopathy and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy produce structural or ECG abnormalities, and Brugada events typically occur at rest or with fever.
  76. The correct answer is C (A value of 0.90 or below, indicating hemodynamically significant lower extremity arterial obstruction): a resting ankle brachial index of 0.90 or below is diagnostic of peripheral artery disease. Values between 0.91 and 0.99 are borderline rather than diagnostic, and results from 1.00 to 1.40 fall in the normal range. A result above 1.40 reflects noncompressible calcified vessels and is uninterpretable rather than confirmatory, while exercise testing is an adjunct for symptomatic patients with normal resting values, not the primary diagnostic criterion.
  77. The correct answer is B (Mitral and tricuspid closure at S1, aortic and pulmonic closure at S2): S1 marks closure of the atrioventricular valves as ventricular systole begins, and S2 marks closure of the semilunar valves as systole ends and isovolumic relaxation starts. Valve opening is normally silent, so options built on opening sounds are wrong. S1 is not produced by mitral closure alone with tricuspid closure making S2, aortic closure belongs to S2 rather than S1, and atrial contraction relates to S4 when audible.
  78. The correct answer is B (A single screening abdominal duplex ultrasound for men aged 65 to 75 who ever smoked): guidelines recommend one time abdominal ultrasound screening for men aged 65 to 75 with any smoking history, and this applies even though he quit years ago. Ultrasound is inexpensive, accurate, and radiation free, so repeated computed tomography and magnetic resonance angiography are not screening tools here. Physical examination detects aneurysms unreliably, and waiting for symptoms defeats the purpose of screening because rupture is often the first symptomatic event.
  79. The correct answer is E (Lisinopril, because blocking angiotensin lowers intraglomerular pressure and slows albuminuric progression): In chronic kidney disease with significant albuminuria, an angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitor or angiotensin receptor blocker is the recommended foundation because efferent arteriolar relaxation reduces intraglomerular pressure and proteinuria. Calcium channel blockers and thiazides lower systemic pressure but lack the same antiproteinuric kidney outcome data. Beta blockers and hydralazine have no comparable role in slowing albuminuric nephropathy.
  80. The correct answer is B (Hypokalemia, hyponatremia, hyperuricemia, hypercalcemia, and a modest rise in glucose): Thiazides increase distal sodium delivery and aldosterone driven potassium loss, impair free water excretion causing hyponatremia, compete with urate secretion raising uric acid, enhance calcium reabsorption, and modestly worsen glucose tolerance. Potassium and magnesium fall rather than rise on these agents. Options describing hyperkalemia, hypoglycemia, or a falling urate reverse the true direction of the class effects.
  81. The cleft produces regurgitation that often dictates surgical timing and technique. Commissural fusion suggests rheumatic disease, and pulmonic stenosis is not a defining feature of this lesion.
  82. The correct answer is E (It mostly affects younger women, and stable cases are often managed conservatively): spontaneous coronary artery dissection predominantly affects younger women, including peripartum patients and those with fibromuscular dysplasia, and stable cases frequently heal with conservative management because stenting risks extending the dissection. The mechanism is an intramural hematoma or intimal tear rather than atherosclerotic plaque rupture. Fibrinolysis is discouraged because it can enlarge the hematoma, and routine multivessel stenting is not the preferred approach.
  83. The correct answer is B (The regurgitant aortic jet impinges on the anterior mitral leaflet, restricting its opening): the classic explanation is that the diastolic regurgitant stream strikes the anterior mitral leaflet, pushing it toward closure and creating functional inflow turbulence that sounds like mitral stenosis. The distinction matters because a normal valve needs no intervention, whereas true rheumatic narrowing with commissural fusion would. An opening snap and echocardiographic leaflet fusion identify genuine mitral stenosis, both absent here. Tricuspid flow murmurs localize to the lower sternal border and vary with breathing, and a myxoma produces a positional tumor plop rather than this pattern.
  84. The correct answer is B (Ventricular remodeling tethers the leaflets and dilates the annulus, preventing effective coaptation): in secondary mitral regurgitation the leaflets and chordae are structurally normal, but displacement of the papillary muscles and annular dilation from ventricular remodeling hold the leaflets apart. Treatment therefore centers on guideline directed therapy for the failing ventricle, with valve intervention considered in selected persistent cases. Myxomatous billowing, chordal rupture, rheumatic retraction, and endocarditis perforation all represent intrinsic valve disease and define primary regurgitation instead.
  85. The correct answer is A (Rheumatic valve scarring after group A streptococcal pharyngitis triggers immune mediated injury): rheumatic fever remains the dominant worldwide cause of mitral stenosis, producing the characteristic commissural fusion, chordal shortening, and fish mouth orifice. Degenerative annular calcification causes stenosis in older adults in high income countries but far less often and usually with milder obstruction. Carcinoid disease preferentially injures right sided valves unless a shunt or lung involvement is present. Congenital parachute mitral valve and radiation induced fibrosis are recognized but uncommon causes.

Stage 2 begins at systolic ≥140 or diastolic ≥90, and urgency describes severe elevation above 180/120, neither of which applies here.

Right sided leads evaluate right ventricular infarction, which accompanies inferior rather than isolated posterior patterns. Signal averaged recordings, ambulatory monitoring, and a post nitroglycerin tracing cannot establish the infarct territory.

Confirmed myocarditis and takotsubo syndrome are specific alternate diagnoses that remove a patient from this working category.
