The correct answer is C (Chamber paced, chamber sensed, and the device response to a sensed event): In the NBG code the first letter names the chamber paced, the second the chamber sensed, and the third the response to sensing, so DDD means both chambers are paced, both are sensed, and the device can either inhibit or trigger in response. A fourth position, when present, indicates rate modulation rather than any manufacturer designation. Upper tracking rate, lead position, battery chemistry, and shock capability are device features or programming details that the three letter mode code does not encode.
  91. The correct answer is A (Calcium channel blockers, which relax coronary smooth muscle and prevent recurrent spasm): calcium channel blockers are first line because they directly relax coronary vascular smooth muscle and reliably reduce spasm frequency. Beta blockade does not prevent spasm and nonselective agents can worsen it, while diuretics address preload rather than vasomotor tone. Antiplatelet agents and statins are reasonable adjuncts for coexisting atherosclerosis but do not dependably suppress spastic episodes.
  92. The correct answer is D (Sex specific 99th percentile limits derived from a healthy reference population): high sensitivity assays should report sex specific 99th percentile upper reference limits because healthy men run higher concentrations than healthy women. A single shared cutoff can miss injury in women and overcall it in men. Thresholds anchored to diseased inpatients, the limit of blank, or a legacy assay do not define the reference limit correctly.
  93. The correct answer is A (Noncompressible calcified arteries, which should prompt measurement of a toe brachial index): an ankle brachial index above 1.40 means medial calcification, common in diabetes and kidney disease, prevents cuff compression of the tibial arteries, so the number cannot be trusted. The digital arteries are usually spared from calcification, so a toe brachial index gives a reliable perfusion measurement in this setting. The result does not indicate normal compliance or borderline disease, and invasive angiography is not the appropriate next step for an uninterpretable screening value.
  94. The correct answer is D (A transient ischemic attack that occurred 2 years before this admission): any history of stroke or transient ischemic attack contraindicates prasugrel because of excess intracranial bleeding observed in trials. Age of 75 years or more and weight under 60 kilograms call for caution or a reduced dose rather than prohibition. Moderate kidney disease and proton pump inhibitor use matter more for dosing and for clopidogrel interactions than for prasugrel eligibility.
  95. The correct answer is A (They avoid lifelong warfarin but undergo structural deterioration, particularly in younger recipients): tissue valves do not require indefinite anticoagulation in patients without another indication, but leaflet calcification and degeneration accelerate in younger patients, who may face reoperation or valve in valve procedures. Mechanical valves are the durable option yet commit the patient to lifelong warfarin, which is why age and bleeding risk drive the shared decision. Bioprostheses carry a broadly similar endocarditis risk to mechanical valves, and both prosthesis types qualify for endocarditis prophylaxis. Metallic closure sounds are a feature of mechanical, not tissue, valves.
  96. The correct answer is A (Routine phlebotomy is avoided because it causes iron deficiency and worsens outcomes): Erythrocytosis in cyanotic disease is a compensatory response to hypoxemia, and repeated venesection produces iron deficient microcytes that raise viscosity and stroke risk. Phlebotomy is reserved for true hyperviscosity symptoms after dehydration is excluded, with volume replacement given. Fixed hematocrit targets and routine hemodilution lack supporting evidence in this population.
  97. The correct answer is E (Sterile platelet and fibrin vegetations, usually mitral, that can embolize or scar the valve): Libman-Sacks lesions are noninfective deposits favoring the mitral leaflets, associated with lupus and antiphospholipid antibodies, and their importance lies in embolic stroke risk and progressive valvular regurgitation from healing and fibrosis. Infected enlarging masses describe infective endocarditis, which requires antibiotics these sterile lesions do not need. Calcified aortic nodules, septal tumors such as myxomas, and transient fibrin strands describe unrelated entities.
  98. The correct answer is A (Resistant hypertension and higher recurrence of atrial fibrillation after cardioversion or ablation): repetitive apneas cause sympathetic surges, intermittent hypoxia, and negative intrathoracic pressure swings that sustain hypertension despite multiple drugs and promote atrial remodeling that undermines rhythm control procedures. Sleep apnea raises rather than lowers sympathetic tone, so daytime hypotension and arrhythmia protection misstate the physiology. Neither aneurysm growth acceleration nor bicuspid valve degeneration is an established consequence of the disorder.
  99. The correct answer is E (A true left ventricular aneurysm formed by thinned scarred myocardium after infarction): persistent ST elevation months after infarction with a thin dyskinetic segment and a wide neck is the classic picture of a true aneurysm, which contains all layers of scarred wall. A pseudoaneurysm is a contained rupture with a narrow neck, the opposite geometry. Recurrent infarction causes new symptoms and dynamic changes, while constrictive pericarditis and chronic embolism produce right sided rather than these left ventricular findings.
  100. The correct answer is D (Venodilation that lowers preload and reduces ventricular wall tension and oxygen demand): nitrates donate nitric oxide, dilating capacitance veins so that preload, wall tension, and myocardial oxygen consumption all fall, with epicardial coronary dilation contributing secondarily. Sinus node slowing describes beta blockers, and late sodium current suppression describes ranolazine. Nitrates neither constrict arterioles nor enhance contractility, and reflex tachycardia can actually accompany their use.
  101. The correct answer is A (Maximum recorded flow speed reaching 4 m/s or higher across the valve): the guideline defines severe high gradient aortic stenosis by a jet of at least 4 m/s, a mean gradient of at least 40 mmHg, and a valve area of 1.0 cm² or less. Velocities near 2.5 to 3 m/s indicate mild to moderate obstruction, not severe disease. A jet of roughly 5 m/s or more marks very severe stenosis, a prognostic subgroup rather than the basic severity threshold itself.
  102. The correct answer is C (Vascular disease such as prior myocardial infarction or peripheral artery disease): The V in CHA2DS2-VASc stands for vascular disease, which includes prior myocardial infarction, peripheral artery disease, and aortic plaque, each worth one point. The remaining components are congestive heart failure, hypertension, age, diabetes, prior stroke or transient ischemic attack, and sex category. Renal dysfunction, atrial size, family history of stroke, and sleep apnea may influence overall risk but are not scored elements of this particular tool.
  103. The correct answer is E (A mean value of 40 mmHg or higher across the stenotic aortic valve): severe high gradient aortic stenosis requires a mean gradient of at least 40 mmHg, corresponding to a peak jet of at least 4 m/s and a valve area of 1.0 cm² or less. Gradients in the 20 to 39 mmHg range indicate moderate obstruction when flow is normal, or possibly severe disease masked by low flow, which prompts stroke volume assessment and sometimes dobutamine testing. Values near 15 mmHg reflect mild disease, and neither repetition on two studies nor body surface indexing changes the defining threshold.
  104. The correct answer is C (The artery giving rise to the posterior descending artery defines dominance): dominance is assigned by which vessel supplies the posterior descending artery and the posterolateral branches, with the right coronary doing so in roughly 85 percent of people, the circumflex in about 8 percent, and codominance in the remainder. Ostial caliber and branch counts are not the defining criteria. Total perfused mass actually favors the left system in most hearts despite right dominance, and sinoatrial nodal supply is a separate anatomic question.
  105. The correct answer is B (Aspirin use within the preceding 7 days before the current presentation): the seven components are age of 65 years or more, three or more coronary risk factors, known stenosis of ≥50 percent, ST deviation, two or more anginal episodes in 24 hours, recent aspirin use, and elevated cardiac markers. Creatinine and systolic blood pressure belong to the GRACE model rather than this score. Ejection fraction and body mass index appear in neither instrument.
  106. The correct answer is E (Intravenous esmolol before any vasodilator, targeting systolic 100 to 120 with heart rate near 60): anti impulse therapy starts with an intravenous beta blocker such as esmolol or labetalol, adding a vasodilator afterward if pressure remains high, with goals of systolic 100 to 120 millimeters of mercury and a heart rate near 60. Starting nitroprusside, nicardipine, or hydralazine without beta blockade provokes reflex tachycardia and increased ejection force, which raises shear stress on the injured wall. Oral outpatient style regimens and permissive targets near 130 or 140 do not achieve acute wall stress reduction.
  107. The correct answer is B (Carvedilol 25 mg twice daily, with 50 mg twice daily for patients over 85 kg): the trial proven target for immediate release carvedilol is 25 mg twice daily, and heavier patients such as this 92 kg man may be titrated to 50 mg twice daily. His heart rate and blood pressure leave clear room for uptitration from the current low dose. Neither 12.5 mg twice daily nor 3.125 mg twice daily is a maintenance target, and three times daily dosing is not how carvedilol is prescribed.
  108. The correct answer is A (Intravenous procainamide, which slows conduction over the accessory pathway itself): In preexcited atrial fibrillation, agents that act on the accessory pathway, such as procainamide or ibutilide, slow the dangerous anterograde conduction, and electrical cardioversion is used if instability develops. Verapamil, diltiazem, digoxin, and adenosine all act preferentially on the AV node, which can shift even more atrial impulses onto the accessory pathway and precipitate ventricular fibrillation. Selective nodal blockade is therefore the wrong target in this specific rhythm despite being routine in ordinary atrial fibrillation.
  109. The correct answer is A (Right atrial collapse during systole, appearing before right ventricular diastolic collapse): the right atrium has the lowest pressure and thinnest wall, so it buckles first when intrapericardial pressure exceeds its pressure during ventricular systole, and right ventricular diastolic collapse follows as pressures climb, being more specific for tamponade. Right ventricular collapse preceding atrial collapse reverses the true order. Left sided chamber collapse is uncommon and late, and four chamber collapse at arrest describes a terminal event rather than the diagnostic sequence.
  110. The correct answer is E (An ICD is recommended for secondary prevention after resuscitated ventricular fibrillation arrest): Survivors of cardiac arrest due to ventricular fibrillation or unstable ventricular tachycardia without a fully reversible cause have a class I secondary prevention indication, supported by trials showing device therapy outperforms antiarrhythmic drugs in this population. Amiodarone suppression on monitoring does not provide equivalent protection and waiting for a second event risks a fatal recurrence. The wearable vest is a temporary bridge rather than long term therapy, and electrophysiologic study guided drug treatment is not an accepted substitute for implantation.
  111. The correct answer is E (Precordial leads V1 through V4, reflecting the anterior wall and septum): the left anterior descending artery supplies the anterior left ventricular wall and the anterior two thirds of the septum through diagonal and septal branches, territory projected onto V1 through V4. Leads II, III, and aVF face the inferior wall, usually right coronary territory. Leads I and aVL with V5 and V6 face lateral territory typically supplied by the circumflex, and aVR alone does not map a single vessel.
  112. The correct answer is A (A shorter interval indicates more severe stenosis because left atrial pressure is higher): the mitral valve snaps open when ventricular pressure falls below atrial pressure during isovolumic relaxation, and the higher the atrial pressure, the earlier that crossover occurs after aortic closure. A short A2 to opening snap interval, roughly under 80 msec, therefore signals severe obstruction, while milder disease produces a longer interval. A lengthening interval with worsening disease reverses the actual physiology. Heart rate influences diastolic duration but does not replace the pressure relationship, and the interval is a reproducible, informative bedside finding rather than random variation.
  113. The correct answer is E (Immediate defibrillation with an unsynchronized high energy shock alongside ongoing CPR): Pulseless chaotic activity without organized QRS complexes is ventricular fibrillation, and survival depends chiefly on minimizing the time to defibrillation while high quality compressions continue. Synchronized cardioversion is impossible because there is no QRS on which to synchronize, and pacing has no role in a fibrillating ventricle. Epinephrine and amiodarone are adjuncts given during ongoing resuscitation, but neither should delay the shock that actually terminates the rhythm.
  114. The correct answer is C (Rheumatic narrowing of the mitral valve producing obstruction to left atrial emptying): a loud first heart sound, a high pitched sound shortly after S2 representing an opening snap, and an apical low frequency rumble form the classic triad, and the doming leaflet with commissural fusion and a reduced orifice confirms rheumatic stenosis. The resulting atrial pressure load enlarges the left atrium and precipitates atrial fibrillation, as seen here. Aortic narrowing produces a systolic basal murmur rather than an apical diastolic rumble, and prolapse produces a systolic click murmur complex. Aortic leaflet incompetence gives an early diastolic decrescendo murmur at the sternal border without commissural fusion.
  115. The correct answer is E (The left circumflex artery or one of its obtuse marginal branches): elevation in leads I, aVL, V5, and V6 localizes infarction to the lateral wall, which the circumflex system supplies. Proximal right coronary and posterior descending occlusions produce inferior changes with elevation in leads II, III, and aVF. Septal perforator and conus branch involvement affects the septum and right ventricular outflow tract rather than the lateral wall.
  116. The correct answer is C (Acute myocardial ischemia, which commonly drives polymorphic tachycardia without QT prolongation): Polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with a normal QT interval, particularly in a patient with active chest pain, is most often ischemic, and urgent evaluation for revascularization is the priority. Congenital long QT syndrome, drug induced repolarization delay, and hypomagnesemia all characteristically prolong the QT interval and produce torsades de pointes. Catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia is an inherited condition of younger patients triggered by exertion, an unlikely first consideration in an older man with chest pain.
  117. The correct answer is B (Ventricular preexcitation with a short PR interval and a delta wave): The strip shows sinus P waves followed almost immediately by QRS complexes that begin with a slurred upstroke, indicating that an accessory pathway is activating ventricular muscle before the normal conduction system. Bundle branch block also widens the QRS but preserves a normal PR interval and lacks the slurred initial deflection. Progressive PR prolongation and abruptly dropped beats describe the two forms of second degree block, and QT prolongation affects repolarization rather than the PR segment and QRS onset.
  118. The correct answer is E (Event risk is low, supporting deferral of statin therapy with periodic reassessment): a calcium score of zero in a borderline risk asymptomatic adult indicates low near term event risk and supports withholding statin therapy while repeating risk assessment in several years. A zero score does not warrant angiography, and it argues against rather than for starting high intensity therapy or adding aspirin. The score cannot exclude noncalcified plaque, which is why reassessment over time remains necessary.
  119. The correct answer is A (Pain, pallor, pulselessness, paresthesia, paralysis, and poikilothermia of the affected extremity): the six classic findings are pain, pallor, pulselessness, paresthesia, paralysis, and poikilothermia, meaning a cold limb that takes on ambient temperature. Pruritus, petechiae, purpura, pitting edema, plethora, and fever belong to dermatologic, venous, or infectious processes rather than arterial occlusion. Papilledema is a neuro ophthalmologic sign and a palpable cord suggests superficial vein thrombosis, so the alternatives each mix in findings unrelated to acute arterial occlusion.
  120. The correct answer is A (A de Winter pattern signifying acute proximal left anterior descending artery occlusion): upsloping J point depression with tall symmetric precordial T waves is the de Winter pattern, an occlusion equivalent of the proximal left anterior descending artery. Hyperkalemia produces narrow peaked T waves with other membrane changes, rate related depression is horizontal or downsloping and resolves with rest, and posterior infarction produces horizontal anterior depression with dominant R waves. Treating the tracing as nonspecific delays reperfusion.
  121. The correct answer is B (The anatomic complexity and extent of coronary disease on the diagnostic angiogram): the SYNTAX score grades the anatomic complexity, extent, and location of coronary disease from the angiogram to inform the surgery versus stenting decision. Operative mortality is estimated by surgical risk models rather than by SYNTAX, and the score does not measure restenosis probability, ischemic burden on perfusion imaging, or completeness of revascularization afterward. Higher scores favor surgery while lower scores make stenting more reasonable.
  122. The correct answer is E (Switching from ticagrelor or prasugrel to clopidogrel, usually about one month after stenting): in the de-escalation trials a uniform strategy switched patients from ticagrelor or prasugrel to clopidogrel about one month after the acute event while aspirin continued. Early aspirin withdrawal with potent monotherapy is a different strategy tested in separate trials, and neither aspirin dose halving, substituting rivaroxaban, nor adding gastric protection constitutes de-escalation. The switch trades platelet inhibition potency for less bleeding once the highest risk window has passed.
  123. The correct answer is D (Pulmonary vascular resistance above 2 Wood units with a wedge pressure of 15 mmHg or less): the 2022 European guidelines define precapillary disease as elevated mean pulmonary arterial pressure with pulmonary vascular resistance exceeding 2 Wood units and a pulmonary arterial wedge pressure at or below 15 mmHg, lowering the resistance threshold from the former 3 Wood units. A wedge above 15 mmHg instead indicates a postcapillary component. Cardiac index, right atrial pressure, and the transpulmonary gradient inform severity but do not define the precapillary pattern.
  124. The correct answer is B (They block the final common pathway where fibrinogen cross links adjacent platelets): glycoprotein IIb IIIa antagonists occupy the receptor that binds fibrinogen, preventing the final step of platelet aggregation regardless of the activating stimulus. Cyclooxygenase acetylation is the mechanism of aspirin, and adenosine diphosphate receptor blockade describes the P2Y12 inhibitors. Vitamin K antagonism describes warfarin, while plasminogen activation describes fibrinolytic agents.
  125. The correct answer is D (Warfarin remains the recommended anticoagulant, since direct agents failed trials in mechanical valves): Patients with mechanical heart valves, and those with moderate to severe rheumatic mitral stenosis, should receive a vitamin K antagonist rather than a direct oral anticoagulant. Dabigatran was studied against warfarin in mechanical valve patients and produced more thromboembolic and bleeding events, and the factor Xa inhibitors have no supporting trial data in this population. Antiplatelet combinations do not provide adequate protection against valve thrombosis or cardioembolic stroke in this setting.
  126. The correct answer is C (Torsemide has more predictable oral absorption, with bioavailability near 80 to 100 percent): oral furosemide absorption ranges roughly from 10 to 100 percent and worsens with gut edema, whereas torsemide is absorbed reliably and also lasts longer. Both drugs remain loop agents acting at the thick ascending limb, not the distal tubule. Torsemide is still highly protein bound and renally active like other loops, and it spares no potassium, so supplementation needs are unchanged.
  127. The correct answer is B (A patent ductus arteriosus shunting from the aorta into the pulmonary artery): Continuous machinery quality flow through systole and diastole at the left infraclavicular region is classic for a ductus, and diastolic runoff explains the bounding pulses. A ruptured sinus of Valsalva aneurysm also produces a continuous murmur but presents abruptly and lower on the sternum. Septal defects and pulmonic stenosis generate systolic rather than continuous murmurs.
  128. The correct answer is E (Critical proximal left anterior descending stenosis with high risk of anterior infarction): biphasic or deeply inverted anterior T waves in a now pain free patient define Wellens syndrome and mark a critical proximal left anterior descending stenosis with high short term infarction risk. Early repolarization, pericarditis, right ventricular strain, and digoxin effect produce different repolarization patterns, and none carries this lesion specific warning. Preserved R waves and absent Q waves distinguish the pattern from completed infarction.
  129. The correct answer is C (Three or more beats above 100 per minute; sustained lasts at least 30 seconds): Ventricular tachycardia is conventionally defined as three or more consecutive ventricular beats at a rate above 100 per minute, and an episode is called sustained when it lasts at least 30 seconds or requires earlier termination for hemodynamic compromise. Definitions built on 60 or 120 per minute, five or ten beat minimums, or two minute and one day markers do not match the accepted convention. A rate of 150 per minute for five seconds describes neither the standard rate floor nor the sustained threshold.
  130. The correct answer is C (Carotid revascularization within two weeks of the index event, given severe symptomatic stenosis): symptomatic stenosis of 70 to 99 percent carries a high early recurrence risk, and trial data show the benefit of revascularization is greatest when performed within two weeks of the neurologic event. Waiting for a second event or for progression on surveillance imaging forfeits most of that benefit, and a planned six week delay was an older practice that allows preventable strokes. Warfarin has no established role in atherosclerotic carotid stenosis without another indication.
  131. The correct answer is D (A PVC burden above roughly 15 to 20 percent of all recorded beats): PVC induced cardiomyopathy is most consistently associated with a high burden, commonly cited as above about 15 to 20 percent of total beats, although it can occasionally occur at lower burdens. Thresholds of 1 or 5 percent are far below the level at which ventricular dysfunction becomes a recognized concern. Symptoms alone and couplet counts do not define the cardiomyopathy risk, which is tied to the overall percentage of ectopic beats over the monitoring period.
  132. The correct answer is C (Coved ST elevation ≥2 mm with an inverted T wave in right precordial leads): The diagnostic type 1 Brugada pattern is a coved ST segment elevation of at least 2 mm followed by a negative T wave in one or more of the right precordial leads, occurring spontaneously or after a sodium channel blocker challenge. A saddleback morphology with an upright or biphasic T wave describes the type 2 pattern, which is suggestive but not diagnostic by itself. Diffuse concave elevation with PR depression indicates pericarditis, and epsilon waves belong to arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy.
  133. The correct answer is A (Colchicine 0.5 mg once daily added to his standard secondary prevention therapy): colchicine 0.5 mg daily reduced cardiovascular events in randomized trials of chronic and recent coronary disease when added to standard therapy. Methotrexate failed to lower events in its cardiovascular trial, canakinumab reduced events but was not approved for this indication, and neither prednisone nor ibuprofen has outcome support, with nonsteroidal agents raising cardiovascular risk. His normal renal and hepatic function makes him an appropriate candidate.
  134. The correct answer is D (Primary adrenal insufficiency with combined glucocorticoid and mineralocorticoid hormone deficiency): Hypotension with hyponatremia, hyperkalemia, salt craving, weight loss, and hyperpigmentation is the classic picture of Addison disease, where cortisol loss drives adrenocorticotropic hormone up and its pigment stimulating fragments darken skin creases and mucosa. Aldosterone deficiency explains the sodium loss and potassium retention. Diuretic misuse and autonomic neuropathy cause orthostatic falls without hyperpigmentation, and pheochromocytoma produces hypertensive spells rather than this chemistry.
  135. The correct answer is C (It leaves alpha mediated vasoconstriction unopposed, allowing coronary spasm to intensify): blocking beta 2 mediated vasodilation leaves alpha adrenergic constriction unopposed in the coronary circulation, so spasm can intensify under nonselective beta blockade. The drug does not meaningfully accelerate calcium channel blocker clearance, raise platelet aggregability, or sensitize the myocardium to catecholamines in a way that explains the warning. Shortened diastole is a feature of tachycardia, and beta blockade lengthens rather than shortens diastole.
  136. The correct answer is B (At least two of the four criteria, such as typical pain plus a rub): acute pericarditis is diagnosed when any two of characteristic chest pain, pericardial friction rub, suggestive ECG changes, and new or worsening effusion are present. No single criterion is mandatory, so frameworks demanding effusion, three findings, or all four findings set the threshold incorrectly. Elevated inflammatory markers and pericardial inflammation on imaging support the diagnosis but are additive findings, not substitutes for the two criterion rule.
  137. The correct answer is C (Verapamil, a nondihydropyridine that slows the sinus and atrioventricular nodes): combining verapamil or diltiazem with a beta blocker in a patient who already has bradycardia and first degree block risks severe bradycardia and higher grade block. Amlodipine and nicardipine are dihydropyridines with predominantly vascular action and little nodal effect. Isosorbide mononitrate and ranolazine relieve angina without depressing cardiac conduction.
  138. The correct answer is C (Stage A, meaning risk factors are present without structural disease, biomarkers, or symptoms): stage A, now called at risk for heart failure, covers patients with hypertension, diabetes, obesity, or cardiotoxic exposures who have normal hearts and normal biomarkers. Stage B requires demonstrated structural disease, elevated filling pressures, or elevated biomarkers, which he lacks. Hypertension itself is a risk factor rather than structural disease, and staging deliberately begins before any imaging abnormality appears.
  139. The correct answer is B (Third degree atrioventricular block with no atrial impulses conducted to the ventricles): Complete heart block is defined by full atrioventricular dissociation in which the atria and ventricles beat independently, with the ventricles maintained by a junctional or ventricular escape at a slow rate. Two to one conduction and high grade block both preserve some conducted beats, which would create a fixed or intermittent relationship between P waves and QRS complexes. Isorhythmic dissociation involves an accelerated lower pacemaker at a rate similar to sinus, and sinoatrial exit block drops entire cycles rather than dissociating them.
  140. The correct answer is B (Area of 1.0 cm² or less, gradient under 40 mmHg, reduced stroke volume): when a weakened ventricle cannot generate normal flow, the gradient stays below the severe threshold even though the calculated orifice is in the severe range. Low dose dobutamine testing is then used to separate truly severe stenosis from pseudosevere disease in which the valve opens further as flow rises. A gradient above 60 mmHg with a jet over 5 m/s describes very severe high gradient disease, the opposite hemodynamic problem. A large valve area with normal or high output does not represent low flow, low gradient physiology at all.
  141. The correct answer is D (Tadalafil, a phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitor prescribed earlier for pulmonary hypertension): riociguat and phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitors both amplify the nitric oxide cyclic guanosine monophosphate pathway, and combining them causes severe hypotension without added benefit, so the pairing is contraindicated and a washout is required when switching. Warfarin is expected therapy in chronic thromboembolic disease and coexists with riociguat routinely. Diuretics, inhaled bronchodilators, and statins have no prohibitive interaction with soluble guanylate cyclase stimulation.
  142. The correct answer is B (Clarithromycin, a strong CYP3A inhibitor that markedly raises ranolazine plasma concentrations): strong CYP3A inhibitors such as clarithromycin and ketoconazole markedly raise ranolazine exposure and are contraindicated because high concentrations prolong the QT interval. Metoprolol, atorvastatin, nitroglycerin, and losartan do not block ranolazine metabolism in a prohibited way, although ranolazine itself can raise simvastatin and digoxin levels. Moderate inhibitors such as diltiazem require a lower ranolazine maximum rather than complete avoidance.
  143. The correct answer is A (Counterclockwise macroreentry around the tricuspid annulus involving the cavotricuspid isthmus): Negative sawtooth flutter waves inferiorly with positive deflections in V1 are the signature of typical counterclockwise atrial flutter, a macroreentrant circuit that passes through the cavotricuspid isthmus. Pulmonary vein firing with chaotic activation describes atrial fibrillation, which is irregular without organized flutter waves. Dual pathway nodal reentry and accessory pathway circuits produce paroxysmal supraventricular tachycardias without sawtooth atrial activity, and a focus near the sinus node would generate upright inferior P waves.
  144. The correct answer is E (The risk of death in hospital and during the months following the event): the GRACE model estimates in hospital and roughly 6 month mortality after an acute coronary syndrome and helps select patients for earlier invasive management. Bleeding risk during antiplatelet therapy is addressed by separate tools such as the CRUSADE score. The model does not predict anatomic findings at angiography, peak troponin values, or stent thrombosis rates.
  145. The correct answer is C (The click moves later in systole and the murmur becomes shorter in duration): squatting increases venous return and afterload, enlarging the left ventricle so the leaflet does not prolapse until later in ejection. The delayed click leaves less systole for regurgitation, so the murmur shortens. An earlier click with a longer murmur is the standing and Valsalva response, when the ventricle is smaller. The sounds remain confined to systole, and a split click or selective muffling is not the expected maneuver response.
  146. The correct answer is C (Exclude a plasma cell disorder using serum free light chains and immunofixation studies): light chain amyloidosis can occasionally produce positive pyrophosphate scans, and missing it delays chemotherapy for a rapidly fatal disease, so serum free light chains plus serum and urine immunofixation must be normal before grade 2 or 3 uptake is accepted as diagnostic of transthyretin disease. When a monoclonal protein is found, tissue biopsy with typing is required instead. Repeat imaging, coronary angiography, family history, and fat pad sampling do not resolve this specific diagnostic fork.
  147. The correct answer is E (Multifocal atrial tachycardia associated with decompensated pulmonary disease and hypoxemia): Multifocal atrial tachycardia is defined by a rate above 100 per minute with P waves of three or more morphologies and varying PR intervals, and it classically accompanies severe pulmonary disease. Wandering atrial pacemaker shares the multiple P wave morphologies but occurs at rates below 100 per minute. Atrial fibrillation lacks discrete P waves altogether, flutter shows organized sawtooth waves, and premature atrial complexes on sinus tachycardia would still show one dominant sinus P wave morphology.
  148. The correct answer is C (Bypass surgery is favored, with lower mortality and fewer infarctions in trials): randomized evidence in diabetic multivessel disease shows bypass surgery lowers mortality and myocardial infarction compared with stenting, at the cost of more early strokes. Survival is therefore not equivalent between strategies, medical therapy alone is inferior for symptomatic three vessel disease, and hybrid procedures lack comparable outcome evidence. Wound healing concerns influence surgical technique but do not overturn the survival advantage.
  149. The correct answer is C (A perimembranous defect involving the thin membranous portion of the septum): Perimembranous defects account for most ventricular septal defects and sit under the aortic valve, bordered by tricuspid tissue. Muscular defects are surrounded by septal muscle, and outlet defects lie just below the pulmonic valve. Inlet defects and Gerbode communications have distinct locations that do not match this description.
  150. The correct answer is D (Reduced renal clearance, which raises natriuretic peptide levels independent of cardiac filling pressures): natriuretic peptides are partly cleared by the kidney, so advanced chronic kidney disease elevates BNP and especially NT proBNP even without congestion, and higher diagnostic cutoffs are often applied. Obesity has the opposite effect, lowering measured levels. Age raises values modestly but far less than the severe renal impairment shown, trace edema does not itself drive peptide synthesis, and diuretics reduce rather than increase wall stress.
  151. The correct answer is C (Empagliflozin, a sodium glucose cotransporter 2 inhibitor studied in high risk patients): the EMPA-REG OUTCOME trial showed this agent reduced cardiovascular death and heart failure hospitalization in type 2 diabetes with established vascular disease. Dapagliflozin and canagliflozin share the class benefit, as do the glucagon like peptide 1 receptor agonists liraglutide, semaglutide, and dulaglutide. Sulfonylureas, dipeptidyl peptidase 4 inhibitors, alpha glucosidase inhibitors, and basal insulin lower glucose without proven reduction in major cardiovascular events.
  152. The correct answer is E (Cangrelor, a direct adenosine triphosphate analog that blocks the P2Y12 receptor): cangrelor is the only intravenous P2Y12 inhibitor, achieving platelet inhibition within minutes and clearing within about an hour of stopping the infusion. Crushed ticagrelor and clopidogrel act through the same receptor but are enteral options with slower and less predictable onset in this setting. Abciximab targets the glycoprotein receptor, and enoxaparin is an anticoagulant rather than a platelet inhibitor.
  153. The correct answer is E (Takayasu arteritis, a granulomatous large vessel disease of young women): constitutional symptoms with pulse deficits, an interarm pressure gradient, and a subclavian bruit in a woman under 40 are the classic presentation of Takayasu arteritis, which inflames the aorta and its major branches. Giant cell arteritis produces a similar histology but affects patients over 50. Polyarteritis nodosa spares the aorta and targets medium vessels, thromboangiitis obliterans causes distal limb ischemia in smokers, and Kawasaki disease is a childhood illness centered on the coronary arteries.
  154. The correct answer is C (They reduce heart failure hospitalization and cardiovascular death whether or not diabetes is present): DAPA HF and EMPEROR Reduced enrolled large nondiabetic populations and showed the same benefit on the combined endpoint regardless of glycemic status, which is why the recommendation is independent of diabetes. The mechanism is not primarily glucose lowering. SGLT2 inhibitors produce only mild diuresis and do not replace loop diuretics, they do not raise ejection fraction by ten points, and no trial showed a direct antiarrhythmic effect of that kind.
  155. The correct answer is D (Vasospastic angina with transient occlusive spasm of an epicardial coronary artery): rest pain clustering between midnight and morning, prompt nitrate response, and transient ST elevation during episodes are the classic signature of epicardial coronary spasm. Plaque rupture typically produces crescendo pain not confined to night, reflux does not cause ST elevation, and microvascular angina is usually exertional without ST elevation. Takotsubo produces a persistent wall motion abnormality rather than brief self terminating episodes.
  156. The correct answer is C (Transient apical ballooning after acute emotional stress without obstructive coronary artery disease): takotsubo, or stress cardiomyopathy, classically follows an emotional or physical trigger in a postmenopausal woman, produces apical ballooning that spares the base, and is attributed to catecholamine surge, with wall motion typically recovering over days to weeks. The dysfunction crosses coronary territories rather than matching one artery. Basal ballooning is a rare variant rather than the classic form, the apical akinesis is transient rather than permanent, and myocarditis is a separate diagnosis requiring different confirmation.
  157. The correct answer is E (Preferential arteriolar dilation raising capillary pressure and driving fluid into tissue): Dihydropyridines dilate precapillary arterioles far more than venules, so capillary hydrostatic pressure rises and fluid filters into dependent tissue without any total body sodium excess. Because volume is not expanded, diuretics work poorly, whereas adding a renin angiotensin blocker dilates the venous side and often relieves the swelling. The stable weight, clear lungs, normal albumin, and normal veins argue against cardiac, hepatic, or lymphatic causes.
  158. The correct answer is B (Group 3 comprises pulmonary hypertension from interstitial lung disease and chronic obstructive disease): the classification assigns pulmonary arterial hypertension including idiopathic and connective tissue forms to group 1, left heart disease to group 2, lung disease and hypoxia to group 3, chronic thromboembolic disease to group 4, and multifactorial conditions such as sarcoidosis to group 5. Emphysema therefore belongs in group 3 rather than group 1. Chronic thromboembolic obstruction defines group 4, left heart transmission defines group 2, and connective tissue disease sits within group 1 rather than group 5.
  159. The correct answer is C (Symptoms directly attributable to documented bradycardia without an identified correctable factor): The class I indication in sinus node dysfunction rests on establishing that the patient's symptoms are caused by the documented bradycardia, since the condition itself does not shorten life and pacing exists to relieve symptoms. A rate below sixty and pauses over two seconds are common and often benign, especially during sleep or in trained individuals. Intrinsic heart rate testing is rarely used and not by itself an implant criterion, and family device history plays no role in the indication.
  160. The correct answer is E (It usually reflects infranodal disease and warrants permanent pacing even without symptoms): Mobitz type II block typically arises below the AV node in the His Purkinje system, carries a substantial risk of abrupt progression to complete heart block with an unreliable escape, and is a pacing indication even in asymptomatic patients once reversible causes are excluded. Block within the node driven by vagal tone that improves with exercise describes Wenckebach physiology. Atropine often fails or worsens infranodal block, and a narrow QRS does not confer a benign prognosis for this lesion.
  161. The correct answer is E (Intimal hyperplasia followed by accelerated, friable atherosclerosis within the arterialized vein): vein grafts first develop intimal hyperplasia in the arterial circulation and then accelerated atherosclerosis that is diffuse, lipid rich, and friable, explaining failure a decade after surgery. Harvest injury causes early rather than decade delayed thrombosis, and scar compression, venous spasm, and a valve like calcific process are not the recognized biology. The friable plaque also explains the high embolic risk during graft intervention.
  162. The correct answer is B (Dapagliflozin 10 mg once daily, the fixed dose studied in the outcome trial): DAPA HF used a single fixed dose of 10 mg daily with no titration scheme, and the heart failure indication carries that same dose. Her eGFR of 48 requires no dose reduction, since the trial enrolled patients down to an eGFR of 30. Twice daily dosing, alternate day dosing, and monthly uptitration are not part of any approved heart failure regimen for this drug.
  163. The correct answer is C (Fabry disease, an X linked deficiency of alpha galactosidase A causing glycosphingolipid accumulation): globotriaosylceramide storage in myocytes, endothelium, nerves, and podocytes produces the combination of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy mimicry, neuropathic pain from childhood, angiokeratomas, and proteinuric kidney disease, and enzyme replacement is available. Amyloidosis causes hypertrophy but not acroparesthesias or angiokeratomas and typically appears later in life. Danon disease, Friedreich ataxia, and Pompe disease each carry hypertrophy but lack this dermatologic and renal constellation.
  164. The correct answer is A (Unstable angina, new rest angina in an acute coronary syndrome without myocyte necrosis): ischemic symptoms occurring at rest with dynamic ST depression but troponin staying under the 99th percentile define unstable angina. A diagnostic troponin rise would reclassify the event as non ST elevation infarction, and a stable unchanged exertional pattern describes stable angina. Vasospastic angina produces transient ST elevation rather than depression, and reflux does not cause ischemic electrocardiographic changes.
  165. The correct answer is D (Raise the warfarin target INR to 3.0 given the added risk factor): atrial fibrillation is a thromboembolic risk factor that raises the recommended INR target to 3.0 for a mechanical aortic valve. Direct oral anticoagulants remain contraindicated with any mechanical prosthesis. Adding antiplatelet agents increases bleeding without substituting for the higher anticoagulation intensity the combined risk requires.
  166. The correct answer is D (Intravenous procainamide infused with blood pressure and QRS monitoring): the irregular wide complex rhythm with varying QRS width represents preexcited atrial fibrillation, and procainamide slows accessory pathway conduction while the patient remains stable. Adenosine, diltiazem, metoprolol, and digoxin all act at the atrioventricular node, which can accelerate conduction down the pathway and precipitate ventricular fibrillation. Electrical cardioversion is the fallback for any instability.
  167. The correct answer is C (Begin enalapril and a beta blocker, both considered compatible with breastfeeding): after delivery, guideline directed therapy should begin promptly, and enalapril with metoprolol are established choices with minimal transfer into breast milk, allowing effective treatment without abandoning breastfeeding. Continuing only hydralazine and nitrates or waiting for repeat imaging withholds superior neurohormonal therapy from a woman with severe dysfunction. Safety data for sacubitril valsartan during lactation are lacking, so it is not the immediate postpartum choice, and her diuretic should continue at the lowest effective dose rather than stop abruptly.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is E (Add dapagliflozin for its heart failure benefit, independent of his glycemic status): sodium glucose cotransporter 2 inhibitors reduce cardiovascular death and heart failure hospitalization in reduced ejection fraction regardless of hemoglobin A1c, so guideline directed therapy calls for adding one even when glucose is controlled. Deferring because the A1c is acceptable misses the point that the indication is cardiac rather than glycemic. Saxagliptin increased heart failure hospitalization in its outcome trial, and neither sulfonylureas nor basal insulin improves heart failure outcomes.
  2. The correct answer is D (Echocardiography at baseline and roughly every three months while therapy continues): trastuzumab produces a largely reversible decline in ejection fraction that develops during treatment, so serial imaging about every three months allows dysfunction to be caught, the drug held, and heart failure therapy started before irreversible injury. Baseline imaging alone misses the interval declines that are typically asymptomatic at first. Monthly magnetic resonance is impractical and unnecessary, troponin cannot replace functional imaging for this agent, and starting surveillance years after completion inverts the risk window.
  3. The correct answer is B (Initiate with electrocardiographic monitoring and QT measurement after each dose titration): sotalol prolongs the QT interval in dose dependent fashion, so initiation is performed with serial electrocardiograms, typically during inpatient monitoring, with dose adjustment guided by the measured interval and renal function. Unmonitored home initiation with a distant follow up tracing misses early torsades risk. A prophylactic defibrillator, a same day intravenous load, and combination with amiodarone are each inappropriate ways to begin therapy.
  4. The correct answer is A (Check potassium and renal function within one week and again at one month): mineralocorticoid antagonist initiation requires early laboratory surveillance, with potassium and creatinine rechecked within the first week, again around one month, and periodically thereafter, because hyperkalemia risk is highest soon after starting, particularly with reduced renal function like hers. Symptom triggered testing misses the asymptomatic rise that precedes dangerous levels. A six month or single three month check leaves the highest risk window unmonitored, and home blood pressure readings cannot detect potassium or creatinine changes.
  5. The correct answer is C (Resume about one week or more after his uncomplicated event if asymptomatic): after an uncomplicated myocardial infarction with successful revascularization, sexual activity is reasonable a week or more after the event when the patient can perform mild to moderate exertion without symptoms. Six month delays and permanent avoidance reflect outdated fear rather than evidence. A maximal treadmill test is not a prerequisite for a low risk patient, and reworking his cardiac regimen around phosphodiesterase inhibitor use puts convenience ahead of ischemia control.
  6. The correct answer is D (Initiate statin therapy, since this score indicates clearly treatment worthy subclinical atherosclerosis): a score of 100 or more, or at the 75th percentile or higher, indicates enough subclinical atherosclerosis that statin therapy is clearly favored in an intermediate risk adult. Repeating the scan in five years discards the information already obtained, and an abnormal score in an asymptomatic patient does not itself justify invasive angiography or stress echocardiography. Aspirin is a separate individualized decision rather than the indicated response.
  7. The correct answer is E (Aspirin plus clopidogrel for at least one month, then long term single therapy): dual antiplatelet therapy with aspirin and clopidogrel is standard after carotid stenting, continued for at least 30 days while the stent endothelializes, after which a single agent is maintained indefinitely. Aspirin monotherapy from the start leaves the fresh stent underprotected against platelet mediated thrombosis. Warfarin and anticoagulant containing combinations add bleeding risk without evidence in this setting, and clopidogrel alone for two weeks is shorter and narrower than the studied regimens.
  8. The correct answer is B (Intravenous atropine in incremental doses while monitoring rate and perfusion): symptomatic sinus bradycardia in inferior infarction usually reflects heightened vagal tone, which responds to atropine as the recommended first drug. Transvenous pacing is reserved for atropine refractory bradycardia or high grade block. Epinephrine and isoproterenol raise myocardial oxygen demand during active ischemia and follow atropine failure, and a fluid bolus does not correct the chronotropic problem.
  9. The correct answer is E (Arrange a supervised exercise treadmill test to evaluate functional capacity objectively): sedentary patients frequently underreport symptoms, and a supervised treadmill test is reasonable in asymptomatic severe stenosis to unmask provocable symptoms, limitation, or an abnormal blood pressure response. Replacement is not yet mandated with preserved ejection fraction and unproven symptom status. Surveillance that trusts self report misses exactly the latent limitation the test is designed to expose.
  10. The correct answer is D (Optimized medical therapy including a renin angiotensin inhibitor, statin, and antiplatelet agent): randomized trials including CORAL showed that routine stenting adds no benefit over medical therapy for atherosclerotic renal artery stenosis, so treatment starts with a renin angiotensin inhibitor, statin, and antiplatelet therapy with monitoring of kidney function. Prophylactic stenting and surgical bypass are reserved for specific failures of medical management, such as refractory hypertension or recurrent flash pulmonary edema. Renin angiotensin blockade is beneficial rather than harmful in unilateral disease, and serial invasive angiography exposes her to risk without guiding therapy.
  11. The correct answer is B (Pulmonary endarterectomy at an experienced surgical center, which is potentially curative): surgical removal of organized thromboembolic material is the treatment of choice for anatomically accessible proximal disease, offering the possibility of hemodynamic normalization that no medical therapy matches, so every patient deserves operability assessment by an expert team. Balloon angioplasty and riociguat are directed at inoperable or residual disease rather than operable proximal obstruction. Anticoagulation alone leaves the obstructive lesions untreated, and transplantation is reserved for disease not amenable to endarterectomy.
  12. The correct answer is C (Refer for aortic valve replacement after heart team evaluation of intervention options): symptomatic severe high gradient aortic stenosis carries a class 1 indication for valve replacement, and exertional angina with dyspnea qualifies. Watchful waiting, vasodilator therapy, and statins do not change outcomes once symptoms appear in severe stenosis. Exercise testing is contraindicated when severe stenosis is already clearly symptomatic.
  13. The correct answer is C (Give clopidogrel 600 mg 24 hours after the last ticagrelor dose, then 75 mg daily): ticagrelor's effect fades quickly, so a de-escalation switch in the first month requires a full clopidogrel load timed 24 hours after the final ticagrelor dose to avoid a gap in platelet inhibition. Starting maintenance dosing without a load, or interposing a five day washout, leaves a fresh stent underprotected. Dropping to aspirin alone this early risks stent thrombosis, and overlapping two P2Y12 inhibitors adds bleeding without benefit.
  14. The correct answer is A (Administer an intravenous heparin bolus followed by a continuous therapeutic infusion): immediate intravenous heparin limits thrombus propagation, protects collateral beds, and improves outcomes while definitive revascularization is organized, and its short half life allows rapid adjustment around procedures. Antiplatelet loading does not halt propagating thrombus in acute limb ischemia. Systemic thrombolysis is not standard for limb ischemia and catheter directed delivery requires imaging first, subcutaneous enoxaparin is too inflexible periprocedurally, and prostanoids have no role in acute embolic occlusion.
  15. The correct answer is C (Transfer for primary PCI, accepting the delay because fibrinolysis is contraindicated): ischemic stroke within the previous three months is an absolute contraindication to fibrinolysis, so PCI is the only reperfusion option and a 140 minute device time is accepted. Full or reduced dose tenecteplase risks hemorrhagic transformation of the recent infarct, and neurology review of old imaging does not change that. Medical therapy alone withholds reperfusion that transfer can still provide.
  16. The correct answer is C (Double the intravenous furosemide dose and reassess urine output within hours): an inadequate early urine response indicates the dose has not crossed the natriuretic threshold, so the correct move is prompt dose escalation with reassessment within hours, not the next day. Repeating an ineffective dose or waiting until morning prolongs congestion and its renal consequences. Sequential nephron blockade with a thiazide is the step after adequate loop escalation fails, ultrafiltration is reserved for diuretic refractory congestion, and converting to an oral agent during acute decompensation reduces delivery of drug to the kidney.
  17. The correct answer is B (Refer for aortic valve replacement based on the abnormal exercise response): exercise induced symptoms or a fall in systolic blood pressure converts asymptomatic severe stenosis into an indication for valve replacement. Continued surveillance ignores a demonstrated abnormal response that predicts adverse events, and repeating the test simply delays definitive care. Dobutamine testing is reserved for low flow low gradient stenosis with reduced ejection fraction, which she does not have.
  18. The correct answer is C (Intravenous furosemide at 2.5 times the total daily home oral dose): in acute decompensation, initial intravenous dosing should at least equal and often reach 2.5 times the home daily oral dose, a strategy associated with faster decongestion in the DOSE trial without meaningful harm. Low fixed doses such as 20 or 40 mg risk underdiuresis in a patient already requiring 80 mg orally each day. Continuing oral therapy ignores impaired gut absorption during congestion, and waiting for output to fall before dosing delays treatment.
  19. The correct answer is B (Repeat the ejection fraction at least 40 days after the infarction): primary prevention defibrillator implantation is deferred until at least 40 days after myocardial infarction because early implantation trials showed no survival benefit and ventricular function often recovers with revascularization and medical therapy. Implanting during the first weeks therefore commits some patients to devices they will not need. A stress test does not determine device candidacy, waiting for an annual visit imposes an arbitrary delay beyond the reassessment window, and amiodarone does not substitute for defibrillator protection.
  20. The correct answer is B (Perform urgent pericardiocentesis with temporizing intravenous volume expansion while preparing): clinical tamponade requires prompt drainage, and cautious fluid can transiently support filling pressures while the procedure is arranged. Beta blockade removes the compensatory tachycardia that maintains cardiac output, and diuresis lowers the elevated filling pressures the patient depends on, so both can precipitate collapse. Elective intubation with sedation drops preload and adds positive intrathoracic pressure dangerously, and corticosteroids do nothing for mechanical compression from malignant fluid.
  21. The correct answer is E (Hold apixaban about 48 hours preoperatively and resume when hemostasis allows): direct oral anticoagulants have short half lives, so a roughly 48 hour hold before a high bleeding risk procedure with normal renal function provides adequate washout, and heparin bridging increases bleeding without reducing stroke in atrial fibrillation. Continuing through spinal surgery risks catastrophic bleeding into a closed space. A seven day hold with a one month gap is excessive, and a warfarin switch complicates rather than simplifies management.
  22. The correct answer is A (Perform synchronized cardioversion without delay using appropriate sedation as time allows): hypotension, chest pressure, and confusion define an unstable tachycardia, and electrical cardioversion is the treatment of choice. A trial of adenosine may be considered only when it does not delay the shock, and this patient has already failed initial measures with worsening perfusion. Diltiazem can deepen hypotension, and neither an electrocardiogram nor a fluid bolus should postpone definitive therapy.
  23. The correct answer is B (Warfarin with an INR target of 2 to 3 regardless of her score): atrial fibrillation with rheumatic mitral stenosis carries very high embolic risk that conventional scoring does not capture, so vitamin K antagonist therapy is indicated irrespective of the CHA2DS2-VASc result. Direct oral anticoagulants have not been validated in significant rheumatic mitral stenosis. Antiplatelet regimens or no therapy leave her exposed to preventable stroke.
  24. The correct answer is A (Within six hours of surgery once significant ongoing bleeding has been excluded): aspirin within six hours of surgery, once bleeding is excluded, improves early graft patency and reduces ischemic events. Waiting for chest tube removal, the two week wound check, or platelet function recovery sacrifices the interval when grafts are most vulnerable to thrombosis. Deferring a month on bleeding grounds misstates the evidence, which shows early aspirin is safe in the absence of active hemorrhage.
  25. The correct answer is D (Transcutaneous pacing now while arranging temporary transvenous pacing): complete heart block with a wide escape indicates infranodal disease, which rarely responds to atropine, so immediate pacing support is required for this hypotensive patient. Repeating atropine wastes time at an infranodal level of block. Adenosine would worsen conduction, theophylline is not acute therapy, and amiodarone can suppress the only escape rhythm she has.
  26. The correct answer is D (Add the remaining foundational classes at low doses rather than maximizing lisinopril first): low doses of several disease modifying classes provide greater benefit than a maximal dose of a single agent, so contemporary practice adds a beta blocker, MRA, and SGLT2 inhibitor early. Titrating lisinopril alone delays therapies with independent mortality benefit. Hydralazine with nitrates is reserved for patients who cannot take renin angiotensin blockers or as add on therapy in selected patients, and amlodipine has no disease modifying role in HFrEF.
  27. The correct answer is A (Hold spironolactone now, counsel on dietary potassium, and recheck the level soon): the trend shown crosses above 5.5 mEq/L, the threshold at which a mineralocorticoid antagonist should be held rather than merely reduced. Continuing the drug unchanged or delaying the recheck for months risks dangerous hyperkalemia on a clearly rising trajectory. The SGLT2 inhibitor does not raise potassium and may even lower hyperkalemia risk, and escalating the loop diuretic in a euvolemic patient is not an appropriate potassium management strategy.
  28. The correct answer is D (Warfarin adjusted to an international normalized ratio of 2.0 to 3.0): moderate or severe rheumatic mitral stenosis was excluded from the direct oral anticoagulant trials, and the INVICTUS trial showed worse outcomes with rivaroxaban than warfarin in this population. Warfarin therefore remains the standard for valvular atrial fibrillation of this type. Apixaban, rivaroxaban, dabigatran, and edoxaban are each inappropriate here regardless of dose or monitoring plan.
  29. The correct answer is A (Intravenous diltiazem or verapamil given slowly with blood pressure monitoring): nondihydropyridine calcium channel blockers terminate nodal reentry effectively and avoid the bronchospasm that adenosine and beta blockade can provoke in severe asthma. Adenosine may trigger significant bronchoconstriction in this population, and esmolol carries the same airway concern. Cardioversion is premature for a stable patient with drug options remaining, and amiodarone is slow and unnecessary here.
  30. The correct answer is D (Return within about two weeks if he remains asymptomatic during ordinary activity): after an uncomplicated infarction with preserved ventricular function, sedentary work can typically resume within roughly two weeks, alongside cardiac rehabilitation. A three month absence or retirement medicalizes recovery far beyond the evidence, and maximal exercise testing is not required to clear a low risk patient for desk work. Returning the day after discharge shortchanges early recovery, medication titration, and symptom surveillance.
  31. The correct answer is A (Annual aortic imaging plus beta blocker or angiotensin receptor blocker therapy): patients with Marfan syndrome need annual imaging of the aortic root and prophylactic medical therapy, and both beta blockers and angiotensin receptor blockers slow aortic root growth in trials. A five year imaging interval could miss the growth that changes surgical timing, while prophylactic replacement at 4.1 centimeters would expose him to operative risk years early, since dissection risk clearly rises with diameter. Activity guidance complements rather than replaces medication, and d dimer has no surveillance role in aneurysm care.
  32. The correct answer is C (Bromocriptine as an investigational adjunct, given with anticoagulation because of thrombotic risk): a cleaved prolactin fragment is implicated in peripartum cardiomyopathy, and prolactin blockade with bromocriptine has shown promising recovery signals in small trials, though it requires concomitant anticoagulation and remains under study rather than universal. Early repeat pregnancy is discouraged because recurrence risk is highest with a persistently reduced ejection fraction. Defibrillator implantation is premature since many patients recover within months, biopsy is not routine, and beta blockers remain part of standard therapy here.
  33. The correct answer is A (A woman with a Fontan circulation presenting for routine ongoing cardiac care): Single ventricle palliation represents anatomic and physiologic complexity of the highest category, and guidelines advise that such patients receive ongoing management with adult congenital specialists. Repaired simple defects without residua and small restrictive shunts can reasonably be followed in general cardiology. Bicuspid valves with normal aortas and mitral prolapse are managed routinely outside specialized centers.
  34. The correct answer is C (Tuberculous pericarditis, supported by a lymphocytic exudate with elevated adenosine deaminase): the laboratory results shown demonstrate an adenosine deaminase of 68 U/L, a protein rich lymphocyte predominant exudate, and low fluid glucose, a pattern highly suggestive of tuberculosis in a patient from an endemic region. Uremic and hypothyroid effusions are typically transudative without adenosine deaminase elevation. A bland viral effusion would not produce these chemistry abnormalities, and a simple reactive process shows normal chemistry.
  35. The correct answer is D (Surgical mitral repair at a center experienced in degenerative valve reconstruction): symptomatic severe primary mitral regurgitation is a class 1 indication for intervention, and surgical repair is preferred over replacement for degenerative disease because it preserves the subvalvular apparatus and avoids prosthetic complications. Transcatheter repair is reserved for patients at high or prohibitive surgical risk. Medical therapy or biomarker surveillance merely defers definitive correction.
  36. The correct answer is C (Continue warfarin at her current dose with close INR monitoring): when the warfarin dose is 5 mg daily or less, continuing warfarin through the first trimester is reasonable because embryopathy risk is low at that dose and warfarin protects the valve more reliably than heparin regimens. Heparin strategies are preferred when the dose exceeds 5 mg. Apixaban is contraindicated with mechanical valves, and stopping anticoagulation invites valve thrombosis.
  37. The correct answer is C (No antibiotic prophylaxis because her lesion is not a high risk condition): current recommendations restrict prophylaxis to the highest risk conditions, such as prosthetic valves or prosthetic material, prior endocarditis, certain congenital lesions, and transplant valvulopathy, and native valve prolapse with regurgitation is not among them. An antibiotic here exposes her to adverse effects without expected benefit. Prophylaxis for gastrointestinal endoscopy was abandoned even for high risk patients.
  38. The correct answer is E (Begin a loop diuretic to relieve congestion and titrate toward euvolemia): symptomatic volume overload in HFpEF is treated with diuretics regardless of the underlying regimen, since congestion drives his orthopnea, edema, and hypoxemia. Raising losartan or adding amlodipine addresses blood pressure without removing the excess sodium and water. A beta blocker does not decongest and can blunt the chronotropic response many HFpEF patients rely on, and exercise testing has no role while he is actively congested.
  39. The correct answer is D (Transition to intravenous unfractionated heparin before the planned delivery date): warfarin is replaced with intravenous unfractionated heparin in anticipation of delivery, typically around 36 weeks, because the fetus remains anticoagulated by warfarin and labor then risks fetal intracranial hemorrhage. Heparin can be stopped shortly before induction and restarted promptly afterward. Stopping anticoagulation or substituting antiplatelet or direct oral agents exposes the mitral prosthesis to thrombosis.
  40. The correct answer is A (A trial of vitamin K antagonist therapy with echocardiographic reassessment of gradients): suspected bioprosthetic valve thrombosis in a hemodynamically stable patient is treated first with a vitamin K antagonist, which commonly restores leaflet motion and lowers gradients on follow up imaging. Reoperation and valve in valve implantation are reserved for anticoagulation failure or instability. Fibrinolysis is directed at obstructive mechanical prosthesis thrombosis, and antiplatelet therapy is insufficient for established thrombus.
  41. The correct answer is A (Hold warfarin three to five days preoperatively and resume it without heparin bridging): a bileaflet mechanical aortic valve without atrial fibrillation, prior thromboembolism, or other risk factors can have warfarin interrupted without bridging because short term thrombotic risk is low. Bridging adds bleeding risk without benefit in this low risk subgroup. Dose reduction produces an INR that is neither protective nor safe for surgery, and apixaban is contraindicated with mechanical valves.
  42. The correct answer is E (Percutaneous balloon mitral commissurotomy performed at a comprehensive valve center): a symptomatic patient with a valve area of 1.5 cm² or less, favorable Wilkins morphology, no left atrial thrombus, and less than moderate regurgitation is the ideal candidate for percutaneous balloon commissurotomy. Valve replacement commits a young woman to prosthesis related risk unnecessarily. Medical therapy alone leaves a fixed mechanical obstruction untreated.
  43. The correct answer is A (Shift the final dose to late afternoon and raise the head of the bed): His 9 pm dose falls within four hours of lying down, so the drug's alpha agonist pressor effect is peaking while he is flat, producing the supine hypertension his monitor captured. Moving the last dose to at least four hours before bedtime and elevating the head of the bed are the standard corrections. Adding pressor agents at night, whether more midodrine, droxidopa, or nocturnal fludrocortisone, worsens the problem, and layering a central sympatholytic on top invites daytime orthostatic collapse.
  44. The correct answer is A (Low dose aspirin begun between 12 and 16 weeks and continued until delivery): in women at high risk for preeclampsia, daily low dose aspirin initiated in this window, ideally before 16 weeks when spiral artery remodeling is completing, reduces the incidence of the disease and its complications. Starting aspirin in the third trimester misses the placental window where the drug acts. Antioxidant vitamins failed in randomized trials, therapeutic anticoagulation is not a preeclampsia preventive, and diuretics treat neither the mechanism nor the outcome.
  45. The correct answer is C (Increase metoprolol succinate toward the target dose since vital signs allow): target doses of beta blockers provide greater survival benefit than intermediate doses, and a heart rate of 68 with acceptable blood pressure and no symptoms permits further uptitration. A resting rate below 70 is not a contraindication to careful dose increases in an asymptomatic patient. Switching agents restarts titration without evidence of advantage, lowering the ARNI sacrifices one benefit for another, and ivabradine is considered only after the beta blocker is maximally tolerated.
  46. The correct answer is A (Sustained ventricular tachycardia and sudden cardiac death from ventricular arrhythmia): QRS duration ≥ 180 milliseconds in repaired tetralogy reflects right ventricular dilation and slowed conduction across scar, and it is a validated marker for ventricular tachycardia and sudden death. This finding strengthens the case for timely pulmonary valve intervention and arrhythmia evaluation. Conduction block, stroke, and aortic events are not the outcomes this marker predicts.
  47. The correct answer is C (A direct oral anticoagulant such as apixaban at standard stroke prevention dosing): a bioprosthetic valve beyond the early postoperative period does not preclude direct oral anticoagulants, which are appropriate for atrial fibrillation with this risk score. Vitamin K antagonists are required only for mechanical valves or significant rheumatic mitral stenosis, and an INR of 3.0 would add bleeding risk without benefit. Antiplatelet combinations underprotect at this score, and appendage occlusion is reserved for patients unable to take anticoagulants.
  48. The correct answer is E (Discharge home with primary care follow up and no routine further cardiac testing): a HEART score of 2 with normal, unchanging high sensitivity troponins identifies a low risk patient in whom discharge without urgent additional testing is explicitly endorsed. Overnight observation, anticoagulation, and angiography all treat a diagnosis her flat biomarker series has effectively excluded. Discharge advice keyed only to pain lasting an hour gives her the wrong return precautions rather than structured follow up.
  49. The correct answer is A (Add a PCSK9 monoclonal antibody such as evolocumab to current therapy): a very high risk patient whose LDL remains at or above 70 mg/dL despite maximal statin plus ezetimibe meets the indication for PCSK9 inhibition, which produces roughly 60 percent further reduction and lowers events. Removing ezetimibe or waiting a year moves him away from goal. Fenofibrate targets triglycerides without proven event reduction here, and bile acid sequestrants are weaker, harder to tolerate, and not the preferred next step.
  50. The correct answer is A (Postpone the elective operation until at least six months after stent placement): elective noncardiac surgery is optimally delayed six months after drug-eluting stenting so dual antiplatelet therapy is not interrupted during the highest risk window for stent thrombosis. Stopping both agents at three months, with or without heparin, invites thrombosis because heparin does not substitute for platelet inhibition. Operating through dual therapy is reserved for surgery that cannot wait, and a truly elective hernia repair can wait, though not forever.
  51. The correct answer is E (Low dose dobutamine stress echocardiography to separate true severe from pseudosevere stenosis): low flow low gradient stenosis with reduced ejection fraction requires low dose dobutamine testing to distinguish true severe stenosis from pseudosevere disease and to assess contractile reserve. Calcification supports severity but does not establish it by itself.
