  52. The correct answer is D (Surgical repair that closes the defect and baffles the anomalous vein leftward): Sinus venosus defects lack the septal rims a device needs and are accompanied here by anomalous pulmonary venous return, which must be redirected to the left atrium. Surgery accomplishes both goals in one operation with excellent results. A significant shunt with chamber enlargement warrants repair rather than observation or vasodilator therapy.
  53. The correct answer is D (Above 450 ms in men and above 460 ms in women): conventional screening labels a corrected QT prolonged beyond 450 ms in adult men and 460 ms in adult women, with values of 500 ms or more marking substantially increased torsades risk. Thresholds near 380 to 400 ms sit inside the normal range and would flag healthy patients. Waiting for 520, 540, or 600 ms before acting would ignore clinically important prolongation well below those extremes.
  54. The correct answer is C (Transfer immediately for primary PCI without administering fibrinolytic therapy beforehand): a first medical contact to device time of 120 minutes or less favors primary PCI, and 90 minutes is well within that window. Fibrinolysis, whether full or half dose, adds bleeding risk without benefit when timely PCI is available. Observing on heparin or delaying transfer for echocardiography withholds definitive reperfusion from an evolving anterior infarction.
  55. The correct answer is A (Invasive coronary function testing with adenosine derived flow reserve and acetylcholine provocation): invasive coronary function testing measures adenosine based flow reserve and microcirculatory resistance and adds acetylcholine provocation, distinguishing microvascular dysfunction from epicardial or microvascular spasm. Intravascular ultrasound looks for hidden plaque rather than physiology, ventriculography addresses takotsubo rather than her chronic exertional pattern, and exercise hemodynamic study targets pulmonary causes. Repeating perfusion imaging would not explain the mechanism already demonstrated.
  56. The correct answer is A (Catheter ablation of the cavotricuspid isthmus with continued anticoagulation afterward): typical atrial flutter is cured by isthmus ablation in well over 90 percent of cases and is preferred over long term drug therapy, particularly when rate control has failed. Adding digoxin or committing to amiodarone accepts ongoing arrhythmia and toxicity risk. Node ablation with pacing is a last resort, and repeated cardioversions do not prevent recurrence.
  57. The correct answer is D (Transfemoral transcatheter aortic valve implantation after heart team confirmation of candidacy): for symptomatic patients older than 80 years, the 2020 guideline recommends transfemoral transcatheter replacement when anatomy is suitable. Surgery offers no advantage at this age with favorable access, and a mechanical prosthesis is a particularly poor fit for an octogenarian. Balloon valvuloplasty and medical therapy alone both leave severe stenosis effectively untreated.
  58. The correct answer is D (Abbreviated dual therapy for one to three months, then single antiplatelet therapy): high bleeding risk patients do well with one to three months of dual therapy followed by single antiplatelet therapy, an approach current drug eluting stents make acceptable. A full year of dual therapy disregards her formidable bleeding profile, aspirin alone from day one underprotects a fresh stent, half dose dual therapy is not an established strategy, and adding anticoagulation multiplies hemorrhagic risk. Abbreviated regimens preserve most of the ischemic protection.
  59. The correct answer is C (Discontinue amiodarone and begin systemic corticosteroids for presumed pulmonary toxicity): subacute dyspnea, cough, and new interstitial infiltrates in a long term amiodarone user represent pulmonary toxicity until proven otherwise, and management is immediate drug withdrawal with corticosteroids for significant disease. Dose reduction allows continued lung injury from a drug whose tissue half life spans weeks to months. Inhaled steroids are inadequate, biopsy is rarely needed before acting, and dronedarone is not a like for like substitute in this setting.
  60. The correct answer is B (Add amlodipine while continuing the current beta blocker dose unchanged): his heart rate is already at goal, so the next move for persistent angina is adding a dihydropyridine calcium channel blocker or a long acting nitrate rather than pushing the beta blocker. Further beta blockade risks symptomatic bradycardia with little antianginal gain. Verapamil combined with a beta blocker invites conduction disease and bradycardia, dropping the beta blocker for ranolazine removes proven first line therapy, and revascularization comes after optimized medical treatment, not before it.
  61. The correct answer is A (Give intravenous labetalol or nicardipine to bring pressure below 185/110): Thrombolysis requires blood pressure below 185/110 mm Hg before the bolus, and titratable intravenous agents achieve that quickly without abandoning reperfusion. Giving alteplase at her current pressure raises intracranial hemorrhage risk unacceptably. Driving pressure below 140/90 sacrifices collateral perfusion of the ischemic penumbra, oral agents act too slowly and unpredictably for this window, and canceling an otherwise indicated lysis for a correctable pressure denies proven benefit.
  62. The correct answer is C (Cross sectional imaging or transesophageal echocardiography to assess for baffle stenosis or leak): Facial fullness with positional symptoms suggests superior limb baffle obstruction, a recognized late complication of Mustard and Senning repairs alongside baffle leaks. Dedicated imaging with magnetic resonance, computed tomography, or transesophageal echocardiography defines the pathways when surface windows fail. Stress testing, coronary angiography, and tilt testing do not visualize the atrial baffles.
  63. The correct answer is B (Attempt a modified Valsalva maneuver with passive leg raise at the bedside): vagal maneuvers are the recommended first intervention for hemodynamically stable narrow complex tachycardia, and the modified technique terminates more episodes than the standard method. Immediate cardioversion is reserved for unstable patients or refractory episodes. Oral beta blockade acts too slowly, amiodarone is not first line for stable supraventricular tachycardia, and overdrive pacing is not an initial bedside therapy.
  64. The correct answer is B (Start tafamidis to slow disease progression and reduce cardiovascular mortality): tafamidis stabilizes the transthyretin tetramer and reduced all cause mortality and cardiovascular hospitalization in ATTR-ACT, making it the disease directed therapy for symptomatic transthyretin cardiac amyloidosis. Chemotherapy targets light chain disease, which his negative monoclonal screen excludes.   71. The correct answer is B (At the end of isovolumic relaxation, when ventricular pressure falls below atrial pressure): after aortic valve closure the ventricle relaxes with both valves shut, and once its pressure drops beneath left atrial pressure the mitral valve opens and the E wave of early rapid filling begins. Atrial contraction produces the later A wave through an already open valve. Isovolumic contraction and the ejection phases occur while the mitral valve is closed, and the mitral and aortic valves are never open together in a normal cycle.

The correct answer is C (Implant a permanent pacemaker and then continue the antiarrhythmic therapy): tachycardia bradycardia syndrome with symptomatic conversion pauses is a recognized pacing indication when necessary drug therapy aggravates the bradycardia, and a pacemaker allows full dosing without pause related syncope. Accepting symptomatic pauses risks injury. Theophylline is unreliable for this purpose, abandoning rhythm control leaves disabling symptoms untreated, and node ablation is reserved for refractory rate control problems.
  132.   133.   134. The correct answer is B (Ablation is a guideline supported first line option for a patient like him): current guidance gives first line catheter ablation a class 1 recommendation for symptomatic paroxysmal atrial fibrillation in younger patients with few comorbidities, where it outperforms drugs for maintaining sinus rhythm. No rule requires failure of one or two drugs first in this group. Waiting for persistence reduces success rates, and neither drug intolerance nor retirement from sport is a prerequisite.
  135.   136. Escalating diuretics alone leaves the regurgitant lesion untreated. Surgery has not shown a survival benefit for isolated secondary disease, and transplantation evaluation is premature before less invasive options.
  137. The correct answer is D (Obtain computed tomography of the abdomen and pelvis to localize the tumor): The laboratory results shown reveal plasma metanephrine and normetanephrine values roughly five times the upper reference limits under ideal sampling conditions, which is biochemically diagnostic of pheochromocytoma or paraganglioma rather than a borderline result needing confirmation. The next step is cross sectional imaging of the abdomen and pelvis, where most of these tumors arise.   138.   139. Pacing addresses the rhythm but not the abscess that continues destroying tissue. Neither observation nor antibiotic additions treat an established perivalvular collection.
  140. The correct answer is D (Use hydralazine with nitrates and a beta blocker while she remains pregnant): ACE inhibitors, ARBs, ARNI, mineralocorticoid antagonists, and SGLT2 inhibitors are avoided during pregnancy because of fetal toxicity, so afterload reduction is provided with hydralazine and nitrates while a beta blocker such as metoprolol provides neurohormonal protection, and loop diuretics may be added cautiously for congestion. The regimens containing lisinopril, sacubitril valsartan, spironolactone, or an SGLT2 inhibitor each expose the fetus to harm. Withholding all therapy leaves maternal heart failure untreated when safe options exist.
  141. Bed rest and seated confinement promote deconditioning and thrombosis without protecting the heart. Jumping straight back to heavy gardening or unsupervised high intensity intervals skips the graded increase in workload that early recovery calls for.
  142. The correct answer is B (A long acting nitrate, or a second calcium channel blocker from another class): when spasm persists despite a maximized single calcium channel blocker, adding a long acting nitrate or combining dihydropyridine and nondihydropyridine classes is the accepted escalation. Nonselective beta blockade can worsen spasm, aspirin does not suppress vasomotor episodes, and ivabradine has no role in spasm prevention. A loop recorder would document rather than treat episodes already captured with diagnostic changes.
  143. The correct answer is A (Prescribe bumetanide 1 mg daily as the approximate equipotent oral dose): the standard oral equivalence is furosemide 40 mg to bumetanide 1 mg to torsemide 20 mg. Doses of 5, 10, or 40 mg of bumetanide represent five to forty times the intended potency and would cause profound volume and electrolyte depletion. A dose of 0.25 mg supplies only a quarter of his established requirement and would allow congestion to recur.
  144. The correct answer is E (Electrophysiology study with catheter ablation of the accessory pathway): symptomatic preexcitation, especially with near syncope and a safety sensitive occupation, warrants ablation, which is curative in most patients and carries low procedural risk. Verapamil and digoxin can accelerate conduction over the pathway during atrial fibrillation and are avoided in preexcitation.   145. The correct answer is E (Oral prednisone in moderate to high dose, with taper guided by thyroid function): type 2 amiodarone induced thyrotoxicosis is a destructive thyroiditis releasing preformed hormone, so glucocorticoids that quiet the inflammatory process are the established therapy and most patients respond within weeks. Methimazole targets new hormone synthesis, which is the mechanism of type 1 rather than this destructive form. Radioiodine fails because the iodine loaded gland has negligible uptake, and thyroidectomy is reserved for refractory or unstable cases after medical therapy.
  146. The correct answer is D (No reprogramming is needed because cautery far below the device rarely interferes): with surgery below the umbilicus, electromagnetic interference reaching a pectoral generator is minimal, and a patient who is not pacemaker dependent tolerates rare inhibited beats without harm.   147. Routine rivaroxaban after transcatheter implantation increased mortality in a randomized trial. Stopping antithrombotic therapy altogether after one year is not a recommended strategy.
  148.   149. Renal artery imaging and metanephrines target causes his history does not suggest.   150. Loop diuretics have a dose response ceiling per dose, so doubling the dose rather than continuing an ineffective one restores natriuresis. Pausing diuresis or relying on fluid restriction allows congestion to persist, and a thiazide alone lacks the potency to serve as primary decongestive therapy.
  151. Stopping both agents invites stent thrombosis, 45 mg twice daily is not an approved ticagrelor dose, and apixaban does not substitute for antiplatelet therapy after stenting.   152. Good exercise capacity is prognostically favorable but does not exclude obstructive disease. Simple reassurance, blaming his workload, adding blood pressure monitoring, or empiric therapy with delayed retesting would each leave the question unanswered.
  153. The correct answer is E (Start intravenous cangrelor at the time of PCI, transitioning to an oral agent later): cangrelor provides immediate, titratable P2Y12 inhibition when enteral absorption is unreliable, which is exactly the problem in an intubated, shocked patient. Crushed ticagrelor or tube delivered prasugrel still depend on gut absorption and cannot justify delaying an urgent procedure. Aspirin alone underprotects a new stent, and a prolonged glycoprotein IIb IIIa infusion used instead of P2Y12 therapy raises bleeding without solving the absorption problem.
  154. Cardiac MRI characterizes myocardium rather than screening for obstructive disease in this context, and ambulatory monitoring evaluates rhythm complaints he does not have.
  155. The correct answer is B (Add ezetimibe 10 mg daily to the maximally tolerated statin regimen): she is a very high risk patient whose LDL remains at or above the 70 mg/dL threshold on maximal statin therapy, which is the trigger for adding ezetimibe first. A PCSK9 inhibitor is reserved for those still above threshold after ezetimibe, both for cost and stepwise logic. Accepting a value in the eighties undertreats her risk category, switching between maximal statins yields little additional lowering, and niacin adds adverse effects without outcome benefit on statin therapy.
  156. The correct answer is A (Increased venous return raises end diastolic volume and thereby augments stroke volume): leg elevation autotransfuses venous blood toward the thorax, stretching the ventricle to a longer sarcomere length and increasing the force of contraction, which is precisely the Frank Starling relationship and identifies a preload responsive circulation. Baroreceptor tachycardia would not explain a stroke volume rise measured independently of rate. The maneuver does not drain the arterial tree, it lowers rather than raises intrathoracic pressure influence on filling, and a catecholamine surge acts too slowly and nonspecifically.
  157. The correct answer is B (Avoid adaptive servo ventilation because it increases mortality in this population): the SERVE-HF trial found increased cardiovascular and all cause mortality when adaptive servo ventilation was used for predominant central sleep apnea in patients with an ejection fraction of 45 percent or lower, so the mode is contraindicated here. Calling it the preferred mode reverses that evidence. Bilevel ventilation with a backup rate is not established therapy for this indication, acetazolamide remains investigational rather than first line, and tracheostomy has no role; optimizing heart failure therapy and considering alternatives with a sleep specialist is the path.
  158. The correct answer is D (Measure after five minutes supine, then at one and three minutes standing): The standard protocol establishes a true baseline with about five minutes of supine rest, then records pressure and heart rate at one and three minutes upright, since the diagnostic fall must appear within three minutes and early and delayed drops can differ. Starting from a seated position blunts the gravitational stress and misses cases. Standing first, checking only once at thirty seconds, or walking beforehand each distorts the baseline or samples the wrong window.
  159. The correct answer is E (Add low dose spironolactone while monitoring potassium and renal function closely): He meets the definition of true resistant hypertension after adherence, measurement, and secondary cause checks, and randomized evidence from PATHWAY 2 shows spironolactone lowers pressure more than alpha or beta blockade in exactly this situation. His potassium and kidney function permit safe use with surveillance. Hydralazine, clonidine, and doxazosin are later additions, and swapping a long acting thiazide for a short acting loop diuretic usually weakens control in preserved renal function.
  160. The correct answer is C (A toe brachial value below 0.70, the accepted diagnostic threshold for this test): the toe brachial index has a lower normal range than the ankle based measurement because digital pressures run lower than ankle pressures, so a value below 0.70 is the accepted diagnostic threshold. Applying the 0.90 ankle cutoff to toe measurements would overdiagnose disease, while 0.30 describes severe ischemia associated with poor wound healing rather than the diagnostic boundary. An isolated toe pressure is interpreted alongside the ratio, not in place of it.
  161. The correct answer is B (Intravenous amiodarone 300 mg given as a rapid bolus during compressions): amiodarone, or alternatively lidocaine, is the recommended antiarrhythmic for ventricular fibrillation refractory to defibrillation, compressions, and epinephrine. Magnesium is reserved for torsades de pointes rather than routine fibrillation. Procainamide infuses too slowly for arrest, and neither bicarbonate nor calcium is given routinely without a specific indication such as hyperkalemia.
  162. The correct answer is A (Start a nonvasodilating beta blocker such as metoprolol and titrate to symptoms): beta blockade is first line therapy for symptomatic obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy because slowing heart rate lengthens diastolic filling and reduces the dynamic outflow gradient. Vasodilators such as lisinopril and amlodipine drop afterload and worsen the obstruction, which is why they are avoided in this setting. Diuretics reduce preload and can intensify the gradient in a patient without congestion, and digoxin increases contractility, the exact property that aggravates dynamic obstruction.
  163. The correct answer is E (It raises intrathoracic pressure, further reducing venous return and cardiac output): tamponade already limits diastolic filling, and the patient depends on spontaneous inspiratory negative pressure to draw blood into the thorax, so positive pressure breaths compress the vena cavae and can cause immediate circulatory collapse, compounded by preload lowering induction agents. Alkalosis induced arrhythmia is not the central mechanism. Ventilation does not rupture the pericardium, does not relocate fluid beyond needle access, and does not primarily injure the aortic valve.
  164. The correct answer is E (Give a second adenosine bolus at 12 mg with a rapid flush): when an initial 6 mg dose fails, guideline based practice is to escalate to 12 mg rather than repeat the same dose. Verapamil is a reasonable later option but is not preferred before completing the adenosine sequence in a stable patient. Cardioversion and amiodarone loading are unnecessary while the patient remains hemodynamically stable.
  165. The correct answer is A (Repeat echocardiography approximately every three months during the trastuzumab course): trastuzumab related ventricular dysfunction is usually asymptomatic, often reversible, and most treatable when found early, so ejection fraction is reassessed about every three months throughout therapy. A single end of treatment study or a two year interval would discover dysfunction only after months of continued exposure. Electrocardiograms do not measure ejection fraction, and natriuretic peptides alone have not replaced imaging based surveillance in this setting.
  166. The correct answer is D (Urgent synchronized cardioversion after anticoagulation is initiated without delay): hemodynamic instability from rapid atrial fibrillation calls for immediate electrical cardioversion, and a clear onset within 48 hours does not remove the need to start anticoagulation. Diltiazem depresses contractility and can worsen hypotension with a reduced ejection fraction. Digoxin and amiodarone act too slowly to rescue a decompensating patient.
  167. The correct answer is D (Intravenous benzodiazepines with nitroglycerin for blood pressure and pain control): benzodiazepines reduce the central sympathetic drive of cocaine toxicity while nitroglycerin relieves vasoconstriction, and together they treat both hemodynamics and ischemia. Beta blockade in the acute phase, including combined alpha and beta agents, is traditionally avoided because unopposed alpha effects can worsen coronary vasoconstriction. Antipsychotics do not address ischemia and can lower the seizure threshold, and catheterization is not the first move in a patient without ST elevation whose vasospastic component may respond to medical therapy.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Continue anticoagulation with periodic reassessment, favoring extended therapy given her unprovoked event): recurrence after an unprovoked pulmonary embolism approaches ten percent in the first year off therapy and continues accruing, so guidelines favor extended anticoagulation when bleeding risk is low, often at a reduced apixaban dose, with the decision revisited regularly. A fixed three month course is appropriate for events with major transient provocation, which hers lacked. Aspirin lowers recurrence far less than anticoagulation, intermittent situational dosing has no evidence base, and switching to injections adds burden without advantage.
  2. The correct answer is B (Invasive angiography within 24 hours based on a rising troponin trajectory): a clearly rising high sensitivity troponin establishes NSTEMI, which favors an early invasive strategy within 24 hours in a stabilized patient. Immediate two hour catheterization is reserved for refractory pain, instability, or arrhythmia, none of which she has. Ischemia guided therapy, outpatient treadmill testing, and coronary CT angiography each understage a biomarker positive event that already merits invasive evaluation.
  3. The correct answer is A (Perform right heart catheterization to define filling pressures and cardiac output): invasive hemodynamic assessment is indicated when volume status and perfusion remain uncertain despite therapy, when renal function worsens without a clear cause, and when advanced therapy candidacy is being evaluated. Serial natriuretic peptides and repeated echocardiograms provide indirect estimates that have already failed to resolve the question. Chest CT quantifies edema without explaining hemodynamics, and renal biopsy is not the evaluation for cardiorenal deterioration in decompensated heart failure.
  4. The correct answer is B (Restrict competitive sport for three months, then reassess symptoms, markers, and testing): athletes are held from competitive exercise for approximately three months from onset, with return contingent on resolved symptoms and normalized CRP, ECG, and echocardiogram, because exertion during active inflammation may worsen disease. Immediate return or a two week limit is far too short for an athlete. A fixed twelve month restriction exceeds the recommendation for uncomplicated cases, and disappearance of the rub alone is an unreliable clearance endpoint.
  5. The correct answer is E (Effective pericardial drainage, by catheter or surgery, combined with intravenous antibiotics): purulent pericarditis behaves like an abscess, so antibiotics alone rarely sterilize the space, and definitive drainage, sometimes with intrapericardial fibrinolysis or surgical washout for loculations, is essential given mortality approaching 40 percent even when treated. Watchful waiting with serial imaging risks rapid tamponade and constriction. Early oral step down is inadequate for this infection, cytology addresses malignancy rather than infection, and corticosteroids do not substitute for source control.
  6. The correct answer is D (An SGLT2 inhibitor, which lowers blood pressure only modestly in most patients): in a patient with borderline blood pressure who tolerates her regimen without symptoms, an SGLT2 inhibitor is an attractive next pillar because its average blood pressure effect is small. An MRA is also indicated eventually, but starting it at full dose with a three month laboratory delay is unsafe practice for potassium surveillance. Withholding all further therapy because of an asymptomatic pressure below 100 forfeits mortality benefit, hydralazine is not a foundational class here, and ivabradine comes only after maximally tolerated beta blockade.
  7. The correct answer is B (Reprogram shock therapies off and pacing to an asynchronous mode beforehand): in a pacemaker dependent patient, a magnet over a defibrillator suspends shock detection but does not change the pacing mode, so electrocautery near the generator could still inhibit pacing unless the device is reprogrammed. A magnet alone therefore leaves the real hazard unaddressed. Full deactivation with rescue pads is far less reliable than programmed pacing, making no changes risks inhibition, and repositioning the generator is unwarranted.
  8. The correct answer is A (Surgical valve replacement with concomitant ascending aortic replacement during one operation): a bicuspid valve requiring replacement with an ascending aorta of 4.5 cm or more warrants combined surgical valve and aortic replacement at the time of surgery, even though 5.1 cm alone would not yet meet the higher 5.5 cm standalone aneurysm-repair threshold used when no valve surgery is otherwise planned. Transcatheter therapy leaves the aneurysm unaddressed, and isolated valve surgery wastes the safest moment to repair the aorta. Waiting for a threshold ignores that the criterion is already met.
  9. The correct answer is A (Refer for aortic valve replacement after multidisciplinary heart team review): severe aortic stenosis with an ejection fraction below 50 percent is a class 1 indication for replacement even without symptoms, and the exhibit shows both severe valve hemodynamics and a fraction of 45 percent. Surveillance or heart failure medication leaves the obstructing valve untreated while the ventricle declines further. Exercise testing adds nothing once ventricular dysfunction already mandates replacement.
  10. The correct answer is E (Continue aspirin with ticagrelor reduced to 60 mg twice daily, reassessing bleeding regularly): in a diabetic patient with prior infarction who tolerated a year of dual therapy without bleeding, extended therapy using the lower ticagrelor dose reduces recurrent ischemic events at acceptable bleeding cost. Stopping the P2Y12 inhibitor outright forgoes that benefit in a high ischemic risk patient. Lifelong full dose ticagrelor and an open ended decade of clopidogrel overtreat without scheduled reassessment, and warfarin is not a substitute for antiplatelet therapy in this setting.
  11. The correct answer is B (Refer for valve surgery because the infection is not being controlled): persistent bacteremia beyond five to seven days of appropriate antibiotics defines uncontrolled infection, an accepted indication for surgery once other sources are excluded. Prolonging or rearranging a failing regimen leaves infected tissue that antibiotics are failing to sterilize. Rifampin is reserved for prosthetic valve staphylococcal disease, and discharge on identical therapy ignores the documented treatment failure.
  12. The correct answer is C (Continue rate control with metoprolol and apixaban without pursuing rhythm restoration): an asymptomatic older patient with years of persistent atrial fibrillation and a well controlled rate gains little from rhythm control, and success rates fall with longer arrhythmia duration. Ablation would not permit stopping anticoagulation at his stroke risk. Amiodarone carries toxicity without clear benefit here, tighter rate targets add no outcome advantage, and cardioversion is unlikely to yield durable sinus rhythm.
  13. The correct answer is D (Begin sacubitril valsartan at the next scheduled dose without any washout interval): the 36 hour washout applies only when converting from an ACE inhibitor, because angioedema risk arises from combining neprilysin inhibition with ACE inhibition. An ARB does not raise bradykinin, so the switch can occur at the next dose. Adding a washout, a lisinopril bridge, or a prolonged taper interrupts or complicates effective therapy without any safety gain, and a week without renin angiotensin blockade needlessly withdraws treatment.
  14. The correct answer is E (Proceed to early valve surgery during this initial hospitalization): valve dysfunction causing heart failure is the strongest indication for early surgery in endocarditis, and outcomes worsen when the operation is delayed for antibiotic completion. An intra aortic balloon pump is contraindicated in severe aortic regurgitation because diastolic inflation worsens the leak. Medical stabilization fails with a structurally destroyed valve, and transcatheter implantation is not performed into active infection.
  15. The correct answer is A (Optimize guideline directed medical therapy first and reassess symptoms before considering intervention): a well collateralized occlusion in a minimally symptomatic patient on no antianginal therapy should first receive guideline directed medical therapy with reassessment, because occlusion intervention improves symptoms rather than survival. Proceeding to retrograde intervention or booking a staged procedure presumes failed medical therapy that has not yet been tried. Surgery is not required simply because a vessel is occluded, and revascularizing nonviable territory offers no benefit.
  16. The correct answer is C (Apixaban with dose selection guided by his age, weight, and creatinine): apixaban has the strongest data among direct oral anticoagulants at low creatinine clearance and remains usable at this level of kidney disease. Dabigatran is the most renally cleared agent and is avoided below a clearance of 30 mL/min. Rivaroxaban would require reduction to 15 mg at this clearance, indefinite enoxaparin injections are impractical, and dual antiplatelet therapy is inferior for stroke prevention.
  17. The correct answer is D (Ivabradine to lower the heart rate and reduce heart failure hospitalization): ivabradine is indicated for symptomatic HFrEF patients in sinus rhythm with a resting rate of 70 beats per minute or higher despite maximally tolerated beta blockade, where it reduced heart failure hospitalization in the SHIFT trial. Diltiazem is a negative inotrope that worsens outcomes in reduced ejection fraction. Stacking a second beta blocker compounds the hypotension that already limits him, rapid digoxin loading is neither needed nor a proven strategy in sinus rhythm, and elevated resting heart rate is in fact associated with worse outcomes.
  18. The correct answer is D (Ischemia guided medical therapy aligned with her goals, reserving angiography for refractory symptoms): an invasive strategy is chosen when its benefit outweighs risk and matches patient goals, and comfort focused care with severe comorbidity favors medical management. Reflexive catheterization or routine early angiography ignores both prognosis and stated preferences. Tertiary transfer escalates rather than aligns care, and stress testing adds little when the decision not to revascularize has effectively been made by goals of care.
  19. The correct answer is C (Evaluate for heart transplantation or durable left ventricular assist device candidacy): inotrope dependence defines advanced heart failure, and a patient with preserved end organ function and surgical candidacy should undergo prompt structured evaluation for transplant or durable mechanical support while those options remain feasible. Indefinite home milrinone without evaluation carries high mortality and forfeits the assessment window. Hospice is appropriate only after advanced therapies are declined or excluded, oral vasodilators cannot replace inotropic support in this physiology, and serial angiography contributes nothing to this decision.
  20. The correct answer is D (Add oral metolazone given about 30 minutes before the intravenous loop dose): distal tubular hypertrophy from chronic loop exposure drives diuretic resistance, and adding a thiazide type agent before the loop dose produces sequential nephron blockade with restored natriuresis. Pausing the loop diuretic or observing another day allows congestion and renal venous pressure to worsen. Spironolactone alone is far too weak a natriuretic to replace loop therapy, and acetazolamide has been studied as an addition to loop diuretics rather than a substitute for them.
  21. The correct answer is D (Low to moderate dose prednisone with a very gradual taper after symptom resolution): guidelines advise 0.2 to 0.5 mg per kilogram per day of prednisone, maintained until symptoms resolve and CRP normalizes, followed by a slow decremental taper, because high doses and rapid withdrawal both increase recurrence. Pulse therapy with abrupt cessation and stopping as soon as pain improves invite relapse. Depot injections triggered by recurrences provide erratic exposure, and unchanged high dose therapy for six months maximizes toxicity without added benefit.
  22. The correct answer is B (Stop aspirin within the first week, continuing apixaban and clopidogrel for 12 months): after PCI in a patient with atrial fibrillation, the default is early aspirin withdrawal with a direct oral anticoagulant plus clopidogrel, which cuts bleeding without a clear rise in stent thrombosis. Extended triple therapy for six or twelve months multiplies bleeding risk in a patient whose score already predicts it. Dropping anticoagulation leaves his stroke risk untreated, and switching to warfarin adds monitoring burden with worse outcomes than a direct agent.
  23. The correct answer is B (Proceed with closure given the significant shunt causing left sided chamber enlargement): A ventricular level shunt loads the left heart, and her Qp:Qs of 1.7 with left atrial and ventricular enlargement plus symptoms meets guideline criteria for closure when pulmonary pressures remain acceptable. Waiting for endocarditis or measurable exercise decline accepts avoidable risk. Vasodilator therapy addresses pulmonary vascular disease she does not have.
  24. The correct answer is E (High intensity statin therapy, because peripheral artery disease is an atherosclerotic risk equivalent): peripheral artery disease is clinical atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease, which places her in the highest risk category and calls for high intensity statin therapy regardless of baseline cholesterol. Moderate intensity dosing undertreats established vascular disease, and claudication severity does not guide statin intensity. Ezetimibe and fibrates are adjuncts rather than foundations of therapy, and delaying medication for a dietary trial leaves a high risk patient unprotected during that interval.
  25. The correct answer is B (In approximately twelve months, the surveillance interval for aneurysms of four to five centimeters): aneurysms measuring 4.0 to 4.9 centimeters warrant ultrasound surveillance at roughly twelve month intervals because their expansion rate and rupture risk sit between small and near threshold lesions. A three year interval applies to aneurysms of 3.0 to 3.9 centimeters, while six month imaging is used once the sac reaches 5.0 centimeters. Immediate reimaging is unnecessary for a technically adequate study, and abandoning surveillance would miss silent growth toward the repair threshold.
  26. The correct answer is B (Refer promptly for angiography and revascularization planning to achieve limb salvage): rest pain worse when supine, a nonhealing toe ulcer, absent pedal pulses, and an ankle brachial index of 0.42 together define chronic limb threatening ischemia, which requires prompt revascularization to prevent limb loss. Supervised exercise and cilostazol are claudication treatments and cannot heal an ischemic ulcer or relieve rest pain of this severity. Antibiotics without restored perfusion do not close ischemic wounds, and the positional, perfusion dependent pain pattern argues against a spinal cause.
  27. The correct answer is C (Optimization of guideline directed medical therapy supervised by a heart failure specialist): secondary regurgitation is a disease of the ventricle, so the first step is maximally tolerated guideline directed medical therapy, which by itself reduces regurgitation severity in many patients. Transcatheter or surgical intervention is considered only when severe regurgitation and symptoms persist despite full optimization. Resynchronization therapy requires its own electrical indication that is not described here.
  28. The correct answer is D (Beta blocker therapy with avoidance of extreme exertion and heavy isometric straining): observational data associate beta blockade with lower dissection recurrence, and counseling targets the shear stresses of extreme exertion, heavy straining, and severe hypertension. Statins address atherosclerosis, which is not the mechanism of her arteriopathy, and lifelong dual antiplatelet therapy adds bleeding without evidence of benefit here. Surveillance by repeated invasive angiography and prophylactic stenting both expose fragile arteries to instrumentation risk.
  29. The correct answer is C (Intravenous labetalol, titrated to reach a safer range within the hour): Severe range pressures in preeclampsia are treated promptly with intravenous labetalol, intravenous hydralazine, or immediate release oral nifedipine, all with established fetal safety records. Renin angiotensin blockers are contraindicated in pregnancy because they injure the fetal kidneys. Nitroprusside risks fetal cyanide exposure and is a last resort, atenolol is associated with fetal growth restriction, and a long acting oral diuretic neither works fast enough nor suits the reduced plasma volume of preeclampsia.
  30. The correct answer is C (Pursue rhythm control, with catheter ablation as a first line option): symptomatic atrial fibrillation with a newly reduced ejection fraction favors early rhythm control, and trial data show ablation improves survival and ventricular function in heart failure. Escalating rate control leaves a likely tachycardia mediated cardiomyopathy untreated. Flecainide is avoided with structural heart disease, and switching anticoagulants is not required before rhythm control.
  31. The correct answer is D (Transfer emergently for primary PCI while continuing intravenous blood pressure control): severe hypertension that stays above 180/110 mmHg despite treatment markedly raises intracranial hemorrhage risk with lytics, so PCI is preferred even beyond the usual 120 minute window. Giving full or reduced dose tenecteplase at this pressure accepts avoidable bleeding risk, and the target for lysis is below 180/110 rather than 200/120. Holding all reperfusion until normalization simply prolongs infarction.
  32.   53.   54. The correct answer is A (Physical counterpressure maneuvers such as leg crossing with sustained muscle tensing): Leg crossing, buttock and thigh tensing, and handgrip squeeze compress venous capacitance beds and raise peripheral resistance, boosting blood pressure enough to abort a reflex faint when started at the prodrome, and randomized data support teaching them to patients with recognizable warning symptoms. The correct answer is B (Discontinue the vasodilating agents because they can worsen the outflow obstruction): dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers and nitrates reduce afterload and preload, which intensifies dynamic outflow obstruction and explains his exertional lightheadedness. A thiazide lowers preload further, and tamsulosin adds alpha blockade with more vasodilation, both moves in the wrong direction; his hypertension should be managed with a nonvasodilating beta blocker or verapamil instead.
  101. The correct answer is D (Surgical mitral valve intervention because significant regurgitation precludes balloon commissurotomy): moderate to severe mitral regurgitation contraindicates balloon commissurotomy because splitting the commissures reliably worsens the leak, so a symptomatic patient needs surgical treatment. Edge to edge repair does not treat the stenotic rheumatic apparatus, and medical therapy leaves a mechanical problem in place. A staged percutaneous strategy begins with a procedure that is itself contraindicated.
  102. The correct answer is C (An embolic protection device deployed distal to the lesion during the intervention): distal embolic protection during vein graft intervention reduces periprocedural infarction from friable atheroembolic debris and is the specifically supported adjunct. Rotational atherectomy is avoided in grafts because it liberates debris, routine aspiration lacks supporting evidence in this setting, stent oversizing raises rupture and embolization risk, and prophylactic fibrinolysis is not a recognized strategy. Protection devices are the one adjunct with trial demonstrated infarct reduction.
  103. The correct answer is B (Amoxicillin 2 g orally thirty to sixty minutes before the procedure): a prosthetic valve of any type places a patient in the high risk category for whom prophylaxis is recommended before dental procedures that manipulate gingival tissue, and the standard regimen is amoxicillin 2 g orally 30 to 60 minutes beforehand. Multiday courses add adverse effects without added protection. Vancomycin is not a first line prophylactic agent, and rinses do not substitute for systemic prophylaxis.
  104. The correct answer is D (High dose aspirin plus colchicine, avoiding other NSAIDs early after infarction): aspirin is the preferred antiinflammatory after myocardial infarction because it is already indicated and does not impair scar formation the way other NSAIDs may, and colchicine reduces recurrence. Indomethacin is specifically discouraged early after infarction owing to concerns about coronary flow and healing. Anticoagulation adds hemorrhagic risk with an effusion, angiography is unnecessary for a classic inflammatory syndrome without ischemic features, and corticosteroids are second line.
  105. The correct answer is E (Prophylactic dose fondaparinux daily for 45 days to prevent thrombotic extension): superficial vein thrombosis at least 5 centimeters long, sitting more than 3 centimeters from the saphenofemoral junction, is treated with prophylactic dose fondaparinux 2.5 milligrams daily for 45 days, which reduced extension and embolic events in the CALISTO trial. Symptomatic care alone leaves a meaningful risk of propagation for a thrombus of this length. Full therapeutic anticoagulation is reserved for junctional proximity or deep involvement, surgical ligation is not first line therapy, and antibiotics treat septic phlebitis rather than bland thrombosis.
  106. Antiplatelet regimens are inferior for stroke prevention in atrial fibrillation. Withholding therapy ignores an established indication, and warfarin is a second choice with intact renal function.
  107.   109.   110. The correct answer is B (Higher end systolic pressure with reduced stroke volume and increased end systolic volume): with a fixed end systolic pressure volume relationship, raising afterload forces the ventricle to generate more pressure and cease ejection earlier, so the loop grows taller and narrower with more blood left at end systole. Lower peak pressure contradicts the imposed resistance. A simultaneously taller and wider loop would require added preload or contractility, end diastolic volume was deliberately held constant, and a preload reduction pattern describes a different intervention.
  111.   112.   166. The correct answer is D (Give full dose fibrinolysis within thirty minutes and then transfer for angiography): When anticipated first medical contact to device time exceeds 120 minutes, guidelines direct fibrinolysis within thirty minutes of arrival for an early presenting infarction without contraindications, followed by routine transfer for angiography within twenty four hours. Anticoagulation alone is not a reperfusion strategy, arbitrary half dosing to hedge bleeding is unsupported in a patient without contraindications, and waiting on uncertain weather gambles myocardium against a forecast.
  167. Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Exercise raises demand across a critical proximal stenosis and can precipitate complete occlusion): the Wellens pattern marks a critical proximal left anterior descending stenosis, and exercise raises oxygen demand and shear across that lesion, which can convert it to complete occlusion and anterior infarction during the test. Electrode placement does not hide anterior ischemia, the syndrome is not myocarditis, medication withdrawal is not the described mechanism, and exercise hyperkalemia is not the recognized hazard. Such patients should proceed to angiography instead of any stress modality.
  2. Delaying until further systolic decline risks irreversible right ventricular injury. Diuretics and training programs do not correct the volume load, and transplantation is far beyond what this stage requires.
  67. The correct answer is E (As a combined lesion whose total load approaches severe disease, warranting intervention given his symptoms): mixed moderate stenosis and moderate regurgitation impose simultaneous pressure and volume overload, and the elevated transvalvular velocity reflects that summed burden, so a symptomatic patient with these values is managed like severe disease. Treating each lesion in isolation misses their additive physiology. The lesions coexist commonly in calcific and rheumatic disease, and dobutamine protocols are designed for low flow, low gradient states rather than a gradient already near severe.
  68. The correct answer is B (Optimize guideline directed heart failure therapy, since the valve is not truly severe): the valve area opening to 1.4 cm² with only a modest gradient rise identifies pseudo severe stenosis, where a weak ventricle underopens a moderately diseased valve. The dominant problem is the cardiomyopathy, so medical therapy is the treatment. Valve replacement or valvuloplasty would expose the patient to procedural risk without addressing the failing ventricle, and calcium scoring is reserved for cases the dobutamine study leaves unresolved.
  69. The correct answer is C (Mexiletine to inhibit the pathologic late sodium current prolonging his repolarization): type 3 long QT syndrome is caused by gain of function in the cardiac sodium channel with a persistent late inward current, and mexiletine directly blocks that current, often shortening the QT interval substantially in this genotype. Sotalol adds class III potassium blockade that lengthens repolarization further. Verapamil and digoxin do not address the sodium channel defect, and replacing beta blockade with flecainide alone abandons proven protective therapy.
  70. The correct answer is D (Loop agents show a threshold dose response, so an ineffective dose must be increased): a dose that fails to reach the natriuretic threshold produces little urine no matter how many times it is repeated, so the correct move is doubling the dose until a ceiling response appears. Repetition does not create accumulation strong enough to cross the threshold. Smaller divided doses fall further below threshold, and neither infusion speed alone nor potassium repletion substitutes for adequate tubular drug delivery.
  71. The correct answer is D (Manage the rhythm as ventricular tachycardia using procainamide or synchronized cardioversion): guidelines direct that an undifferentiated wide complex tachycardia be treated as ventricular tachycardia because it is the most common cause and the most dangerous to misjudge. Verapamil given during ventricular tachycardia can cause hemodynamic collapse through vasodilation and negative inotropy. Watchful waiting and outpatient beta blockade leave a dangerous rhythm untreated, and digoxin loading does nothing for a ventricular mechanism.
  72. The correct answer is B (Apixaban plus clopidogrel, dropping aspirin early to reduce major bleeding events): multiple randomized trials showed that a direct oral anticoagulant with a P2Y12 inhibitor, discontinuing aspirin within about a week, substantially cuts bleeding without a significant increase in stent thrombosis. Prolonged triple therapy roughly doubles major bleeding. Anticoagulation alone does not adequately protect a fresh stent, and dual antiplatelet therapy without anticoagulation leaves cardioembolic stroke risk unaddressed.
  73. The correct answer is D (It is a sensitive but nonspecific sign, reflecting raised right atrial pressure broadly): a dilated noncollapsing cava is present in nearly all significant tamponade, so its absence argues strongly against the diagnosis, yet it also occurs with right heart failure, tricuspid disease, pulmonary hypertension, and volume overload, so alone it cannot confirm compression. It therefore neither mandates immediate drainage nor excludes tamponade in the opposite direction. Caval size says nothing about fluid chemistry, and it does not localize where an effusion sits.
  74. The correct answer is D (Ventricular interdependence from a fixed pericardial volume shifting the septum with respiration): the rigid pericardium fixes total cardiac volume, so inspiratory augmentation of right heart filling can only be accommodated by displacing the septum leftward, and expiration reverses the shift, producing the bounce, while dissociation of intrathoracic from intracardiac pressures exaggerates the effect. Bronchospasm does not push directly on the septum. Conduction alternans is an electrical curiosity unrelated to respiration, fluid shifts require an effusion, and respirophasic coronary stunning is not a recognized mechanism.
  75. The correct answer is B (An eccentric anteriorly directed jet streams into the right superior pulmonary vein, raising pressure regionally): a flail posterior leaflet hurls its jet anteriorly and toward the right superior pulmonary vein, so hydrostatic pressure rises selectively in that venous territory and edema appears unilaterally, classically mimicking pneumonia. Baseline regional flow differences do not create lobar edema. Lobar collapse is not part of this physiology, lymphatic capacity fails diffusely rather than lobe by lobe, and flail leaflets cause regurgitation rather than intermittent inflow obstruction.
  76. The correct answer is E (A valve attributable fall in ejection fraction marks ventricular injury, an indication without symptoms): severe stenosis with ejection fraction below 50 percent, absent another cause, is a class one indication because the ventricle is already failing under the pressure load and recovery becomes less complete the longer replacement waits. Velocity alone in a truly asymptomatic patient with a normal ventricle is handled differently. Exercise testing clarifies equivocal symptoms rather than overriding ventricular dysfunction, waiting for a 35 percent threshold sacrifices recoverable myocardium, and endocarditis prevention is not the rationale.
  77. The correct answer is B (Arm exercise lowers subclavian resistance, augmenting flow reversal away from the brainstem): with a proximal subclavian stenosis, the arm fills partly through retrograde vertebral flow, and exercise dilates the arm's muscular bed, dropping distal resistance and pulling even more blood down the vertebral artery away from the basilar circulation. That demand dependent steal explains symptoms confined to arm activity. Basilar vasospasm and exercise triggered embolization would not track arm use so reproducibly, carotid compression does not produce a brachial pressure gradient, and raised cardiac output does not divert posterior circulation flow.
  78. The correct answer is A (Elective repair is indicated now, because his diameter exceeds the male threshold): at 5.7 centimeters his aneurysm has passed the 5.5 centimeter elective repair threshold for men, where annual rupture risk begins to exceed the mortality of planned repair in acceptable risk patients. Waiting for 6.0 centimeters or for symptoms invites rupture, which is fatal in most patients who reach the hospital. Surveillance intervals apply below the threshold rather than above it, and no medical therapy shrinks an established aneurysm of this size.
  79. The correct answer is E (Fibrinolysis can extend an intramural hematoma and worsen a spontaneous dissection): A young postpartum woman has a high pretest probability of spontaneous coronary artery dissection, where lytic therapy promotes bleeding into the vessel wall, extends the dissection, and can convert a patent artery into complete occlusion, so angiographic definition of the mechanism should come first. Claims that fibrinolysis fails in young women, or that postpartum physiology clears the drug too fast, have no pharmacologic basis. Anterior infarctions actually derive large benefit from timely reperfusion, and a two hour transfer normally favors giving lytics rather than withholding them, which is why the dissection concern is the decisive factor.

Troponin thresholds cannot exclude occlusion.   82. The correct answer is B (The loop shifts leftward with a smaller end systolic volume and larger stroke volume): positive inotropy steepens the end systolic pressure volume relationship, allowing the ventricle to eject to a lower end systolic volume from the same end diastolic volume, which widens the loop leftward and raises stroke volume and ejection fraction. A downward rotation of that line describes depressed contractility. Contractility acts independently of afterload changes, a rightward end diastolic expansion reflects added preload, and reduced systolic pressure with unchanged ejection matches neither prediction.
  83. The correct answer is E (Postpone the elective operation until at least six months after stent placement): Purely elective surgery should optimally wait six months after drug eluting stent implantation so the P2Y12 inhibitor can be interrupted at much lower thrombotic risk, and a knee replacement loses nothing by waiting. Operating next month on both agents invites major orthopedic bleeding, while stopping both with a heparin bridge leaves an unhealed stent unprotected because heparin does not inhibit platelets. A stress test does not address stent healing at all, and permanent cancellation denies a beneficial operation that only needs correct timing.

Outpatient monitoring is suitable for low risk presentations. Thrombosis favors early timing, anticoagulation gaps, and a mobile low density mass. Continuing an intolerable regimen unchanged invites nonadherence that ends all benefit. Permanent abandonment for reversible symptoms sacrifices proven secondary prevention, red yeast rice contains an unregulated statin like compound with the same muscle effects, and coenzyme Q10 has failed placebo controlled trials for this indication.

  100. Biliary ultrasound and colonoscopy investigate the wrong organs for this presentation, and plain radiographs are insensitive early, becoming abnormal mainly after infarction has occurred.
  101. The correct answer is D (This is alcohol triggered atrial fibrillation, and continued heavy use risks progression toward cardiomyopathy): the holiday heart pattern describes atrial fibrillation provoked by binge drinking in structurally normal hearts, and sustained heavy consumption can progress to a dilated alcoholic cardiomyopathy, so reduction or abstinence is the central intervention and has been shown to cut recurrence. Proceeding straight to ablation ignores a reversible trigger. Ethanol is directly arrhythmogenic through autonomic and electrophysiologic effects, and converting to daily drinking deepens rather than removes the risk.

  103. Insertion time and access logistics are secondary practical matters rather than the physiologic contraindication.   108. The correct answer is C (Modest creatinine shifts during decongestion rarely justify withdrawing therapies that lengthen survival): small expected creatinine changes reflect hemodynamic adaptation and hemoconcentration, while reflexive discontinuation of disease modifying therapy is associated with worse long term outcomes and often never gets corrected after discharge. These drugs do not become protective at higher levels, and they do not acutely improve creatinine. Doubling doses to compensate has no basis, and potassium binders address a different problem than the one at hand.
  109. The correct answer is D (Low dose dobutamine echocardiography to test whether the gradient rises as flow increases): a weak ventricle may fail to generate a high gradient across a truly severe valve, and augmenting flow separates true severe stenosis, where the gradient rises while area stays small, from pseudosevere disease, where the area opens up. Anatomy review and wedge measurement do not resolve flow dependence. Waiting or treadmill testing in suspected severe stenosis with dysfunction delays a decision the stress study can make now.
  110.   111. The correct answer is D (Treat the fever aggressively with antipyretics and seek monitoring if it persists): fever accentuates the sodium channel dysfunction of Brugada syndrome and is a well documented trigger of ventricular fibrillation, so prompt antipyretic therapy and a low threshold for monitored observation are the core recommendations. Flecainide is a sodium channel blocker that can unmask or worsen the Brugada pattern and is used diagnostically, not therapeutically. Beta blockers do not protect in this disease, and an electrophysiology study does not address the immediate febrile risk.
  112. The correct answer is A (Average measurements across several consecutive cycles and weigh the flow independent dimensionless index): irregular cycle lengths change stroke volume beat to beat, so gradients must be averaged over multiple cycles, and the dimensionless index below 0.25 adds a ratio that is relatively insensitive to flow, together pointing to severe stenosis. Cherry picking the highest or lowest single beat builds bias in either direction. Cardioversion may fail or be inappropriate and is not a prerequisite for valid assessment, and plain radiography cannot quantify severity.
  113. The correct answer is E (Hypotension with oliguria and granular casts, showing hypoperfusion and tubular damage together): hemoconcentration occurs in patients who are clinically improving, whereas falling pressure, somnolence, near anuria, and muddy brown casts demonstrate acute tubular injury from hypoperfusion. No single creatinine cutoff separates the two states, and the urea to creatinine ratio is confounded by diuretics themselves. Weight loss and diuretic dose describe therapy intensity, not the kidney's condition.
  114. The correct answer is E (Negotiate staged withdrawal, if he insists, with scheduled imaging, biomarkers, and rapid reinstitution criteria): when a fully informed patient declines maintenance therapy, the safest achievable plan mirrors trial methodology, removing one agent at a time with surveillance echocardiography, natriuretic peptides, and predefined triggers to restart. Abandoning him or issuing ultimatums forfeits the chance to catch early relapse. Recovered ventricles that relapse frequently improve again with prompt reinstitution, so claiming universal irreversibility misrepresents the evidence.
  115.   116.   117. The correct answer is B (Vasodilators alone trigger reflex tachycardia that raises aortic wall shear stress): Dropping arterial pressure with a pure vasodilator activates the baroreflex, and the resulting tachycardia and increased contractility steepen the rate of pressure rise with each ejection, the very force that propagates a dissection flap. Establishing beta blockade first blunts that reflex so pressure can then be lowered without increasing shear. Vasodilators do lower systolic pressure regardless of rate, cyanide accumulation relates to nitroprusside dose and duration rather than beta blockade, and stroke volume augmentation is neither true nor desirable here.
  118. The correct answer is E (Measuring lipoprotein(a) once in first degree relatives, since the trait is strongly heritable): lipoprotein(a) is among the most heritable cardiovascular traits, so a single measurement in first degree relatives is recommended to identify carriers early. The children need testing before any therapy is considered, waiting for symptoms forfeits the purpose of screening, and calcium scanning in teenagers is neither sensitive nor appropriate for detecting this inherited trait. Reassurance would misrepresent an autosomal codominant condition.
  119. The correct answer is D (The posteromedial muscle usually depends on a single arterial supply from the posterior descending artery): the posteromedial papillary muscle typically has solitary perfusion from the posterior descending artery, so an inferior occlusion can infarct it completely, whereas the anterolateral muscle enjoys dual supply from diagonal and circumflex branches. Apex proximity and wall stress are not the operative factors. Inferior infarcts are not intrinsically more transmural, chordal caliber does not determine rupture site, and right ventricular branches are not the muscle's principal source.

The correct answer is D (Proceed with surgery, continue aspirin, and restart the second agent quickly afterward): A perforated viscus cannot wait, and aspirin continuation with early resumption of the P2Y12 inhibitor is the accepted compromise for surgery in the highest thrombotic window after stenting. Refusing or deferring an emergency operation risks death from sepsis, prophylactic platelet transfusion does not substitute for a coherent antiplatelet plan, and a three day delay for an intravenous agent misjudges the surgical urgency.
  121. Revascularizing dead muscle releases potassium, myoglobin, and acid, producing hyperkalemia, renal failure, and cardiovascular collapse, so embolectomy and thrombolysis are withheld and amputation is planned once she is stabilized. Anticoagulation alone and vasodilators address neither the nonviable tissue nor the systemic threat it poses.
  122. Tethering from ischemia requires a remodeled ventricle, which she lacks. Prolapse and rheumatic fusion are excluded by the described anatomy, and there is no evidence of infection or perforation.
  123.   124. The correct answer is A (Record posterior chest leads to evaluate for a circumflex territory occlusion): Right precordial ST depression with prominent R waves and upright T waves is the mirror image of posterior wall injury, and posterior leads V7 through V9 confirm it within minutes at no risk. Managing this as a routine non ST elevation syndrome with next day angiography leaves an occluded artery untreated overnight. Perfusion imaging and a six hour repeat tracing both introduce unacceptable delay, and fibrinolysis should not be given before the diagnosis is established by the posterior leads.
  125. The correct answer is A (Radiation injury affects pericardium, valves, coronaries, conduction tissue, and myocardium, often decades later): mediastinal radiation produces a panoramic late cardiotoxicity including constrictive pericarditis, ostial and proximal coronary stenoses, aortic and mitral thickening, conduction disease, and restrictive myopathy, typically surfacing ten to thirty years after exposure, so her workup should screen several territories rather than one. Limiting concern to the pericardium or to the first post treatment year misses most of the disease burden.   126. Waiting for symptoms forfeits the window before irreversible injury.
