  127. The correct answer is C (Mediastinal fibrosis, damaged internal mammary conduits, and concomitant valve disease raise surgical risk): prior mediastinal radiation causes fibrosis of surgical planes, can damage the internal mammary artery within the treated field, and frequently produces concomitant valvular and pericardial disease, all of which raise surgical risk and often shift left main strategy toward percutaneous treatment. Laryngeal fibrosis and hemostasis preventing thrombocytopenia are not characteristic late effects. Stents remain a viable option in irradiated arteries, and constrictive physiology does not improve simply because a sternotomy is performed.
  128. The correct answer is B (Triptans cause vasoconstriction and can provoke coronary spasm, so alternatives are preferred): triptans are serotonin receptor agonists that constrict coronary arteries and are contraindicated in vasospastic angina because they can trigger spasm and infarction. The receptor systems are related rather than independent, spacing doses around diltiazem does not remove the mechanism, the warning covers oral as well as injectable forms, and background calcium channel blockade cannot be assumed protective. Nonvasoconstrictive migraine options such as gepants are preferable for her.
  129. The correct answer is C (Careful intravenous vasodilator therapy to unload the ventricle while pressure remains adequate): with intense vasoconstriction, high filling pressure, and a preserved mean arterial pressure, afterload reduction with an agent such as nitroprusside lets the failing ventricle eject more at lower wall stress, often raising output without inotropic exposure. Volume would worsen a wedge of 26, and vasopressin raises the resistance that is already strangling output. Mechanical support is premature, and acute beta blocker uptitration depresses contractility in decompensation.
  130. The correct answer is C (Without a positive vasoreactivity response, calcium channel blockade offers no benefit and risks hemodynamic compromise): the benefit of calcium channel blockers is confined to the small minority with an acute positive response, and in nonresponders these agents lower systemic pressure and contractility without dilating the remodeled pulmonary bed, which can precipitate right heart decompensation, so she needs approved pulmonary arterial hypertension therapy instead. The class does not work irrespective of testing. Verapamil is actually avoided for its negative inotropy, dosing frequency is irrelevant to the failure, and repeating a negative test until it turns positive misunderstands what the test measures.
  131. The correct answer is E (Rapidly conducted atrial fibrillation misclassified as a ventricular arrhythmia by the device): an irregularly irregular rhythm whose ventricular electrograms match the patient's baseline morphology indicates supraventricular conduction, and atrial fibrillation crossing the rate cutoff is the most common cause of inappropriate shocks. True ventricular fibrillation produces chaotic electrograms unlike baseline. Lead fracture and electromagnetic interference create nonphysiologic noise rather than organized matching electrograms, and T wave oversensing shows a distinctive alternating pattern with double counting.
  132. The correct answer is C (Cardiogenic shock with preserved pressure, shown by hypoperfusion markers and minimal pulse pressure): a systolic of 104 with a pulse pressure of 16 mm Hg, cool skin, oliguria, rising lactate, and rising creatinine indicate low output despite an acceptable pressure reading. Elevated lactate with cool extremities argues against sepsis, which usually produces warm vasodilated skin. A fluid bolus for presumed dehydration would worsen congestion, and chronic kidney disease cannot explain acute lactate elevation.
  133. The correct answer is E (Perform immediate synchronized cardioversion after brief sedation as consciousness and pressure allow): hemodynamic instability during preexcited atrial fibrillation calls for electrical cardioversion without delay, because pharmacologic conversion is too slow for a hypotensive, obtunded patient. Procainamide is appropriate only while the patient remains stable. Esmolol blocks the atrioventricular node and can accelerate pathway conduction, intravenous amiodarone carries similar nodal blocking risk in this rhythm, and magnesium does not address the pathway at all.
  134. The correct answer is D (Obtain cardiac magnetic resonance imaging to distinguish true infarction from myocarditis): Infarction with nonobstructive coronary arteries is a working diagnosis rather than a final one, and magnetic resonance identifies the actual cause in most patients by separating subendocardial infarction from myocarditis and stress cardiomyopathy. Reassurance and discharge abandon the search while the mechanism, and therefore the therapy, remains unknown. Repeating the same angiogram adds little new information, empiric heart failure treatment skips the diagnosis that should direct it, and biopsy is reserved for suspected specific myocardial diseases after noninvasive imaging.
  135. The correct answer is C (Rapid correction can overshoot below the therapeutic floor, creating valve thrombosis risk and subsequent warfarin resistance): a mechanical prosthesis is intolerant of even brief subtherapeutic periods, and vitamin K frequently drives the INR beneath target while also blunting warfarin response for days afterward, so a nonbleeding moderate elevation is better managed by simply holding doses. Anaphylaxis is a rare intravenous phenomenon rather than the core rationale. Oral vitamin K actually works within a day or so, it does not activate platelets, and diagnostic clarity is not the guideline's concern.
  136. The correct answer is B (Replace saxagliptin with an agent shown to reduce heart failure events, such as empagliflozin): saxagliptin increased heart failure hospitalization in the SAVOR-TIMI 53 trial, making it a poor companion for her recurrent admissions, while sodium glucose cotransporter 2 inhibitors reduce cardiovascular death and heart failure events independent of glycemic control. Leaving the regimen intact because the hemoglobin A1c is at goal ignores that the problem is drug choice rather than glycemia. Pioglitazone promotes fluid retention and is avoided in symptomatic heart failure, and metformin remains acceptable in stable compensated patients.
  137. The correct answer is A (Renal artery stenting, since recurrent flash pulmonary edema justifies revascularization): recurrent flash pulmonary edema with bilateral renal artery stenosis is a recognized indication for revascularization, because the trials showing no benefit from routine stenting excluded exactly this decompensating phenotype. Applying those neutral trial results to her situation misreads their enrollment criteria. Nephrectomy sacrifices salvageable renal mass, further diuretic layering has already failed to prevent repeated admissions on five drug therapy, and renal denervation does not address the fixed arterial obstruction driving her episodes.
  138. The correct answer is A (Withdraw or substitute the pressor medications and remeasure before antihypertensive therapy): She is taking three recognized pressor exposures at once, a nonsteroidal antiinflammatory drug promoting sodium retention, a sympathomimetic decongestant, and estrogen containing contraception, and a new rise coinciding with them makes drug induced hypertension the leading explanation. Removing or replacing the culprits and remeasuring may resolve the problem without lifelong therapy. Starting an antihypertensive against ongoing pressor drugs treats the wrong lever, secondary hormonal testing is premature before this reversible cause is addressed, and monitoring alone lets an identified exposure continue.
  139. The correct answer is E (Rupture of the posteromedial papillary muscle causing torrential acute mitral regurgitation): The posteromedial muscle has a single blood supply from the posterior descending artery, and its rupture after inferior infarction floods a small unprepared left atrium, so pressures equalize quickly and the murmur is soft or nearly absent despite catastrophic regurgitation. Septal rupture classically produces a harsh murmur with a thrill rather than a quiet one. Free wall rupture presents as tamponade and electromechanical dissociation rather than pulmonary edema, and stent thrombosis or dynamic obstruction would not produce this valvular examination.
  140. The correct answer is E (Tethering of the lateral annulus by adherent pericardium while septal motion compensates): the thickened pericardium adheres to the lateral wall and restrains its longitudinal excursion, while the septum, free of pericardial attachment, augments its early diastolic recoil, so medial e' becomes preserved or elevated and exceeds lateral e'. Symmetric fibrosis would depress both velocities as seen in restriction. Circumflex compression and selective lateral hypertrophy are not features of constriction, and atrial contraction affects the a' wave rather than early diastolic e'.
  141. The correct answer is B (Transition to a direct oral anticoagulant to lower the bleeding hazard): Every major trial of anticoagulated patients undergoing stenting showed less bleeding with direct agents than with warfarin based regimens, and her poor time in therapeutic range plus monitoring barriers make warfarin specifically unsuitable. Tightening the target range does not fix the control problem her circumstances create. Stopping anticoagulation exposes her to stroke that antiplatelet agents cannot prevent, permanent aspirin addition increases bleeding rather than stabilizing anything, and arbitrary dose halving of warfarin produces subtherapeutic levels without protecting her.
  142. The correct answer is D (Surgical injury to the sinus node and its blood supply during atrial reconstruction): Extensive atrial suture lines in Mustard and Senning operations damage the sinus node and its artery, so sinus node dysfunction accumulates steadily and many survivors eventually need pacing. Progressive atrioventricular nodal block is instead typical of congenitally corrected transposition. Coronary reimplantation belongs to the arterial switch, which he did not undergo.
  143. The correct answer is A (Sydenham chorea can establish rheumatic fever alone, because its long latency lets serology fade): chorea appears months after the inciting streptococcal infection, by which time titers and acute phase markers have normalized, so the Jones framework accepts chorea as sufficient on its own without supporting criteria. Insisting on serology misunderstands this recognized exception. Tics are stereotyped rather than flowing and random, contemporaneous culture positivity is not expected at this latency, and although echocardiography is advisable, carditis is not required for the diagnosis.
  144. The correct answer is A (Integrate perfusion signs like cool limbs, minimal pulse pressure, and oliguria over pressure readings): compensatory vasoconstriction can hold systolic pressure in a deceptively normal range while organs fail, so recognition training must anchor on perfusion evidence, including narrow proportional pulse pressure, mottling, urine output, and lactate. Natriuretic peptides track stretch, not perfusion, and ejection fraction correlates poorly with shock state. Serial pressures repeat the original error, and warm skin is an unreliable basis for exclusion.
  145. The correct answer is C (Hypertensive pregnancy disorders roughly double lifetime cardiovascular risk, so she merits ongoing risk factor surveillance): preeclampsia is now recognized as a sex specific risk enhancer that approximately doubles later hypertension, ischemic heart disease, stroke, and heart failure, so guidelines advise eliciting this history and maintaining regular blood pressure, lipid, and glucose assessment for decades. Normal pressures today lower but do not erase that trajectory. The excess risk is arterial as well as thrombotic, extends far beyond future pregnancies, and has no established link to kidney stones.
  146. The correct answer is A (The defect vents a failing right heart, and closure removes that decompression pathway): With pulmonary resistance near systemic levels, the right ventricle depends on the shunt to unload itself, accepting cyanosis as the price of maintaining cardiac output. Sealing the defect forces the entire venous return through a diseased pulmonary bed and can precipitate acute right heart failure. Arrhythmia, infection, and device leak concerns are secondary to this fundamental physiologic objection.
  147. The correct answer is E (Reassurance, since isolated voltage criteria and early repolarization are training related findings): endurance conditioning raises vagal tone and produces sinus bradycardia, sinus arrhythmia, early repolarization, and increased QRS voltage from physiologic chamber enlargement, all recognized normal variants in an asymptomatic athlete with a benign history. An electrophysiology study and pacemaker referral pathologize expected adaptation. Beta blockade would deepen the bradycardia it claims to treat, and restricting sport until the heart rate rises misreads a healthy training effect as disease.
  148. The correct answer is C (Refer promptly for coronary angiography because these responses indicate severe proximal disease): Exercise induced ST elevation in leads without infarct Q waves signals transmural ischemia from a critical proximal stenosis or spasm, and a falling blood pressure with continuing workload means the ischemic ventricle cannot sustain output, together among the highest risk treadmill findings. Deferring to outpatient imaging or a medication trial with repeat testing leaves anatomy this dangerous undefined. Attributing exertional ST elevation with hypotension to benign repolarization misreads a provoked change, and calcium scoring quantifies plaque burden rather than the flow limiting lesion already demonstrated.
  149. The correct answer is C (Suction events from an underfilled ventricle, addressed with volume and device team speed adjustment): overdiuresis and heat losses shrink preload until the inflow cannula transiently pulls against the ventricular wall, producing low flow alarms and dizziness, so cautious volume repletion, diuretic reduction, and coordinated speed reduction are the response. Pump thrombosis produces power rises and hemolysis rather than positional brief alarms. A fractured driveline shows electrical faults, insufficiency causes chronic underperfusion, and treating undocumented arrhythmia is premature.
  150. The correct answer is A (Begin inotropic support because additional volume may overdistend the right ventricle): Once filling pressure is high and output is still inadequate, more fluid shifts the septum leftward and worsens left ventricular filling, so dobutamine becomes the appropriate next step. Continued boluses chase a number while overdistending a ventricle that has stopped converting preload into output. Nitroglycerin and furosemide both drain the preload this ventricle depends on, and tamponade is not supported when the picture began with infarction and responded partially to volume.
  151. The correct answer is E (Type A disease by ascending involvement, requiring emergency surgical repair despite the distal tear): the Stanford system classifies a dissection by whether the ascending aorta is involved, not by where the entry tear sits, so retrograde extension into the ascending aorta makes this type A disease requiring emergency surgery. Calling it type B based on tear location would apply a medical or endovascular pathway to a patient facing tamponade, coronary compromise, and rupture risks. Controlled pressure does not neutralize those mechanical threats, and computed tomography has already answered the classification question without further imaging.
  152. The correct answer is C (Give high dose intravenous methylprednisolone now, before biopsy, to protect her vision): cranial symptoms with markedly elevated inflammatory markers, anemia, and thrombocytosis indicate giant cell arteritis, and transient monocular vision loss signals impending permanent blindness, so high dose intravenous methylprednisolone is given immediately without waiting for biopsy. Delaying treatment for histology or vascular imaging risks irreversible visual loss, since biopsy findings persist for about two weeks after therapy begins. A moderate oral taper is inadequate for ocular involvement, and tocilizumab supports remission maintenance rather than emergency vision rescue.
  153. The correct answer is E (Clopidogrel, because more potent agents raise bleeding substantially when combined with anticoagulation): The antithrombotic trials in anticoagulated stent patients enrolled overwhelmingly clopidogrel treated participants, and registry plus trial data show markedly higher bleeding when prasugrel or ticagrelor is combined with an oral anticoagulant. The claim that prasugrel potency compensates for withdrawn aspirin misstates the goal, which is bleeding reduction rather than maximal inhibition. Reversible binding does not translate into safer real world bleeding for ticagrelor in this setting, monthly alternation has no evidence whatsoever, and the agents are demonstrably not interchangeable on bleeding endpoints.
  154. The correct answer is E (Chronic venous congestion drives progressive fibrosis, cirrhosis, and hepatocellular carcinoma risk): The Fontan circuit transmits elevated caval pressure directly to the hepatic veins, and years of congestion produce fibrosis in most long term survivors, with some progressing to cirrhosis or hepatocellular carcinoma. Surveillance with imaging and laboratory assessment detects these complications before decompensation. Viral transmission, drug toxicity, and embolic infarction are not the mechanism of Fontan associated liver disease.
  155. The correct answer is D (Evaluate for percutaneous left atrial appendage occlusion given high recurrent hemorrhage risk): lobar hemorrhage from probable amyloid angiopathy carries one of the highest recurrence rates of any bleeding source, making long term anticoagulation especially hazardous, and appendage occlusion offers stroke protection without chronic anticoagulant exposure. Early resumption of full dose therapy invites rebleeding in this substrate. Dual antiplatelet therapy still raises intracranial bleeding risk while protecting poorly against cardioembolism, and a lowered warfarin target sacrifices efficacy without proven safety.
  156. The correct answer is D (Empiric therapy without hemodynamic confirmation may treat the wrong disease, since other causes dominate): most pulmonary hypertension is group 2 or group 3, where phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibition lacks benefit and can worsen congestion or gas exchange, so an intermediate probability echo should trigger workup for left heart disease, lung disease, and chronic thromboembolism, with right heart catheterization before any targeted therapy. Waiting for deterioration is not the alternative, since the correct move is investigation now. Renal dosing and cost are minor issues beside prescribing a vasodilator for an unconfirmed diagnosis.
  157. The correct answer is E (Atrial fibrillation conducting over an accessory pathway with varying degrees of preexcitation): the irregular rhythm identifies fibrillation, the beat to beat change in QRS width reflects shifting fusion between pathway and nodal conduction, and extremely short RR intervals show the pathway has a short refractory period. Fixed aberrancy would produce a uniform wide morphology rather than variable width. Polymorphic ventricular tachycardia rarely presents as recurrent self terminating palpitations over years, and flutter with alternating block produces a patterned rather than chaotic irregularity.
  158. The correct answer is E (Both drugs amplify the same cyclic guanosine monophosphate pathway, producing severe hypotension when combined): riociguat stimulates soluble guanylate cyclase to generate cyclic guanosine monophosphate while sildenafil blocks its degradation, so together they multiply vasodilation and cause the profound pressure drop seen here, which is why the pairing is prohibited in labeling and why every prescriber must reconcile pulmonary hypertension medications before adding a phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitor. The timing after a new prescription argues against simple disease progression. No nitrate is required for the interaction, and neither protein binding displacement nor histamine mediated anaphylaxis is the mechanism.
  159. The correct answer is C (Surgical release of the compressing musculotendinous structures to free the artery): anatomic popliteal entrapment is treated with surgical myotomy to release the aberrant muscle, which removes the compression before repetitive trauma causes fixed stenosis, thrombosis, or aneurysmal degeneration, and a healthy artery needs no vascular reconstruction. A stent placed in a flexion segment under repeated muscular compression is prone to fracture and occlusion. Abandoning sport leaves the anatomic lesion and its progression risk in place, anticoagulation treats none of the mechanics, and botulinum toxin lacks durable evidence for anatomic entrapment.
  160. The correct answer is D (Hemoconcentration from effective decongestion, so continue diuresis toward examination based dry weight): a modest creatinine rise while weight falls steadily and symptoms improve reflects intravascular volume contraction from successful fluid removal, which predicts better outcomes, not injury. Stopping now would strand her short of decongestion, the strongest driver of readmission. Nothing suggests contrast exposure, glomerular disease, or a low output state needing inotropes when perfusion and symptoms are improving.
  161. The correct answer is A (Continuing low dose warfarin is a reasonable option, since embryopathy risk is dose dependent): warfarin is the most effective agent against mechanical valve thrombosis, and at daily doses of 5 mg or less the fetal embryopathy risk is low enough that continuation is a guideline endorsed option after shared decision making. Direct oral anticoagulants are contraindicated with mechanical valves. Fixed dose unmonitored heparin has an unacceptable thrombosis record, aspirin alone provides no adequate protection, and pregnancy is prothrombotic rather than protective.
  162. The correct answer is D (Intravenous nicardipine lowering mean pressure about 25 percent over the first hour): Hypertensive encephalopathy demands a titratable intravenous agent with continuous monitoring, but chronic hypertension shifts cerebral autoregulation rightward, so reductions beyond roughly a quarter of the presenting mean risk watershed ischemia; nicardipine allows precisely that controlled descent. Normalizing pressure within an hour overshoots the autoregulatory floor, and racing to her old baseline by morning repeats the error on a longer clock. Oral loading is too slow and erratic for active brain injury, and observation abandons a reversible emergency to progress.
  163. The correct answer is A (Early surgical repair now, since durable repair at negligible risk preempts irreversible ventricular injury): with a flail leaflet, severe regurgitation, and repair essentially assured at a primary valve center, early operation is reasonable even before symptoms or triggers appear, because waiting risks atrial fibrillation, pulmonary hypertension, and occult ventricular decline. Watchful waiting is acceptable only with vigilant surveillance and loses ground if follow up lapses. Edge to edge devices are less durable than surgical repair in a good operative candidate, medical therapy does not treat primary regurgitation, and five year intervals are far too sparse.
  164. The correct answer is B (Modest creatinine rises during effective decongestion often reflect hemoconcentration and need not stop diuresis): in decompensated heart failure, relieving venous congestion improves the physiology that injures the kidney, and trial analyses show small creatinine bumps during successful decongestion do not predict worse outcomes, so therapy should continue toward euvolemia guided by examination. Reflexively giving fluid back recongests the patient and undoes the gains. Nothing here suggests irreversible renal failure requiring dialysis, and improving jugular pressure with weight loss confirms the cardiac origin of his symptoms.
  165. The correct answer is B (Chronic myocardial injury from renal disease given the stable elevated values): Infarction requires a rise or fall of troponin with evidence of ischemia, and a flat elevated pattern in an asymptomatic dialysis patient represents chronic injury from ventricular hypertrophy, microvascular disease, and reduced clearance. Treating this as evolving infarction with anticoagulation adds bleeding risk without a thrombotic target, and unstable angina is defined by ischemic symptoms he does not have. Two closely agreeing results reflect a stable biologic state rather than error, and embolism would be expected to produce symptoms, hypoxemia, or a changing biomarker rather than this stability.
  166. The correct answer is E (Evaluate promptly for temporary mechanical circulatory support before irreversible organ injury develops): Rising lactate and oliguria despite two escalating agents define deteriorating shock, and outcomes with temporary support devices depend on deployment before multiorgan failure is established, ideally with shock team involvement. Adding another pressor and waiting half a day, or simply escalating current doses, increases myocardial oxygen demand while the perfusion deficit worsens. A large fluid bolus congests a failing ventricle, and renal replacement therapy treats a downstream marker while ignoring the circulatory cause.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is E (Treat the aortic stenosis alone first, expecting secondary mitral regurgitation to lessen once afterload falls): relieving aortic obstruction lowers ventricular systolic pressure and remodeling stress, and moderate secondary mitral regurgitation with a structurally normal valve frequently improves afterward, so staged reassessment avoids an unnecessary second prosthesis. Prophylactic double valve surgery adds operative risk for a lesion that may resolve. The stenotic valve is the primary symptomatic driver, mitral first sequencing leaves the dominant lesion untreated, and waiting for ejection fraction recovery inverts cause and effect.
  2. The correct answer is D (Delayed aortic valve closure making the split audible during expiration instead): severe outflow obstruction prolongs left ventricular ejection so the aortic component falls after the pulmonic component, reversing their order, and the normal inspiratory delay of pulmonic closure then moves the two sounds together, so the split paradoxically appears in expiration. An atrial septal defect causes fixed splitting rather than reversed splitting. Right bundle branch block widens the normal split in inspiration, accelerated pulmonic closure is not the physiology here, and stroke volume swings do not silence the components.
  3. The correct answer is B (Treat sepsis, volume, and fever first while considering amiodarone if compromise persists): in septic patients, atrial fibrillation is usually driven by inflammation, catecholamines, and volume shifts, and aggressive nodal blockade with diltiazem or repeated metoprolol can worsen hypotension in a vasopressor dependent patient. Amiodarone offers rate and rhythm benefit with less blood pressure penalty when the arrhythmia itself causes compromise. Cardioversion often fails durably while the septic trigger persists, and flecainide is unsafe in acute illness with possible structural disease.
  4. The correct answer is D (Bilateral renal artery stenosis causing angiotensin dependent filtration and episodic volume overload): Recurrent flash pulmonary edema with preserved systolic function, resistant hypertension, diffuse atherosclerosis, and a sharp creatinine rise on an angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitor together form the classic signature of bilateral renovascular disease, where both kidneys retain sodium avidly and filtration collapses once efferent tone is blocked. Repeated infarction would leave enzyme and wall motion evidence, significant mitral regurgitation would persist on echocardiography, pheochromocytoma produces adrenergic spells, and rebound from missed doses does not explain the renal response to ramipril.
  5. The correct answer is A (Outflow obstruction is dynamic, so exertion can provoke severe gradients a resting study misses): obstruction in this disease intensifies with catecholamines, reduced preload, and vasodilation, so a trivial resting gradient can exceed 50 mm Hg during exercise, and provocative or exercise echocardiography is required before dismissing exertional syncope. Arrhythmia also matters, but the claim that gradient measurements are uninformative is exactly backwards for exertional events. Neither a fixed severity threshold of fifteen nor systematic echocardiographic underestimation reflects real measurement behavior.
  6. The correct answer is C (Anti Xa peak levels drawn after dosing, maintained in the therapeutic target range with dose adjustment): pregnancy continuously changes volume of distribution and renal clearance, so weight based low molecular weight heparin dosing drifts subtherapeutic, and valve thromboses in pregnancy have clustered in patients dosed without anti Xa guided titration to peak levels near 0.8 to 1.2 units/mL. Platelet surveillance addresses a different complication and does not confirm efficacy. INR does not measure heparin effect, echocardiography detects failure after the fact, and activated clotting time is not the validated assay.
  7. The correct answer is A (Withhold warfarin, recheck the INR shortly, and resume a lower dose without vitamin K): for INR elevations below roughly 10 without bleeding, simply omitting doses is preferred, and in mechanical valve patients vitamin K is specifically avoided because overcorrection creates a window of valve thrombosis risk and later warfarin resistance. High dose intravenous vitamin K and prothrombin complex are reserved for serious bleeding. Continuing the usual dose leaves her exposed to hemorrhage, and even moderate oral vitamin K risks dropping her below the protective range.
  8. The correct answer is E (Give a rapid intravenous crystalloid bolus and avoid further preload reducing agents): Profound hypotension after nitroglycerin with clear lungs and venous distension identifies right ventricular infarction, a preload dependent state that responds to volume. Furosemide would further empty the failing right ventricle and deepen the shock, and beta blockade would suppress the compensatory contractility and heart rate. Norepinephrine may eventually be needed but should not replace the volume the ventricle actually lacks, and upright positioning with positive pressure both reduce venous return further.
  9. The correct answer is C (Sedation followed by synchronized cardioversion, with amiodarone to prevent early recurrence): a patient with tenuous perfusion and active congestion tolerates neither prolonged tachycardia nor the negative inotropy and vasodilation of full dose procainamide, so prompt synchronized cardioversion is the safest route to sinus rhythm. Amiodarone then suppresses recurrence with the least hemodynamic penalty in severe left ventricular dysfunction. Verapamil can precipitate collapse in ventricular tachycardia, and an unsynchronized shock risks inducing ventricular fibrillation when a synchronized one is feasible.
  10. The correct answer is C (Progressive neointimal tissue growth within the stent narrowing the lumen over months): In stent restenosis is a proliferative healing response that builds gradually, so it presents months later as crescendo exertional angina rather than sudden infarction, exactly this tempo. Acute stent thrombosis produces abrupt occlusion and infarction rather than three weeks of predictable exertional symptoms, and adherent dual therapy makes it less likely. Edge dissection declares itself early after implantation, persistent microvascular obstruction would not first appear at ten months, and implanted stents do not cause pericardial constriction.
  11. The correct answer is A (Acute thrombosis of the stent precipitated by premature antiplatelet therapy discontinuation): An abrupt ST elevation event within weeks of stenting, immediately after stopping a P2Y12 inhibitor, is stent thrombosis until proven otherwise, because the struts remain unendothelialized and fully thrombogenic this early. Neointimal proliferation takes months and presents as gradual exertional symptoms rather than sudden occlusion. De novo disease progression and edge vasospasm rarely produce infarction on this compressed timeline, and atrial embolization requires an arrhythmic substrate this vignette never establishes.
  12. The correct answer is C (South Asian ancestry is a recognized risk enhancer favoring earlier statin initiation): South Asian ancestry is explicitly listed as a risk enhancing factor because observed atherosclerotic risk exceeds what the pooled cohort equations predict for this group. The calculators were derived from cohorts that underrepresent this population, so his estimate likely understates rather than overstates risk, and the enhancement bears directly on coronary prevention decisions. Genetic testing is not required, and his father's surgery at 61 does not itself meet the premature family history threshold.
  13. The correct answer is E (Sympathetic inhibition with propranolol, sedation, or stellate ganglion blockade as an adjunct): electrical storm is perpetuated by a surge of sympathetic tone, which each shock amplifies, and dampening it with nonselective beta blockade, sedation or anesthesia, or stellate ganglion block reduces recurrences. Isoproterenol is useful for pause dependent torsades, not scar related monomorphic tachycardia. Raising detection rates merely hides treatable arrhythmia, extraction addresses a problem that does not exist here, and calcium has no antiarrhythmic role in this setting.
  14. The correct answer is B (Norepinephrine as the initial vasopressor while emergency revascularization is arranged): Norepinephrine restores perfusion pressure with less arrhythmia than the alternatives and is the preferred first vasopressor in cardiogenic shock, with revascularization remaining the definitive therapy. Dopamine produced more arrhythmic events and worse outcomes in the cardiogenic subgroup of comparative trials. Phenylephrine adds afterload without inotropy to a failing ventricle, milrinone vasodilates and can deepen hypotension when used first, and epinephrine raises lactate and arrhythmia burden compared with the preferred agent.
  15. The correct answer is E (Acute occlusion of the proximal left anterior descending artery requiring immediate reperfusion): upsloping precordial ST depression with tall symmetric T waves and ongoing pain is the de Winter pattern, indicating acute proximal left anterior descending occlusion that needs immediate reperfusion rather than delayed angiography. Posterior injury from the circumflex shows horizontal depression in V1 to V3 with dominant R waves, demand related subendocardial ischemia lacks the hyperacute T morphology, and pericarditis causes diffuse elevation. A patent artery with microvascular obstruction is a post reperfusion phenomenon.
  16. The correct answer is E (Her congenital valve malformation predisposes to accessory pathways causing reentrant tachycardia): Apical displacement of the septal tricuspid leaflet defines Ebstein anomaly, and the malformed atrioventricular junction leaves muscular connections that function as accessory pathways. Preexcitation with paroxysmal reentrant tachycardia is therefore an expected part of the same disease rather than coincidence. Sinus node stretch and normal variant explanations ignore this established association.
  17. The correct answer is C (Acute rheumatic fever, because one major and two minor criteria coexist with evidence of streptococcal infection): migratory polyarthritis is a major criterion, fever and the elevated sedimentation rate supply two minor criteria, and the antistreptolysin O rise documents the antecedent streptococcal infection the framework requires. Carditis strengthens but is not a prerequisite for diagnosis. Dismissing the serology ignores the classic migratory pattern, reactive arthritis is a diagnosis of exclusion that still often prompts prophylaxis, and juvenile idiopathic arthritis is typically persistent rather than migratory.
  18. The correct answer is C (Congenital absence of the pericardium, a typically incidental and clinically silent anomaly): complete left sided absence lets the heart shift and rotate leftward, classically with lung interposed between the aorta and pulmonary artery and a prominent pulmonary artery contour, and complete forms are usually benign incidental findings. A mediastinal teratoma would appear as a discrete mass rather than interposed lung. Lobar collapse shows volume loss with displaced fissures, an unrecognized pericardiectomy is implausible without surgical history, and right ventricular enlargement of that degree would not be hemodynamically silent.
  19. The correct answer is B (Volume loading a failing, congested right ventricle worsens septal shift and output, so decongestion serves better): with central venous pressure at 19 mmHg the right ventricle sits on the descending limb of its function curve, and added volume dilates it further, bows the septum leftward, impairs left ventricular filling, and drops systemic output, so careful diuresis or ultrafiltration with vasopressor support is the sound approach. Right heart failure with congestion behaves opposite to hypovolemic shock. Neither hemodilution nor reactions to balanced fluids is the operative harm, and slowing the same misguided infusion does not rescue it.
  20. The correct answer is C (Cardiac sarcoidosis may underlie the block, and it carries ventricular arrhythmia risk that alters device choice): unexplained high grade atrioventricular block in an adult under sixty with hilar adenopathy should prompt evaluation for sarcoidosis with advanced imaging such as cardiac magnetic resonance or positron emission tomography, because granulomatous myocardium also generates ventricular tachycardia and often justifies a defibrillator rather than a simple pacemaker. Immunosuppression may help but does not reliably abolish the arrhythmic substrate, so device deferral on steroids alone is unsafe. Assuming congenital disease or lymphoma skips the cardiac workup this pairing requires, and high grade block with presyncope is never a three month outpatient problem.
  21. The correct answer is E (Reduced intravascular volume lets modest pericardial pressure equal diastolic filling pressures): tamponade physiology depends on equalization between intrapericardial and cardiac diastolic pressures, not on any absolute threshold, so a hypovolemic patient with low filling pressures can be compressed by an effusion generating only mild intrapericardial pressure, the entity called low pressure tamponade. No fixed 15 mmHg requirement exists. Effusions do not invalidate atrial pressure measurement, vasodilation does not cause anatomic chamber collapse, and blaming transducer zeroing dismisses a coherent physiologic picture.
  22. The correct answer is A (High dose caffeine and stimulant additives plausibly explain his findings, and counseling on intake is warranted): energy drinks combine large caffeine doses with taurine and other stimulants, and case series link heavy consumption to tachyarrhythmias, blood pressure elevation, and QT changes in young people, so exposure reduction with short observation is the sensible first move. Calling these products inert contradicts the published physiology. Thyroid storm requires more than isolated tachycardia after a clear exposure, and neither pacing evaluation nor indefinite beta blockade fits a transient stimulant effect.
  23. The correct answer is D (Reassure her, review interacting medications, and continue both antiplatelet agents): Bruising and minor gum bleeding are nuisance events without hemodynamic or hemoglobin significance, and interrupting therapy two months after stenting for them exposes her to thrombosis risk that far exceeds their harm. Stopping either agent this early converts trivial bleeding into a meaningful ischemic gamble. Dose halving has no evidence base and undermines both drugs, and hospital admission for stable nuisance symptoms consumes resources without changing anything.
  24. The correct answer is A (Ranolazine reduces angina frequency but has not reduced death or myocardial infarction): in the large randomized experience ranolazine lessened angina and improved exercise tolerance but did not reduce death or myocardial infarction, so preventive therapy continues unchanged. No mortality benefit exists to justify statin withdrawal, the diabetic subgroup showed improved glycemic markers rather than fewer infarctions, and event reduction comparable to beta blockade has not been demonstrated. Extensive human outcome trials exist, so missing animal data is not the explanation.
  25. The correct answer is E (Bypass surgery added to medical therapy reduced long term mortality in this population): extended follow up of surgical therapy for ischemic cardiomyopathy showed a significant reduction in all cause mortality when bypass was added to medical therapy, despite early procedural risk. Symptom benefit without survival effect understates that result, and medical therapy alone proved inferior over ten years. Viability imaging did not identify who benefited, and no comparable survival evidence exists for stenting in this population.
  26. The correct answer is C (A benign repolarization variant and apical cardiomyopathy both produce this, so image him): anterior T wave inversions preceded by convex elevation are a recognized normal pattern in Black athletes, yet apical hypertrophic cardiomyopathy can look identical, so echocardiography, with magnetic resonance if the apex images poorly, is the discriminating step. A normal examination does not exclude apical disease, and blanket disqualification overreacts to a pattern that is often benign. Short deconditioning periods do not reverse either pattern quickly enough to separate them.
  27. The correct answer is B (Staphylococcal infection of prosthetic material is difficult to eradicate with antibiotics and often requires operative intervention): organisms adherent to prosthetic material form biofilm that shields them from antibiotics and host defenses, and Staphylococcus aureus prosthetic valve endocarditis has high failure and mortality rates managed medically, so early surgical involvement is warranted even in apparent stability. Prosthetic infection is not equivalent to native disease. Endothelialization does not protect against staphylococcal invasion, valve in valve planning is not the purpose of urgent consultation, and antibiotic penetration into prosthetic material is poorer, not better.
  28. The correct answer is D (Failure to capture from an elevated threshold at the lead myocardium interface): pacing spikes delivered on schedule without a following QRS define failure to capture, and a progressively rising threshold points to problems at the lead tissue interface such as microdislodgment, fibrosis, or exit block. Oversensing inhibits output, so no spike would appear at all. Battery depletion and crosstalk alter timing or output delivery rather than producing on time spikes that fail, and pacemaker mediated tachycardia produces a paced tachycardia rather than dropped beats.
  29. The correct answer is E (The flat trend without significant rise or fall indicates chronic myocardial injury): Acute infarction is defined by a dynamic change in troponin, and six stable values across eighteen hours in a dialysis patient with a musculoskeletal pain source and unchanged ECG identify chronic injury instead. Elevation above the reference limit alone cannot confirm infarction in renal disease, where baseline values commonly sit high. Anticoagulation and emergency angiography add bleeding and procedural risk without an acute thrombotic target, and attributing consistent reproducible values to assay drift misreads a stable biologic signal.

The correct answer is C (Resume a direct oral anticoagulant now, with left atrial appendage occlusion an alternative): after a treated, nonrecurrent gastrointestinal bleed, restarting anticoagulation improves survival in high stroke risk patients, and appendage occlusion is a guideline supported option when long term anticoagulation is truly unacceptable. Aspirin provides little stroke protection in atrial fibrillation while still causing bleeding. Warfarin does not bleed less than the direct agents in the gut overall, and acid suppression alone offers no cardioembolic protection.

  39. The correct answer is C (Induction vasodilation and positive pressure can collapse right ventricular output, so hemodynamic preparation is essential): induction agents drop systemic vascular tone and right coronary perfusion while positive pressure ventilation raises pulmonary vascular resistance and reduces venous return, a combination that can produce irretrievable circulatory collapse in a preload dependent, afterload sensitive right ventricle, so vasopressors running before induction, cautious agent selection, and the most experienced operator are standard. Peri-intubation arrest in this population is a hemodynamic event rather than an airway anatomy problem. Aspiration, oxygen toxicity, and anesthetic depth are minor considerations beside the circulatory physiology.

The correct answer is B (This is atrial flutter with variable block, warranting the same anticoagulation rules as fibrillation): regular sawtooth flutter waves with a changing conduction ratio produce an irregular ventricular response while the atrial rhythm itself remains organized, and guidelines assign flutter the same thromboembolic risk scoring and anticoagulation thresholds as atrial fibrillation. The irregular response does not convert the diagnosis to fibrillation, and multifocal atrial tachycardia shows discrete P waves of varying morphology rather than sawtooth activity. Organized flutter still promotes appendage thrombus, and cavotricuspid isthmus ablation remains highly effective for typical flutter.

Annuloplasty actually suits the atrial form well. Tethered leaflets do not coapt reliably, the physiologies differ fundamentally, and atrial disease is often modifiable.

Early cardiomyopathy is frequently asymptomatic, and it can present at any age. No single cavity dimension separates the entities, and a normal pressure response does not exclude myocardial disease.

The correct answer is E (Carvedilol occupies beta receptors competitively, while milrinone works distal to that receptor): dobutamine must bind beta receptors already saturated by a nonselective blocker, so usual doses produce little inotropy, whereas a phosphodiesterase inhibitor raises cyclic AMP beyond the receptor. Dobutamine needs no renal activation, and meaningful tachyphylaxis takes days rather than an hour. Carvedilol does not induce hepatic clearance of catecholamines, and vagal withdrawal is not dobutamine's mechanism.

The correct answer is B (Ventricular tachycardia with atrioventricular dissociation between independent atrial and ventricular activity): cannon A waves and a varying first heart sound occur when the atria intermittently contract against closed atrioventricular valves, which happens only when atrial and ventricular rhythms are dissociated. In a patient with prior infarction, this finding makes ventricular tachycardia the overwhelmingly likely diagnosis. Orthodromic reentry and supraventricular tachycardia with aberrancy both require linked atrial and ventricular activity, so they cannot produce these variable findings.

  97. The correct answer is E (One measurement generally suffices, because the level is genetically set and statins do not lower it): lipoprotein(a) is genetically determined and stable across adult life, and statins do not lower it, with some data suggesting a small rise, so a single lifetime measurement usually suffices. The correct answer is A (Cumulative organ toxicity over decades argues for ablation or safer agents first): amiodarone is often the most effective drug, but pulmonary, thyroid, hepatic, ocular, and neurologic toxicity accumulate with dose and duration, so guidelines position it behind catheter ablation and better tolerated agents in a relatively young patient facing decades of exposure. Structured monitoring does detect most toxicity early, and rhythm control is a reasonable, often preferred strategy in symptomatic patients.

The correct answer is D (Imaging of extracoronary arteries to screen for underlying fibromuscular dysplasia): A majority of patients with spontaneous coronary dissection harbor fibromuscular dysplasia or related arteriopathies, so head to pelvis vascular imaging is advised to detect renal, carotid, and intracranial involvement including aneurysms. Cholesterol gene testing and calcium scoring evaluate atherosclerosis, which is not the mechanism of her event and is already unlikely given her lipids. An electrophysiology study has no screening role after an uncomplicated course, and a marrow examination pursues a clonal thrombotic disorder her arterial wall disease does not suggest.
  157. The correct answer is B (Extending the postventricular atrial refractory period so retrograde P waves are ignored): pacing at the upper tracking rate with an atrial sensed event after every paced beat describes pacemaker mediated tachycardia, an endless loop in which retrograde conduction is sensed and tracked. Lengthening the postventricular atrial refractory period makes the device blind to those retrograde signals, breaking the loop. Output and base rate changes do not affect tracking behavior, and asynchronous modes eliminate sensing altogether at the cost of competitive pacing.
  158. The correct answer is C (Apixaban with clopidogrel, discontinuing aspirin within the first week after intervention): Trials of anticoagulated patients after stenting consistently showed that dropping aspirin early and continuing a direct anticoagulant with a P2Y12 inhibitor markedly reduces bleeding without a significant rise in stent thrombosis. Prolonged triple therapy for a year produces the highest bleeding rates of any strategy studied. Stopping the anticoagulant leaves his substantial stroke risk uncovered, switching to warfarin within triple therapy adds bleeding compared with direct agents, and an anticoagulant alone provides inadequate platelet inhibition for a fresh stent.
  159. The correct answer is A (Tachycardia limits regurgitant time each beat, so slowing the rate enlarges backward flow): the rapid rate is a compensation that trims each diastolic interval during which blood leaks backward, so blunting it prolongs regurgitation and can precipitate collapse. Beta blockers do not acutely dilate the root or meaningfully raise systemic resistance in this way. Reduced contractility is an additional concern but the decisive mechanism is diastolic prolongation, and fever monitoring is irrelevant to the hemodynamic danger.
  160. The correct answer is D (Admit now, stop the checkpoint inhibitor, and begin high dose intravenous corticosteroids promptly): checkpoint inhibitor myocarditis carries mortality approaching half when fulminant, deteriorates unpredictably, and conduction disease plus a markedly elevated troponin marks exactly the high risk group, so drug cessation and immediate immunosuppression cannot wait. Continued dosing or delayed imaging risks abrupt heart block or ventricular arrhythmia. Low dose home steroids and observation without treatment both underdose a potentially lethal inflammatory process.
  161. The correct answer is D (Interrupt therapy, recheck the enzyme, and rechallenge later at a lower intensity): A creatine kinase modestly above normal but far below ten times the upper limit, with symptoms and no interacting cause, warrants temporary interruption, confirmation of enzyme normalization, and a structured rechallenge at reduced intensity or with an alternative agent. Dismissing the elevation entirely ignores a symptomatic abnormality worth documenting and retesting. Hospitalization for hydration targets rhabdomyolysis, which requires massive enzyme elevation and pigmenturia he lacks. Permanent discontinuation with an allergy label forfeits secondary prevention for a manageable effect, and increasing the dose escalates the very exposure producing his symptoms.
  162. The correct answer is B (Discordant repolarization changes from ventricular hypertrophy warranting serial troponins and comparison tracings): Hypertrophy produces secondary ST elevation discordant to deep anterior S waves and a lateral strain pattern, which together mimic infarction. Anterior infarction is not established by millimeter criteria alone when voltage is extreme, and posterior infarction produces depression in the right precordial leads rather than the lateral ones. Pericarditis causes diffuse concave elevation with PR changes, and a channelopathy pattern shows coved right precordial elevation rather than strain morphology.
  163. The correct answer is C (Therapeutic anticoagulation, managing it like deep vein thrombosis given junctional proximity): a saphenous thrombus within 3 centimeters of the saphenofemoral junction behaves like proximal deep vein thrombosis because propagation across the junction is common, so full therapeutic anticoagulation is recommended rather than prophylactic dosing. The 45 day prophylactic fondaparinux regimen applies to superficial thrombosis sitting farther from the junction. Symptomatic care and watchful imaging both gamble on a short distance the clot can cross quickly, and surgical ligation with aspirin is inferior to anticoagulation for preventing embolic complications.
  164. The correct answer is E (Glucocorticoid therapy first, deferring revascularization until active inflammation is controlled): revascularization performed during active Takayasu arteritis fails at high rates from restenosis, anastomotic complications, and progression of inflamed vessel walls, so glucocorticoids come first and intervention is deferred until disease activity is controlled, since arm claudication is not limb threatening. Stenting does not treat the underlying arteritis, and operating before immunosuppression sews grafts into actively inflamed tissue. Withholding immunosuppression altogether allows ongoing arterial damage well beyond the symptomatic subclavian segment.
  165. The correct answer is B (The lungs inactivate circulating vasoactive substances before blood reaches the left heart): serotonin and related mediators from hepatic metastases drain into the systemic venous return, bathe the right sided valves, and are then largely degraded by pulmonary monoamine oxidase, so the left heart sees little active hormone unless a patent foramen ovale or pulmonary metastases exist. Left sided leaflets do carry responsive receptors and become involved when exposure occurs. Pressure washing is not the protective mechanism, hepatic venous drainage enters the systemic veins directly, and the pericardium filters nothing.
  166. The correct answer is C (The defect lacks supportive rims and the anomalous vein needs surgical rerouting): This is a superior sinus venosus defect, which sits outside the fossa ovalis without circumferential septal tissue to anchor a device, and the anomalous right upper pulmonary vein must be baffled back to the left atrium. Surgery addresses both problems in a single operation. Chamber enlargement argues for repair rather than against it, and shunt direction is not the barrier here.
